#### Legends & Miracles (karamat)

Sorley prefaces the whole category with a dismissal and then prints it anyway: "Many stories of the
miraculous order are told of him. But no serious-minded person will attach any value to them."
(p. 180, folio 171). Every attribution below is his.

- **The letter Alif.** "One typical story of this kind is that when he was being taught the alphabet
  he would not go beyond the letter Alif, which is the symbol of God and unity. The moral drawn from
  this is, of course, that from the days of his childhood he was engrossed by a vision of the divine
  unity." (p. 180, folio 171).
- **The Risalo thrown into the Kirar lake and rewritten from memory.** "When Tamar and Hashim, the
  poet's favourite disciples and secretaries, brought him the complete Risa.Io[text-layer?], Shah
  Abdul Latif turned over the pages and remarked of the frequent laments of Sasui that the work was
  simply a safety valve for the peccant humours of the flesh and flung it suddenly into the Kirar
  lake. His secretaries expostulated with him and he allowed them to rewrite the whole from memory."
  (p. 184, folio 175, attributed to "Dayaram GidumaP[text-layer?]" and footnoted to "Dayaram
  Gidumal : Something about Sind by Sigma, p. 41."). Sorley: "it can be no more than a stupid
  magnification of some trivial incident. But it is more likely to be a legend intended to build up
  the tradition of the inspired delivery of the poems."
- **The curse that explains his childlessness.** "' It is said that the Shah's wife while
  enceirite[text-layer?] longed one day for a certain kind of fish and ·her maid sent one of his
  followers to procure it. The Shah happened to enquire about this follower and learning the cause
  of his absence observed : ' When the embryo is such a trouble to my fakirs, what will the full
  blown adult be ? May such a blossom be nipped in the bud ! The child, we are told, in consequence
  was still-born to the great grief of the mother and the Shah left no heir surviving him. '"
  (pp. 184–185, folios 175–176, footnoted to "Dayaram Gidumal : Something about Sind by Sigma,
  p. 42."). Sorley: "Clearly the obj ect of this fairy tale is to account for the poet's
  childlessness." (p. 185, folio 176).
- **The blessing that produced a Kalhora heir.** "Indeed tradition likes to add that the birth of
  Ghulam Shah Kalh6ro[text-layer?] was the result of the blessing of Shah Abdul Latif." (p. 182,
  folio 173). **Attributed to "tradition"; Sorley marks it with "likes to add".** This is the only
  karamat in the book that connects the poet directly to the man who built his tomb.
- **Marvels inside the verse, attributed by the text to the poem and not to a holy person.** Iron
  turned to gold at a touch — "If .Thou .but touch this iron ' me ', gold I should be by reason"
  (p. 338, folio 329); the Prophet bearing the loads of all who ask (p. 339, folio 330); the
  lightning crossing the Prophet's tomb and filling the stream (p. 343, folio 334); the Angel
  within "two bows' distance" of God, attributed only to "the Holy Word" with **no chapter and no
  verse** — **(not stated)** (p. 340, folio 331); the Lord decreeing "An exceHent[text-layer?]
  landing-place" for a fare-less ship (p. 330, folio 321); "' Love came himself, the cable sought /
  And pushed the boat from the land.'" (p. 328, folio 319); the martyrs "garlanded and crowned …
  By maids of Paradis�.·[text-layer?]" (p. 349, folio 340); three orders of creation mourning them
  — "Men in i;heir[text-layer?] homes, · beasts in the wild / And angels .in · the sky" (p. 349,
  folio 340); Hur's blood dyeing beard and teeth "As is pomegranate· flower" and his turban shining
  "As shines full fortnig4t's[text-layer?]. moon" (p. 351, folio 342); the holy men whose
  footprints are in Lahut and who hold a ruby, **neither explained** — **(not stated)** (p. 357,
  folio 348); the ascetics who pass Lahut and reach Ulwahet[text-layer?] and "not one word from
  them ·escapes" (p. 362, folio 353); the mountain that weeps with the sufferer (p. 376, folio 367);
  the death-angels Azrad[text-layer?], Munkar and Nakir met on the road and mistaken for the
  lover's messengers (p. 383, folio 374); the deer that speak after Sasui's death (p. 390,
  folio 381); the beloved's fragrance reaching the hills (p. 387, folio 378); the lover carrying
  her across the passes (p. 397, folio 388).
- **A fakir's prophecy fulfilled, inside `THE KING AND THE MINSTREL`.** "His si�ter[text-layer?] had
  a son named Bijal about whom a· fa.kif[text-layer?] had predicted that he was fated to kill
  Diach" (p. 352, folio 343), and, at the headnote's close, "obtained, as the fakir had foretold,
  the head of the king" (p. 352, folio 343). **The fakir is unnamed** — **(not stated)** — and it
  is the book's only occurrence of the word in the singular inside a story. The king's salvation
  follows: "He found acceptance . .under Allal}'s[text-layer?] door ; / His million-'numbered .
  needs fulfilled, told . o'er." (p. 356, folio 347).
- **The primordial covenant, made the origin of two of the folk romances.** In the Suhini cycle:
  "Time was when God, the One and the Eternal, spake / Unto the souls and thundered : ' Am I not
  your Lord ? ' / Then, even then, to Suhini had there come a love / And longing for the neatherd.
  'Twas of God's own will / That might of waters broke her earthen pot in twain." (p. 406,
  folio 397). In the Marui cycle: "When there fell on mine earsthe[text-layer?] Word, / ' Am I not
  then your Lord ? ' / And with ' Yes ' my heart gave assent, / It was then that my promise I made
  / With the folk in the hedges pent." (p. 422; printed again at p. 290, folio 281 as "When there
  fell on mine ears the Word / " Am I not then your Lord ? "1"). **This is the doctrinal hinge by
  which the folk romances are made religious: the heroine's love is dated to before the world.**
  **The one Koranic phrase the book footnotes inside the poet's own verse is this one**, sourced
  "From the Koran. Rodwell's Translation. Edition Everyman, p. 3 rn[text-layer?]." (p. 290,
  folio 281) — **the page figure is unreadable**. Sorley then cross-reads it against Whinfield's
  introduction to the Masnawi on the pre-eternal contract (p. 291, folio 282, footnote 1 citing
  "The Masnawi-i-Manavi. Translated by Whinfield. Introduction, pp. xxii-xxvii.").
- **Music itself is treated as a supernatural power, and Sorley says so in order to dismiss it.**
  "was sufficient to create in the minds of the listeners a belief t.hat this music was _a kind of
  myste'rious and almost superhuman art ' closed to the bulk of m'arikind[text-layer?]. … Music in
  fact was still a form of mumbo-j'umbo." (p. 228, folio 219). The modes are "deified and are
  represented in religious art in stereotyped ways as gods and goddesses with special powers"
  (p. 231, folio 222), and Sorley calls the whole scheme "a great deal of artificiality
  ind[text-layer?] mythological make-believe" (p. 231, folio 222). **The Sur Sorath's
  head-sacrifice is the book's only narrative miracle attached to the poet's own work**: "the
  singer, the ' master of music ', is depicted · as charming the king so overwhelmingly as to
  induce him to yield up his head to the singer in loving sacrifice which gains him communion with
  God" (p. 250, folio 241), and "it makes clear the relation believed to exist between music and
  immortal things" (pp. 228–229, folios 219–220). **In chunk 016 neither the king nor the singer is
  named; the index later supplies "Diach the King hath yielded up his head   347" (p. 310,
  folio 301) and the glossary supplies both, "Bijal  Name of the singer" and "Diich[text-layer?]
  Name of the King" (p. 430, folio 421). The book never states that the untitled `THE KING AND THE
  MINSTREL` sections are the Sur Sorath** — **(not stated)**.

#### Significance

- **What the work is, stated on the book's first page of text and repeated.** "THE collection of
  mystical poems known as the RisaJ.o[text-layer?] of Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit is the only classic
  which the language of Sind has yet produced in the realm of deeply imaginative literature."
  (p. 7, folio vii); "the beautiful mystical poetry of Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit-the only classic
  work in the Sindhi language" (p. 19, folio 10); "Nor is there in Sindhi literature either before
  or after Shah Abdul Latif's time anything fit to be compared with'.[text-layer?] the Risalo"
  (p. 212, folio 203); the Risalo is "the only gem cast up from the sea of Sind's vernacular
  literature" (p. 216, folio 207).
- **His religious character, as Sorley states it at the outset.** "They are the wor�[text-layer?] of
  a natural poet of Siifi[text-layer?] leanings." (p. 7, folio vii); the thought "is also everywhere
  permeated with the deep subtleties of Sufi philosophy" (p. 8, folio viii). **No silsila is named
  there or anywhere.** The dedication poem calls him "your lowly hamlet bard" and "your humble
  minstrel" whose "melodies / Enthralled a rust!c [text-layer?] people holds" (p. 9).
- **The standing of the poems in Sind, as Sorley reports it.** "In Sind his poems are · held in such
  universal and popular esteem as is accorded only to poetry which has successfally [text-layer?]
  interpreted the most intimate thoughts and the sincerest feelings of a people. In some
  fori:n [text-layer?] or other_ the verses are. known to all classes." (p. 7, folio vii).
- **The poet as the true measure of Sindhi Islam — the hinge of Sorley's argument about Sindhi
  religion, and his single most quotable sentence for this archive.** "The real form and meaning of
  Islam in Sind is not to be found in these ebullitions of popular and incredible fanaticism, but
  is to be read in the beautiful spiritual poems of Shah Abdul Latif. It will be most unfair to
  judge Sind by the former and forget the latter." (p. 169, folio 160). **The "ebullitions" are the
  dancing of darwishes at Tatta and, in the pages immediately before, the mendicant extortion and
  shrine scenes at Sehwan.** The surrounding judgement about the century: "It was the Kalhora's
  practice of deferring to the priestly class that led to the extraordinary reverence for pirs,
  Sayids and holy men, which may be said to have been the predominant characteristic of eighteenth
  century Sind." (p. 163, folio 154).
- **His audience crosses the Muslim–Hindu line, asserted five times and given three different
  mechanisms.** "the poetry of Shah Abdul . Latif, while itself in the true tradition of Islamic
  mysticism, nevertheless exercises over Hindus in Sind a spell almost as complete as that
  exercised by it over Muslims" (p. 175, folio 166); his message "lies deep in the hearts of all
  classes of Sind's population, Muslim and Hindu, lettered and unlettered" (pp. 178–179,
  folios 169–170); "the typical lslamic[text-layer?] mysticism. of the Risa.Io[text-layer?] is
  understood afl:d[text-layer?] loyed[text-layer?] in Sind by Hindus as much as by Muslims"
  (p. 225, folio 216), the cause there being "a kind of rapprochement between the
  -de_eper[text-layer?] mystical ide�s[text-layer?] of Muslim and Hindu thought" (p. 225,
  folio 216); the musical mechanism at p. 231 (folio 222, above); and the story mechanism — "The
  thought -and. expression_ are Islamic, coloured by the doctrines of the Sliii[text-layer?]
  philosophy. The music is · Indian. The stories are Hindu, one of. therii[text-layer?]
  frorn[text-layer?] t4e[text-layer?] Punj a.9[text-layer?] , two o f them from Cutch or Kathiawar,
  and the rest are .stoiies[text-layer?] of Lower -Sind'ctirrent[text-layer?] amongst the Hindu
  p eoples liv_in·g[text-layer?] �m[text-layer?] the · bo:r;ders[text-layer?] of the
  T_har[text-layer?] prior to the general conversion .of these . p,eople_[text-layer?] to · Islam"
  (p. 271, folio 262), with the consequence "the present-day Hindu peoples of Sind feel that their.
  cu,lture[text-layer?] n-o[text-layer?] less than the culture of Islam has gone to the fashioning
  qf[text-layer?] the final product" (p. 271, folio 262). **`Sliii`[text-layer?] is the book's word
  for Sufi in that sentence.**
- **The counter-fact Sorley sets against it, and refuses to resolve.** "The religion of the Risalo
  is thoroughly Islamic. God ha_s[text-layer?] at least thirty-four differ�nt[text-layer?]
  appellations, several of them from the .list of the Ninety-Nine'.Names[text-layer?] of God. But
  this would in itself be ··against the- pqpularity_[text-layer?] of tI:ie[text-layer?] poems
  amongst Hindus. As we have seen aJr�ady[text-layer?], the poem�·a:re[text-layer?] loved by
  a�l[text-layer?] classes in Sind" (p. 268, folio 259) — **"at least thirty-four" is a countable
  figure and it survives the text layer intact**; "But this does not alter the fact that the poetry
  of the Risalo is Islamic poetry with an appeal which was .wider[text-layer?] than the Islamic
  poetry of tbe[text-layer?] day" (p. 246, folio 237); "**It would be a great mistake
  howev�r[text-layer?] to assume that the religion of the poet is anything but that of Islam.**"
  (p. 296, folio 287), conceding first that "Here there is an echo of Plotinus and Hellenism :
  there of Alexandrine Christianity with a hint now and then of something that springs from the
  Buddhism and Vedanta doctrines of India". Also "The mysticism of the . Risalo is Islamic, The
  emphasis throughout is guided by the stern monotheism of the· Prophet[text-layer?]" (p. 280,
  folio 271) — **the printed comma where a full stop belongs is as printed**.
- **The caste-indifference couplet, spoken in the poet's own name.** "There came fresh message
  yester night / From Rano. Won is our behest. / The Giver gave it, says Latif. / Of
  comer's[text-layer?] caste why,make[text-layer?] inquest ? / Who came in com!ng[text-layer?]
  welcomed were." (p. 414, folio 405). **The plainest statement of come-as-you-are welcome in the
  book, and it carries the poet's signature in the line immediately before. No gloss, no context
  and no shrine setting is supplied** — **(not stated)**. Compare "The gift,s[text-layer?] of the
  Almighty do hot[text-layer?] depend on caste" (p. 338, folio 329).
- **The reason the poems became popular, stated causally and against the mysticism.** "_Despite the
  depth and sincerity of the mystic philosophy of the Risalo; it is doubtful if the poems would
  have attained their · over­whelming -popularity . had it not been for the fact that several of
  the best known �f[text-layer?] them ·are wiitten[text-layer?] . round folk stories current in the
  Sind· copntrysid�[text-layer?]" (p. 268, folio 259). And, in Sorley's first person: "I have
  myself not the sligJltest[text-layer?] doul?t[text-layer?] : that one great r�a�on[text-layer?]
  for the popular appeal of Shah Abdul .J.,atif'?[text-layer?] poetry )s[text-layer?]
  th�[text-layer?] fact that it w�s[text-layer?] the first great attempt to interpi;e�[text-layer?]
  the · feel�ngs[text-layer?] of the populace in language that all, both Muslim and
  HinduJ[text-layer?] could understand" (p. 246, folio 237).
- **He is the first literary user of Sindhi, and the language is defended in its own right.** "Shah
  Abdul Latif was the first great exponent of the imaginative use of the Sindhi language" (p. 215,
  folio 206); "He is a poet using for the first time with supreme skill the language of the country
  folk" (p. 217, folio 208). The language passage the book does not connect to him: "A language is
  usually a key to a people. The Sindhi language is a beautiful instrument of precision admirably
  adapted to the civilization for which it was intended-a peace-loving agricultural and pastoral
  culture elaborated by an industrious and non-martial people. But it is naturally deficient in
  words suited to the kind of civilization for which Burton stood" (p. 120, folio 111) — **offered
  as a defence of Sind against Richard Burton and flagged in the notes as an unlinked passage.**
- **The poetry is unrepeatable, argued three ways.** "It is extremely doubtful whether poetry like
  Shah Abdul Latif's can ever again be composed in Sind. The verses can of course be imitated and
  have been imitated" (p. 214, folio 205); "the probabilities are all against Sind's producing a
  second Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 215, folio 206); "No one will ever produce again this particular
  kind of poetry amidst the same local environment and in the same halo-content of thought, belief
  and feeling" (p. 215, folio 206).
- **Its present hold, and Sorley's qualification of it.** "the poems of Shah Abdul. Latif still bind
  Sind with a powerful spell of love and admiration" (p. 215, folio 206); but "Modern delight in
  the poems is of a different genre entirely… Though the ideas in the poems are still believed they
  are not believed in quite the same way or for quite the same reason" (p. 215, folio 206). Had
  British education not intervened, the poems "w�uld [text-layer?] have remained a rather vague
  body of sung and recited verse handed down from one singer to another and familiar, as songs; to
  the Sindhis who delight in Hste:r;iing[text-layer?] to this form of literary· composition"
  (pp. 233–234, folios 224–225); "without . the stimulus of English education and · the interest in
  literature produced by it the poems of Shah Abdul Latif would still have been today in much the
  same condition as that in which the 'J3riti?h[text-layer?] found them in 1843" (p. 234,
  folio 225).
- **His genius is unexplained by his background — the governing claim of the Birth of a Classic
  chapter.** "In Shah Abdul Latif's case the enquirer finds small help towards knowing how this
  outstanding poet came to the fulfilment of his·genius. Shah Abdul Latif's life in fact would seem
  to prove the truth of the old adag�[text-layer?] ' poeta nascitur, non fit '" (p. 212, folio 203),
  restated as the chapter's closing line, "They p�ove[text-layer?] inqeed[text-layer?] _ the truth
  of the saying' �hat[text-layer?] the poet is born and. not made" (p. 226, folio 217).
- **His originality is limited in the same breath as it is asserted.** "Shah Abdul Latif cannot be
  called conspicuous for any great originality of thought. But he expresses supremely well a
  species of religious philosophy current amongst the better educated men of his time" (p. 217,
  folio 208); "Shah Abdul Latif is no mere imitator of Jalaluddin Rumi,. of Jami, or Hafiz or Al
  Bistami" (p. 216, folio 207); "The fact that Sind was the home of several skilled exponents of
  Sufi thought during the middle ages accounts fully for the mastery of Sufi expression exhibited
  by Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 252, folio 243) — **no order, no silsila, no khanqah and no named
  exponent is given** — **(not stated)**.
- **Sorley's demotion of him against the Persians, recorded in full because the archive will want
  the author's own limits on his subject.** "In comparison with the achievements o{[text-layer?]
  the great Persian masters Shah Abdul Latif's poetry is as the miniature of the plaque artists
  when set beside the great canvases of Michael Angelo or Rafael ·;[text-layer?] or as when the
  minor elegances of the light operas of Sullivan or Offenbach are put in
  j uxtaposition[text-layer?] with the magnificent music of great operatic composers like Wagner or
  Verdi. The work is fine but it is on an infinitely lower plane of achievement. Excellent in a
  humble way though it be, it is not in the same class" (pp. 252–253, folios 243–244); "the Sindhi
  poet is cast in a much more homely an.cl[text-layer?] simple mould" (p. 253, folio 244); "Such
  sublimity of expression will be looked for in vain in the verses of the Sindhi :poet[text-layer?]"
  (p. 253, folio 244). **Set against chunk 012's verdict "He is incompar­ably the greatest man whom
  Sind has yet produced in the realm of imaginative art" (p. 178, folio 169) — the two are not
  contradictory (one is about Sind, one about Persia) but they belong together.** The counterweight:
  "Yet when ,Shah[text-layer?] Abdul Latif is drawing a moral from some little everyday occurrence
  or stating the simple conviction of the Oneness of things, the delights of union with God, or the
  pangs of separation, he can be fully as effective as any of the great Persian poets. Many of his
  shorter passages would not disgrace them" (pp. 253–254, folios 244–245). And the final
  characterisation: "the genius of the Sindhi poet is essentially lyrical. It is neither
  philosophic nor discursive" (p. 254, folio 245); "Shah Abdul Latif is primarily a poet and not a
  philosopher" (p. 293, folio 284); "Shah Abdul Latif was not a c�nscious[text-layer?] and
  consistent philosopher" (p. 262, folio 253).
- **He is placed at the top of Sind's Sufi poets, and dated.** "Of the Sufi poets of Sind about whom
  an immense modern literature is now poured forth, none are superior to Shah Abdul Latif and most,
  if not all, of them are later in date." (p. 299, folio 290); "Between the thirteenth century when
  Lal Shahbaz flourished and **the eighteenth century when Shah Abdul Latif lived** there is very
  little that can be. said to be securely established in the . way of verified historical fact."
  (p. 299, folio 290) — **a direct statement by the author that the archive's Sindhi material
  between the 1200s and the 1700s is not securely evidenced.** The Preface adds only "this
  eighteenth-century Sindhi poet" (p. 306, folio 297). His literary descent: "an expression of the
  later Sufism in India, especially as influenced by the Muhammadan domination of Upper India from
  the fifteenth century onwards" (p. 284, folio 275); "who represents an Indian Muslim development
  of the philosophy of Jala.Iuddin[text-layer?] Riimi[text-layer?]" (p. 290, folio 281); "how true
  Shah Abdul Latif is to the spirit of Siifi[text-layer?] belief" (p. 265, folio 256); "how Shah
  Abdul Latif is in the authentic line of Sufi poets" (p. 295, folio 286).
- **THE WARNING A SHRINE ARCHIVE MUST CARRY: the Risalo is nearly useless as topography.** "There
  is surprisingly little of Sind topography in the Risalo. Places are indeed mentioned : mountains
  are named and features of Sind scenery receive passing remark. But there is nothing individually
  distinctive of place, mountain or scenery : nothing of the peoples of the land, and nothing
  whatever of the stirring political events of his own day. Where a mountain is named it is more to
  emphasize the danger and difficulty of crossing it than with any idea that its location and
  character have any human interest. **His place names might be any place names for all the value
  they possess as guides to definite localities**" (p. 266, folio 257). The method that follows:
  "the knowledge of Sind and its rustic civilization to be culled from the Risalo is gained from
  the study of internal evidence and not otherwise" (p. 266, folio 257). **This should travel with
  any entry that cites the poems for a place**, and it is reinforced by the story-migration
  argument at p. 270 (folio 261): "The story wanders from Catch[text-layer?] over Lower Sind to
  Kech Mekran… so that names nearly similar have been given as belonging to places in the land of
  the Bahichis[text-layer?]."
- **The silence on politics, stated twice.** "He produced a very considerable quantity of verse. But
  it contains little or no reference to the current political events which he had witnessed. He was
  interested not in the transient phenomena of his age but in eternal verities, which formed a much
  better subject for immortal verse th.an the petty wars and intrigues of the KalhOra[text-layer?]"
  (p. 180, folio 171); "nothing whatever of the stirring political events of his own day" (p. 266,
  folio 257). **The two agree.**
- **The audience the poems were composed for, in the book's own summary of rural Sind — and it puts
  shrines into that landscape.** "It was a land of cultivators and herdsmen, keepers of camels,
  buffaloes, sheep, goats and oxen, **of tombs and Sayids**, of dust and heat, of the annual rise
  and fall of the waters of the Indus but inefficiently controlled by a crude system of irrigation.
  For the common folk living in such a country Shah Abdul Latif composed his verses" (p. 265,
  folio 256). The poems' own subject is the same world: "They are concerned with the open-air life
  of a popula­tion[text-layer?] that works and lives largely in the fields, turning the river
  waters on to the land, and gathering the grain from the threshing floor into the large earthenware
  j ars[text-layer?] that hold a home food-supply sufficient to last till the floods of the Indus
  inundate the lands once again in the due fulfilment of the seasons" (p. 256, folio 247).
- **The text is at two removes from him, and the manuscripts disagree.** "The . poems, as we know
  them today� have; however, suffered from the . fact that they were not written down as composed.
  They have also been subj ected _t o[text-layer?] careless compilation :by persons unfamiliar with
  literary tradition _and . ill-equipped with scholarly taste"; "Thus the poems as we know them
  today are not identical with the verses that came from the lips of Shah Abdul Latif himself as he
  recited them. They are instead the verses as subse­quently written down by others, altered by
  them, and edited by them." (both p. 233, folio 224); "In the form in which they now exist there
  is much extrari~ous[text-layer?] ·matter. It is . often difficult to say with any accuracy ·
  whether a doubtful passage is the work of Shah Abdul Latif or not.· The manuscripts of the poems
  are. very discrepant in this respect." (p. 233, folio 224). **No manuscript count, no shelfmarks
  and no dates for any manuscript** — **(not stated)**; the index carries the topic as "Risalo
  (Shah jo) … (MSS. and editions) 1 70[text-layer?], 1 75[text-layer?] and n, 224, 225" (p. 439),
  **which is the exact page range the archive needs for it**. The transmission account: "Tradition
  says that he did not write his poems at all but that they were collected and transcribed by
  amanuenses. This passes the bounds of credibility�[text-layer?] The poems were certainly later
  collated by scribes and formed into a regular collection which exists in several manuscript
  forms" (pp. 183–184, folios 174–175). The explanation of the form: "Doubtless the poetry of
  Orientals owes th�s[text-layer?] untidiness to its origin in half spontaneous lyrical
  composition, a kind of semi­improvisation which permits thoughts to be uttered exactly as they
  are conceived" (p. 259, folio 250).
- **Two named signs of corruption, and three interpolating poets.** "Among the more obvious signs of
  careless compilation and lack of literary taste are the frequent incorporation in the poems of
  explanatory asides like ' As Shah Latif says ' and the insertion of short verses by other poets,
  as for instance Inayat, Lakhmir and Jiinej o[text-layer?]." (p. 233, folio 224). **The glossary
  confirms all three as poets** — "Iniyat[text-layer?]  Name of a Sindhi poet.";
  "Jiinejo[text-layer?]  Name of a Sindhi poet."; "Lakhmir  Name o'f[text-layer?] a Sindhi poet."
  (pp. 430–431, folios 421–422) — **and the index converges them on one printed page**: "Inayat,
  1 75[text-layer?], 224" (p. 435); "J iinejo[text-layer?], 224" (p. 436); "Lakhmir (in Risalo)
  224" (p. 436). All three appear in the signature position inside the verse: "Inayat says : ' The
  water's dread / Lives in the rolling ·ocean . swell�'[text-layer?] ." (p. 334, folio 325); "' Her
  liberty lies_ in Ari's hand ' / (Let Poet Inayat tell) ." (p. 377, folio 368); "(In the words of
  Inayat)" (p. 367, folio 358); "Daylong I thought of friends (so Lakhmir sings) ." (p. 345,
  folio 336); "' Make Sanwan plans ', saith Junej6[text-layer?]." (p. 346, folio 337). **The book

  carry the name "Inayat"; see the Doubts list.**
- **The poet's own signature inside the verse, in six forms.** "Latif" (pp. 323, 332, 334, 336, 338,
  339, 343, 345, 360–366, 380–397, 414–425; folios 314, 323, 325, 327, 329, 330, 334, 336, 351–357,
  371–388, 405–416); "the Sayid" (pp. 322, 330, 334, 354–366, 382, 400–424; folios 313, 321, 325,
  345–357, 373, 391–415); "Latif the Poet" (pp. 334, 405; folios 325, 396, the latter "Thus hath

  soul.)"); "Abdul Latif" once in full — "Abdul Latif it is / Who makes these harmonies." (p. 356,
  folio 347); and the speaker-label `Sayid.` used as a rubric at the head of his own stanzas

  NOT the poet** and must not be merged with the signature.
- **The text Sorley translated is not the Risalo but a named abridgement.** "The poems which I have
  transla,ted[text-layer?] do not form the complete mass of verse attributed to Shah Abdul Latif.
  They are instead tl;ie[text-layer?] poems contained in the abridged compilation called· the '
  Munta­khab ' arranged by K�zi[text-layer?] Ahmad�[text-layer?]." (p. 234, folio 225). "This
  collection is fully representa­tive of the nature of the poetry and the scope of 'its- subject
  matt~r[text-layer?], and . forms propably the hest[text-layer?] known anthology of the poems in
  use today." (p. 234, folio 225). "The Muntakhab consists of twenty-seven poems of very unequal
  length in a variety of metres." (p. 234, folio 225), the figure repeated at p. 231 (folio 222).
  **No date is given for the compilation** — **(not stated)** — though the bibliography dates a
  printing: "Kazi Ahmad Shah. Risiile[text-layer?] jo Muntakhab. 19IQ[text-layer?] and subsequent
  editions." (p. 191, folio 182). **Chunk 017 records that the book never says what the Muntakhab
  is, who compiled it, when, or how it relates to the Risalo as a whole (p. 268, folio 259) — the
  single largest gap in that chunk for manuscript history.** The index carries Sorley's verdict
  inside the entry: "Muntakhab, (abridgement of Risa.Io[text-layer?]) 222, 225, (arrangement
  unsatis­factory[text-layer?]) 226 ; 23 I[text-layer?], 259, 262" (p. 438). **The canon was
  growing in Sorley's own present**: "Then~~[text-layer?] has lately been a tendency to
  expaiid[text-layer?] and enlarge the volume of Shah Abdul Latifs ' poetry and it bas[text-layer?]
  not always been j udiciously .pursued." (p. 234, folio 225).
- **Sorley re-ordered the poems, and printed his three categories.** "in tr;;mslating[text-layer?]
  the poems I have declined to follow the order of the poems given in the Muntakhab. Instead, while
  keeping · the individual poems entire, · l[text-layer?] have rearranged them according to their
  subject matter" (p. 235, folio 226); the three are (1) "the poems that may be described as
  primarily mystical in form and expression"; (2) "the poems that describe characteristic features
  of Sind rural life, like the coming . of the welcome rain, **the habits of fakirs and holy men**,
  the tragic story of Hassan and Husain,· the thoughts of the-spinning woman"; (3) "the Sindhi love
  stories told by mothers to thei,:[text-layer?] children in tlie[text-layer?] cradle", "which
  forms tne[text-layer?] g~eater[text-layer?] bulk of the poetry" (p. 235, folio 226). **Category
  (2) names the habits of fakirs and holy men as a subject class of the poetry — the nearest the
  book comes to putting shrine content inside the verse.** The Muntakhab's own three classes are
  given separately: "first, the mystery of divine love, second, · the folklore of Sind in its
  love-stories, and third, some prominent ·features of the common experience of the Sind rustic
  countryside" (pp. 234–235, folios 225–226). **In the printed translation the poems carry no sur
  titles at all** — every heading is an English thematic title, and "Not one of the sur names …
  appears as a heading anywhere in these pages"; the sur names survive there only in a concordance
  table at p. 317 "keyed to somebody else's edition, not to this translation".
- **The verse unit is the `bait`, named once.** "One great difficulty in dealing with the mass of
  poems of the Muntakhab lies in the irregular length of the baits… The baits are usually
  self-con­tained[text-layer?]. The thought often changes · abruptly[text-layer?] from one to
  another." and "Personally I regard the irregularity in length as some­thing[text-layer?] worth
  preserving because it is a sign of the spontaneity which is a supreme merit of the original."
  (both p. 307, folio 298).

  folio 261). **The Sasui–Punhun cycle is "the longest collection of germane poems in the Risalo"

  (p. 370; pp. 390–397, folios 381–388) — **all (not stated)** — **which is worth recording
  precisely because a shrine archive will expect a Sasui tomb here.**

- **Sorley's declared methodological limit, in the first person.** "Historically we may trace as far
  as we can the origin of some of these ideas. Philosophically we may analyse the depth of the
  thought or the worth of the metaphysic. Aesthetically we may describe the effect upon the
  introspective mind of him who listens to the poetry in which Sufism is declared. **But beyond
  that we cannot go.**" (p. 297, folio 288). And his last word: "This may be all delusion and
  word-weaving. But if it is merely delusion, the delusion is a very pleasant one, shared by many,
  like Shah Abdul Latif, whos�[text-layer?] verses will continue to delight mankind." (p. 297,
  folio 288).
- **Doctrine, as the book states it.** God and man are apart: "The Sindhj[text-layer?] poet is a
  true M-uslim[text-layer?] in that for him G,0d[text-layer?] and man are things apart and that
  approach .to th�[text-layer?] 'divine ·is possible only. by - a long pedod[text-layer?] of
  struggle and effort" (p. 237, folio 228); "It insists upon the utter unworthiness of man when
  compared with the majesty, mystery and completeness of God. Thus love for Shah Abdul Latif is
  merely the means of approaching the divine. It is not a description of the divine itself."
  (p. 240, folio 231). The mysticism is classified four ways by exclusion: "It is .not a pure
  l~ve[text-layer?] mysticism, nor . a nature mysticism, no:i;[text-layer?] an .
  in.telle_ctual[text-layer?] · mysticism" (p. 236, folio 227) but "a love mysticism with a rich
  religious signifi.cance[text-layer?]" (p. 235, folio 226). The two characteristics said to run
  through all of it: "a belief in the unity, through effort, of the human soul with God, and the
  transitoriness of temporal things, which are a veil hiding the true nature of illumination"
  (p. 291, folio 282). Separation is the master text: "There is hardly a poem in the Risalo that
  does not preach this doctrine" — "' The seas of separation roll / And drown each single separate
  soul.'" (p. 292, folio 283, from `Sur Kalyan`). Self is the great obstacle and the great means
  (p. 294, folio 285). Oneness against twoness is stated twice in nearly the same words on widely
  separated pages (pp. 280, 321, 337; folios 271, 312, 328). The technical pair is printed twice in
  two spellings: "ishq maj azi[text-layer?] and ishq hakikki[text-layer?]" (p. 262, folio 253) and
  "ishk hakkiki … ishk maj azi[text-layer?]" (p. 291, folio 282), glossed only as "false and true
  love"; **no Arabic or Persian term is printed for "non-existence"** — **(not stated)** (p. 383,
  folio 374). "Die before you die" has a whole section (XXX) to itself (pp. 293, 388; folios 284,
  379). The imagery inventory: the moth and the flame, "clay in the oven", the wine and the
  wineshop "familiar to all lovers of Omar Khayyam", the camel as "the stupid waywardness of the
  human heart", the Healer or Physician, the Friend or Beloved, and "The commonest image, however,
  in the Risalo is the struggle on the ' path ' or the ' way ' to the Beloved" (pp. 262–264,
  folios 253–255). **The one explicitly economic image**: "In the beautiful poem of the Spinning
  Woman, which has a deep mystical meaning, God is the banker, the saraf or merchant, who weighs
  out the spun yarn and rewards the righteous spinners according to their merits" (p. 264,
  folio 255) — **"saraf" is the only piece of commercial vocabulary given in the original.**
- **The material world inside the verse, which is where the book's social and economic content for
  this row actually sits.** The spinners: the door-to-door tout (pp. 366–367, folios 357–358); the
  merchant who "when spinners he calls" convenes the workforce (p. 365, folio 356); weighing,
  inspection and a spinner questioned "Apart" (p. 367, folio 358); "an ounce in the tare" as the
  only named unit of weight (p. 367, folio 358); and the deserted-market stanza — "The wheels are
  dismantled. The spinners where flown ? … they have gone and closed up their huts. / In the midst
  of the trees no cotton plants are : / So here are no spinners. The empty bazaar / Has ensalted my
  heart. '" (p. 367, folio 358). The merchant voyage: six named commodities, "Cloves, cardamoms and
  store of cloth, / Sweet-smelling grass and ambergris" and "cloth and pearls" (pp. 333–334,
  folios 324–325); the sailing season named by the north wind and the off-season work of twisting
  lanyards and oiling boats (pp. 329–330, folios 320–321); five fare words in eight lines — "scot",
  "lot", "fee", "money-lot", "purse" — and a stated refusal of carriage without payment (p. 330,
  folio 321); the twelve-month absence (p. 330, folio 321); named parts of a vessel, hatches, keel,
  strake, timbers, rudder, prow, spar, cable, hawser, anchor, lanyards, canvas, bunting, sails,
  sheets (pp. 331–332, folios 322–323). The craft-decline lament: "The lapidaries now are gone /
  Wh9[text-layer?] diam.mid[text-layer?] pierce4[text-layer?] and ruby red… Where
  crafts�en[text-layer?] wrought of yor.e,[text-layer?] the smiths / · Beat worthless - _ pewter
  ·now _ in�tead.[text-layer?]" (p. 333, folio 324). Grain and famine: "0 Lord of Rain, . for
  Allah's sake… Make / The grain that groweth cheap to buy." (p. 344, folio 335); "The
  grain-amassers, mad on gain, / Do wring their hands : and fifteen grows / From .five-a threefold
  quickening… May all who trade in famfue[text-layer?] greed, / May all the mise.rs[text-layer?]
  disappear. ." (p. 347, folio 338) — **"fifteen … From five" is the only numerical relation in
  chunk 020 and it is printed as words, not numerals**; "The corn is cheap : there's
  butte,r[text-layer?] in the jar" (p. 345, folio 336). Transhumance ordered by the rain: "Take the
  cattle out / And make your way across the plain. / Desert your huts. Your panniers fill"
  (p. 343, folio 334). The water-storage pits "That rnen[text-layer?] scooped mi([text-layer?] in
  days: agone" (p. 346, folio 337). The textile economy of the Marui poems — silk, lac-dyed coarse
  cloth, shawls, wool, striped cloth, velvet, broadcloth against "My coarse rough blanket" — set
  against a herding economy of goatherds, herdsmen, huts and flocks (pp. 427–429, folios 418–420).
  Camel husbandry in working detail, tying, binding, shackling, hobbling and saddling (pp. 335–336,
  folios 326–327). The potters' kiln described in working detail and made the figure of concealed
  love (p. 372, folio 363). And, in the Sasui headnote, "a famine, an inter-state grain purchase, a
  negotiated price remission and a camel caravan" — "Owing to famine in Kech the people of that
  land had sent a deputation to Bhambh6r[text-layer?] to purchase corn. The leader of the caravan
  from Kech succeeded in obtaining a remission in the price of the grain from Sasui by promising to
  bring Punhiin[text-layer?]" (p. 370). **Not one of the trades named anywhere in the translation
  is attached to a shrine, a saint or an endowment** — **(not stated)**.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

**Person-level attachment.** `bhit-bhit-shah` is the book's dominant row (406 single-id bullets),
but in several chunks the attachment is to **the poet, not to the site**: chunk 004 states that "The
mapping to `bhit-bhit-shah` rests on the running head alone" across pp. 54–66, and chunk 015 that
"'Bhit' appears in this chunk only in the running head … and in the book's title. The
`bhit-bhit-shah` mapping therefore rests entirely on the poet, not on any mention of the site";
chunks 001 and 005 say the same of pp. 1–23 and pp. 67–79. **Since the running head is itself
damaged on nearly every page, and prints `BRIT` on seventy-odd of them, a mapping that rests on the
running head alone rests on a damaged string.**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind", "the language of Sind"; refers to: poems "composed in-the first half of the eighteenth .century" and their standing in the author's own day; author's argument): the Risalo "is the only classic which the language of Sind has yet produced in the realm of deeply imaginative literature"; "In Sind his poems are · held in such universal and popular esteem as is accorded only to poetry which has successfally [text-layer?] [successfully] interpreted the most intimate thoughts and the sincerest feelings of a people. In some fori:n [text-layer?] [form] or other_ the verses are. known to all classes" (p. 7, folio not stated); the poetry "exercises over Hindus in Sind a spell almost as complete as that exercised by it over Muslims" (p. 175, folio 166), and its appeal lies "deep in the hearts of all classes of Sind's population, Muslim and Hindu, lettered and unlettered" (pp. 178–179, folios 169–170).
- (book's word: "the people of Sind"; refers to: the author's own day set against an unnamed past, period not stated; author's dedicatory verse, "DEDICATED TO THE PEOPLE OF SIND"): "Gone are the days when tempered blade / And matchlocks'. fire laid waste your ·fields. / The drums of peacefulness are struck : / Your land a full abundance yields." (p. 9, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the province"; refers to: "the period 1690-1760"; author's argument): "Nowhere is the need for an adequate description of the Moghul power in its decline felt more than in the case of Sind. There the growing ineffectiveness of the suzerainty of Delhi produced local events which shaped the destiny of the province · in a manner definitely individual"; the task is hard "owing to the extreme paudty [text-layer?] [paucity], indeed the almost total absence, of reliable contemporary record ... for the actual years of the period 1690-1760" (p. 14, folio 5); the Company's records for the poet's lifetime "(1689-1752) [are] totally devoid of any reference to Sind" (p. 28, folio 19); "There is no European record for Sind between the years I662 [text-layer?] [1662] and 1758" (p. 42, folio 33).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 1592 to "the first decade of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Sind was added to the Moghul Empire by Akbar in 1592. It remained an integral part of the Empire till the disastrous advent of N adir Sh ah [text-layer?] [Nadir Shah] in 1737 when it fel l [text-layer?] [fell] first under Persian and then in 1747 under Afghan dominance"; "the Moghul governors appointed from Hindust an [text-layer?] [Hindustan] gave place to Sindhi governors bowing the knee as little as they dared to Moghul, Persian and Afgh an [text-layer?] [Afghan] in turn. By 1760 the ruling dynasty of the Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhora] was almost in fact but not at all in theory independent"; the Talpurs were "never in fact completely independent of their Afgh an [text-layer?] [Afghan] ove rlords [text-layer?] [overlords] till th e [text-layer?] [the] first decade of the nineteenth century" (p. 18, folio 9). The five stages are dated: Kalhora governors from "1701"; "by 1736" semi-independent; "after the invasion of Nadir Shah in 1739" Persian suzerainty and tribute; "about 1747" suzerainty passing "to the Pathan kingdom at Kabul"; from "1778" the Kalhora–Talpur civil war (p. 35, folio 26); a Company letter of 8 April 1758 (quoted source) has the Patans subduing "the whole Principality of Scindy" and making "it tributary to them, as it had been to the Persians ever since Nadir Shaw's conquest" (p. 63, folio 54); in the poet's life Nadir Shah "made Sind tributary to Persia" and Ahmad Shah Durani "made Sind subject to Kabul" (p. 180, folio 171).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the Kalhora and Talpurs, "for one hundred years" up to "the British conquest in 1843"; author's argument): their "combined efforts" established "in Sind a petty Muslim state which preserved, up till the time of the British conquest in 1843,. the characteristic features of Moghul administration and added to that a peculiar brand of Islamic quasi-theocracy ... a policy of splendid isolation" (p. 19, folio 10); "The aim of all Sind policy from 1701 onwards was to make Sind independent of Moghul, Persian and Pathan, to diminish the payment of tribute ... to preserve the land as a closed terrain into which no foreigners of any sort were allowed entrance except with the utmost difficulty"; Sind in 1843 "was then to all intents and purposes a sovereign and petty Muslim state" (pp. 35–36, folios 26–27); "the Kalh6ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora] and Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs] between them succeeded in keeping Sind shut to the influences of the outside world for a hundred and fifty years" (p. 41, folio 32).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the days of Shah Jahan to the days of Sir Charles Napier", "from 1600 to 1840"; author's argument): "there is little reason to doubt that the peoples of Sind in the first half of the eighteenth century lived more or less in the way in which Sir Charles Napi�r [text-layer?] [Napier] found them living in 1843" (p. 19, folio 10); "the main administrative structure of the government remained largely unaltered from the days of Shah Jahan to the days of Sir Charles Napier. The economic foundation was not greatly changed despite the rise, towards the second half of the eighteenth century, of the four large towns of modern Sind, Karachi, Hyderabad, Sukkur and Shikarpiir [text-layer?] [Shikarpur], which displaced Tatta, Nasarpiir [text-layer?] [Nasarpur], Rohri, Sehwan, Kandiaro"; the social structure was "made static ... by the isolation of Sind" (p. 30, folio 21); the Sind of Napier "was not very different in essentials from the Sind of Ghulam Shah Kalh6ro [text-layer?] [Kalhoro]", which in turn "did not differ ... from the Sind of Akbar, Jahangir, Shah Jahan and Aurangzeb" (p. 31, folio 22); "the framework of the Moghul administration in Sind remained little altered in essentials from 1600 to 1840, the change in spirit was considerable" (p. 153, folio 144).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the land "throughout the centuries" and in the author's day; author's argument): "Sind is difficult country for the historian"; the terrain is "woefully devoid of informative historical monuments"; "The land is an alluvial tract over which throughout the centuries the Indus has wandered in a multitud� [text-layer?] [multitude] of shifting channels. The buildings were constructed mostly of mud, sticks or wattles, just as they are outside all the large towns today"; the climate "for six months in the year is as hot as anything to be found in India and is cursed with dust storms"; "A shifting alluvial plain liable to inundation, surrounded on every side but that of the sea by forbidding desert or barren hilly country" (pp. 19–20, folios 10–11). Elsewhere: "Sind is deficient in hu,ilding [text-layer?] [building] stone" (p. 101, folio 92); "mefal [text-layer?] [metal] does not exist in Sind" (p. 108, folio 99); greenery is "not common in the arid land of Sind except in the immediate vicinity of the river or its large riverain branches" (p. 182, folio 173).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Sind's history "throughout time", "from rooo [text-layer?] [1000] A.D. onwards", and "between 1659 and 1758"; author's argument): "For the most part Sind has remained apart from the really crucial events happening in the rest of neighbouring Asia"; "the restless tide of Indian h1story [text-layer?] [history] beats upon the barren reef of Sind ' s isolation"; Sind's history is "episodic, isolated, characteristic of the non -bellicose [text-layer?] [non-bellicose] nature of the bulk of its population throughout time, and as showing ·to a late date the theo cratic [text-layer?] [theocratic] foundation of Islamic society" (p. 21, folio 12); "No event of prime i mportance [text-layer?] [importance] graced the annals of Sind between 1659 and 1758"; the history "has been strangely monotonous, self-contained and of l ittle [text-layer?] [little] interest to the outside world", isolated by "the nature of the country with its fortresses of desert and barren land on all sides and its climate which con fines act ive campa igning [text-layer?] [confines active campaigning] to a period of a few months annually" and by "an incalcula ble [text-layer?] [incalculable] river" (p. 22, folio 13); in Indian history "from rooo [text-layer?] [1000] A.D. onwards Sind receives hardly any mention", and Moreland found it impossible "to include Sind in his economic reconstruction of the Moghul Empire" (p. 23, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the land"; refers to: "since the time of the Siimra [text-layer?] [Sumra] in the thirteenth century and onwards" and "1843"; author's argument): "The peoples of Sind, whether indigenous or immigrant, have never shown any military genius ... The people as a whole have always been peaceful and industrious"; "the land has been predominantly Muslim in population and the government had a strong theocratic basis with much virtual power in the hands of the priestly class of Sayids"; the British "entered on Sind as if it were almost an unknown land" (p. 23, folio 14); the Sindhi language suits "a peace-loving agricultural and pastoral culture elaborated by an industrious and non-martial people" (p. 120, folio 111).
- (book's word: "the country"; refers to: "up to the days of the British conquest"; book's historical prose): the five native histories of Sind "are all written in Persian, which was the Court language of the ruling houses of the country up to the days of the British conquest" (p. 24, folio 15).
- (book's word: "Siibah [text-layer?] [Subah] of Multan", "Upper and Lower Sind"; refers to: from Akbar's conquest "in 1592" to "as late as 1843"; book's historical prose — the book itself marks Moghul Sind as part of another unit): "What is certain is that Upper and Lower Sind were never definitely united till a much later day"; "even as late as 1843 ... unification between Upper and Lower Sind was not complete"; under Akbar "the country as a whole was made part of the Siibah [text-layer?] [Subah] of Multan and sarkars were established in two places, Bakhar in the north and Tatta in the Indus deltaic reiion [text-layer?] [region]"; "From the time of Shah Jahan till the first decade of the eighteenth century" the Moghul's authority was wielded by officials "sent from other parts of the Empire"; with the Kalhora "the repre­sentative of the Emperor was a native of Sind" (pp. 31–32, folios 22–23); restated: "Sind consisted of two sarkars (divisions) of the siibah [text-layer?] [subah] (province) of Multan. The headquarters of the sarkars were Bakhar for Upper Sind and Tatta for Lower Sind" (p. 150, folio 141). Quoted source, Major P. Goldney's report: "Upper Sind at the period of the invasion of Hindustan and the sack of Delhi by Nadir Shah formed part of the Sii bah [text-layer?] [Subah] of Multan", and the "Moghulee" portion (Sukkur, Bukkar, Shikarpur) was then annexed to the Durani kingdom (pp. 38–39, folios 29–30; repeated p. 164, folio 155).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "one hundred and fifty years after its annexation by Akbar"; author's argument): "The history of the Moghul occupation of Sind is full of examples" of conflict between local and imperial revenue demands; "The internal history of Sind for one hundred and fifty years after its annexation by Akbar shows as clearly as any other part of the Moghul dominions these characteristic deficiencies of Moghul government" (pp. 33–34, folios 24–25).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Lower. Indus valley"; refers to: the Kalhora period, with Lieut. James's report "1847"; book's historical prose and quoted source, Lieut. James on the Chandookah pargana): the Kalhora "were a Sindhi tribe of obscure origin of the type that is now classed as J amot [text-layer?] [Jamot], that is, non-Baliich [text-layer?] [non-Baluch] original inhabitants of the Indus valley", "the first Sindhi dynasty to wield permanent power since the fall of the Samma"; descent from a sainted mendicant is "a kind of being who has always made a very vivid appeal to the Muslim inhabitants of the Lower. Indus valley" (p. 36, folio 27). James: "Chandookah in common with the rest of Sind has been the scene of many a bloody conflict ... we can subsequently see · in Sind but one continued battle­field, the scene of usurpation, tyranny and bloodshed ... till finally the British bayonet gave the worn-out country peace" (pp. 36–37, folios 27–28).
- (book's word: "the Sind population", "a distracted land"; refers to: the Kalhora supremacy and the Talpurs after it; author's argument): "It was certainly unfortunate for the masses of the Sind population · that a dynasty of their own people ... should have proved so poor a substitute for the government of the Moghuls"; the Talpurs "did succeed in giving a distracted land peace for forty years" (pp. 40–41, folios 31–32).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the land"; refers to: from the earlier Kalhora to 1843; book's historical prose and author's argument): Baluchis "then settled in Sind in considerable numbers" helped the Talpurs destroy "the failing powers of the Sindhi ruling family" (p. 35, folio 26); "The Baliichis [text-layer?] [Baluchis] had by this time become the strongest military force in the land. The earlier Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhora] had encouraged Baliichis to settle in Sind" (p. 40, folio 31); under the Kalhora the government became "a sort of co-operation between the KalhOra and the chief Baliichi tribes"; the Talpurs "were able to weld Sind :fnto [text-layer?] [into] a more complete unity than it had ever known ... But this unity was achieved by asserting a Baliich hegemony which had unfortunate results upon the non-Baliichi elements in th.e population, especially the Hindus"; "Under the Moghnls [text-layer?] [Moghuls] and the KalhOra Sind was never at peace. Under the Tfilpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs] it was. But the price of peace was the tyranny of a Baliich minority" (pp. 151–152, folios 142–143); "Baliichis as a class are not at all intolerant", the intolerance being traced to the Kalhora "as fanatical Muslims" (p. 152, folio 143).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper Sind", "central S1.nd" [text-layer?] [Sind], "Middle Sind"; refers to: the early seventeenth century to 1699; book's historical prose and quoted sources — Withington, Company letters, Hamilton): Withington's narrative "is eloquent of the state of disorder prevailing in that part of .Sinq [text-layer?] [Sind] in the early days of the seventeehth [text-layer?] [seventeenth] century, and shows how weak was Moghul control 9ver [text-layer?] [over] areas outside easy · distance of the larger towns" (p. 78, folio 69; again p. 81, folio 72); the Sameja were in 1640 "in rebellion against the Moghul Governor to the disturb­ance of the peace of central S1.nd" [text-layer?] [Sind] (p. 51, folio 42) and in 1656 were pillaging "in Middle Sind" (p. 56, folio 47); a 1656 letter shows "The extent to which the BalU.chi [text-layer?] [Baluchi] population were outside the effective control of the Moghul officers" (p. 55, folio 46); in 1699 "travelling in Sind was still an undertaking not to be lightly faced. There was very little security or safety and the countryside was infested by bands of robbers" (p. 90, folio 81), "the later Moghul empire in Sind was no better than the early one in preserving law and order" (p. 91, folio 82); Hamilton's picture shows "the Moghul Empire in Sind at the end of the seventeenth century ... the lawlessness in the countryside, the absolute autocracy of the Governor ... the thriving river trade", yet "near the centre of government the Moghul administra�ion [text-layer?] [administration] was, within its limits, not lacking in practical efficiency" (p. 94, folio 85).
- (book's word: "Upper Sind", "those upper countries of Sehwan or Seuestan"; refers to: 1644–1656; quoted source, East India Company letters): Spiller, 1644: "the people are so exceedingly opprest and kept so miserably poor that notwithstanding the soil is fertile and propper and would produce large quantities of good indicoes they have nether the will nor means to manure and sow the ground" (p. 52, folio 43); trade in Upper Sind was harder to finance than in Lower Sind (pp. 52–53, folios 43–44); "In 1647 there was much unsettlement in Upper Sind" from a conflict of authority (p. 53, folio 44); "The bad government of Upper Sind and the corruption of the petty officials receive caustic comment in the records of 1652" (p. 55, folio 46).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Scinda", "Scindye"; refers to: 1659–1660 and other named years; quoted source, Company letters, and author's argument): "In that year and the next, Sind was ravaged by famine and plague": "the famine and plague in Scinda is so great that it hath swept away most part of the people"; "neaver famine raged worse in any place, the living being hardly able to burye the dead" (p. 56, folio 47); the author repeats the 1659 famine, adds Aurangzeb's jaghir yielding little "as the result of drought, locusts, plague and floods in three successive seasons" (1648–52) and Hamilton's 1696 plague, and judges that "in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries public health in Sind was not good" (p. 143, folio 134); Burnes (quoted) on "The unhealthiness of Lower Sind": epidemics, ague, fevers and ophthalmia from dust (p. 144, folio 135).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Scindy"; refers to: "1754-8", "1756 to 1758" and "about the year 1757"; book's historical prose and quoted source, Robert Erskine, 14 November 1760): "from 1756 to 1758 Sind wasin [text-layer?] [was in] a pitiable condition of unrest"; "the troubled condition of Sind at this period, 1754-8, was merely typical of what was happening elsewhere" (p. 39, folio 30); "Sind no less than the rest of India was in confusion" in 1758 (p. 46, folio 37); Erskine: "was Scindy under so good and quiet a government and as plentiful in grain and most other necessaries of life as it ever was till about the year 1 75 7" [text-layer?] [1757], it would profit the Company (p. 66, folio 57).
- (book's word: "Scindy"; refers to: Moghul times and "about 35 years agoe" from 1775; quoted source, Mr Callendar's account, P.D.D. 1775): "While the Mogul's government flourished Scindy was a province of the Empire and being considered as an Honorary appointment was annually bestowed on some person of rank whose expences civil and military generally exceeded the revenues" (p. 61, folio 52); "Scindy tho a small track of country is naturally fertile and was rich and populous when it unfortunately fell under the government of this family about 35 years agoe, but has gradually declined ever since"; "Its chief political connections are with the Patan and Siccs" (pp. 61–62, folios 52–53).
- (book's word: "the Sind Government", "Sind"; refers to: 1758–75 and the tribute from Nadir Shah to Timur Shah; author's argument and quoted source, Callendar via the author): the Sind Government regarded the Afghan government at Kabul "as a form of unwarranted alien oppression" and its customs and transit dues were "as unreasonable and oppressive as they had been in the Moghul days" (pp. 57–58, folios 48–49); "In Sind, however, this change never took place. The Company was always very much the timid guest of the Sind Government" (p. 58, folio 49); the tribute was reduced "from 21 lakhs (Nadir Shah l7J9 [text-layer?] [1739]) to 14 lakhs (Ahmad Shah 1747), t o I I [text-layer?] [to 11] lakhs (Ahmad Shah about 1760), to , 7 lakhs (Timiir Shah about . 1775)" and "was always in arrears" (p. 154, folio 145).
- (book's word: "Sind coasts", "the Sind coast"; refers to: the 1750s–1760s; book's historical prose and quoted source, Company letter of 1758): pirates "in those days infested the Gujarat, Kathiawar and Sind coasts" (p. 58, folio 49); "indeed the whole coast are rogues on both s�des [text-layer?] [sides]" (p. 63, folio 54); "The existence o f pirates o n the Sind coast caused continual anxiety to shippers" (p. 71, folio 62).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi craftsmen"; refers to: 1760–64; book's historical prose from Company records): letters note "the paucity of skilled workmen available in Sind" (p. 67, folio 58) and "The inability of Sind to provide suitable artisans" (p. 72, folio 63); "It was impossible to get Sindhi craftsmen capable of making the kind of article needed" (p. 75, folio 66); "artificers could not be procured in Sind" for saltpetre coppers (p. 105, folio 96).
- (book's word: "Sindhi Muhammadans", "Sind"; refers to: the author's own day, present tense, applied to 1759; author's argument): "As Sindhi Muhammadans are notoriously unhappy away from Sind, it was perhaps their fondness for desertion that made them un­suitable in Bombay" (p. 64, folio 55).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the 1640s, the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; author's argument from Company records): "even at that time big business was in the hands of Hindu traders, some of whorn [text-layer?] [whom] did succeed in acquiring considerable wealth" (p. 54, folio 45); brokers, agents and dalals "were mostly Hindus" who could not display opulence (p. 100, folio 91); "In Sind there were many considerable Hindu traders on whom tl�e [text-layer?] [the] Company's agents relied" (p. 113, folio 104).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Synda"; refers to: the seventeenth century; author's argument and quoted source, Fremlen 1636): Sind, "with its internal waterway system of the Indus, its easy connexion with Lahore and Multan ... its thriving cotton industry, and its vast emporium at Tatta with its busy port at Laribunder, was soon envisaged as a profitable centre of trade with Europe" (p. 97, folio 88); Fremlen, 1636: "For of all Indian goods none are in such request as those of Synda" (p. 51, folio 42); "Tatta and Nasarpiir [text-layer?] [Nasarpur] were the main centres of the industry in Sind" (p. 102, folio 93).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; author's argument): "In Sind itself transport was all by pack animals, mostly camels ... Wheeled traffic was practically unknown"; "The oriental system of markets and fairs on saints' days at saints' tombs was well established ... In fact in this respect Sind's commerce was · on the old medieval system" (p. 98, folio 89); "trade in Sind was on a very well-organized basis, both in respect of land and water j ourneys [text-layer?] [journeys]; the business was soundly established and the government supervision very thorough" (p. 100, folio 91).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "about the time of the British annexation", "after half a century of Talpiir [text-layer?] [Talpur] rule", against "the days of the Moghul and KalhOro rule"; book's historical prose from Postans): "Sind had then for nearly a hundred years ceased to be a source of supply of cottons for the European market"; "In fact Sind was now importing English chintzes and calicoes instead of exporting her own"; exports were "rice, ghi, opium, indigo, dried fish and leather"; "Upper Sind supplying Lower Sind with dry grains ... ghi, cotton and indigo and receiving in return rice, fish, manufactured leather and arms"; this was trade after Talpur rule "during which the chief object had been to shut Sind off from the outside world" (pp. 100–101, folios 91–92).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro times", with comparisons to the author's day; author's argument and ethnographic present): "During Moghul and Kalhoro times the chief industries were the manufacture of cotton goods, saltpetre and indigo, leather and fish"; hand flour-milling, oil pressing and the potter's craft "can still be seen in Sind exactly as it was in Moghul and Kalhoro days"; apart from those industries "Sind deserves little special notice industrially" (pp. 101–102, folios 92–93); "the popular modem [text-layer?] [modern] idea of Sind as a desert is as fallacious as the view that seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind was a purely agricultural country ... Sind supported half a dozen thriving industries", with camels and buffaloes supporting much of the population; Crowe (quoted): "Camels are bred in every part of Sind" (p. 110, folio 101).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Middle Sind"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries; book's historical prose): saltpetre production was "estimated up to 10,000 maunds (crude) annually" (p. 104, folio 95); "The supply from Sind was never more than a useful auxiliary of the main supply obtained from Bengal and Bihar", and "The resources of Sind were, however, running low towards the end of the second factory's days" (p. 105, folio 96); of indigo, "Sind was never very important as a source of supply ... Indigo growing was concentrated mostly in Middle Sind ... The quality was, however, inferior" (p. 106, folio 97).
- (book's word: "Sind", "a Muslim land"; refers to: "always", the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, and the author's day; author's argument and ethnographic present): "Sind has always been a land with large numbers of buffaloes and oxen"; "Sind had always, as it still has, a plentiful supply of hides"; "Sind saddlery and ornamental leather-work ... were famous" (p. 107, folio 98); "In Sind, a Muslim land, this prejudice against leather is not felt by three-quarters of the population" (p. 108, folio 99).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: "always", the Ain-i-Akbari's day, and "the days of the second factory"; author's argument and quoted source, Ain-i-Akbari): "Sind has always been noted for the excellence of its fresh and salt water fish"; the Ain says "the staple food in Lower Sind ' consists of rice and fish'"; "The Lower Sind littoral was, with Gujarat, Kanara and Malabar, one of the great areas which supplied this indigenous seafaring population" (pp. 108–109, folios 99–100).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "By 1770", "from 1775 onwards" to "after 1843"; author's argument): "Sind suffered with the rest of cotton manufacturing India", and the later Kalhora turned "Sind into a self-contained Muslim state closed for nearly three­quarters of.a century to Englishmen ... Sind relapsed into a condition of lethargy which it maintained till, -after 1843"; "The decline of Sind's trade from 1775 onwards was not due to the incapacity of the Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhora] or the narrow parochialism ·of the Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs]" (p. 116, folio 107).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 1836 and the nineteenth-century British accounts; quoted source, Assistant-Surgeon Heddle, May 1836, and author's argument): Heddle: "The people of Sind have also been much decried ... The large masses of the indigenous population are particularly industrious ... The merchants of Sind are active and intelligent, well protected though heavily taxed by the Government. . . . The people are orderly and obedient and the laws are respected" (pp. 116–117, folios 107–108). The author holds that "many of the criticisms of Victorian writers are unjust" (p. 30, folio 21), that Burton "despised Sind for what he considered its lax Muhammadanism" (p. 120, folio 111), and that Burton's view that Sind "came into British hanas [text-layer?] [hands] in an almost exhausted state" cannot be accepted "without considerable qualification" (p. 143, folio 134).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro days" against the author's present, and earlier Arab and Moghul sources; author's argument): "Of Sind it is particularly untrue. Sind is one of those lucky lands that are not seriously troubled if the monsoon·rains fail, because Sind is the creation, the slow geographical accretion, of a river"; its calamities are "unchecked inunda­tion, of drifting sand that covers fertile land, of changing river channels" (p. 118, folio 109); "Sind has always been able within its limits to' [text-layer?] support in modest comfort a tolerably well-fed population"; "The land was considered a sort of Arabia Felix with a quiet, industrious and intelligent people living in isolation from the rest of the Indian continent" (p. 119, folio 110); "famines such as devastated less fortunate parts of India had ordinarily no terrors for the Sindhi peasant" (p. 129, folio 120); "Sind was relatively immune from famines" (p. 143, folio 134).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the Moghul and Kalhoro days", with figures for 1828, 1832 and 1847 and "the present day"; author's argument and quoted sources): "In the Moghul and Kalhoro days Sind did not support one quarter of the population it now supports" (p. 118, folio 109); Del Hoste "calculated in 1832 that the population was 850,000, of which about one-fourth were Hindus", Burnes in 1828 "about one million" (p. 126, folio 117); "between 1740 and 1 800 [text-layer?] [1800] there was a great shifting of the population of Sind, the chief concentration altering from Tatta to Shikarpiir [text-layer?] [Shikarpur], Hyderabad and Karachi"; the Commissioner in Sind in 1847 put the population "at l,274,732 [text-layer?] [1,274,732]" on "48 ,000 [text-layer?] [48,000] square miles"; "during Moghul and Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] days the population cannot have exceeded one million altogether, or less than a quarter of what it is at the present day" (p. 127, folio 118).
- (book's word: "Upper and Lower Sind", "the Chandookah and the Lar"; refers to: 1847 and "just as they are today"; quoted source, the Commissioner in Sind, 1847): bajri and jowari "more prevalent in the Upper", wheat and barley "in the Lower ·portion. Rice is grown partially to the northward but more e�tensively [text-layer?] [extensively] in the low southern parts" (p. 127, folio 118); "The Chandookah and the Lar were the two great rice producing areas just as they are today"; cultivators "were considered in 1847 as being ordinarily in better circumstances than in most parts of India" (p. 128, folio 119).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind"; author's argument): "The society was an agricultural and pastoral one with cottage industry well-diffused and with town-life, as we understand it today, concentrated in Tatta only" (p. 121, folio 112).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the earliest days", "Moghul and Kalh6ro [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] times", the Talpurs, Col. Scott's report of 1853, and the author's day; author's argument and quoted source, Lieut.-Col. Scott): "It is impossible to exaggerate the important place irriga­tion in Sind has always played", "where rainfall is scanty and never regular" (p. 121, folio 112); "The Sind canals, Persian wheeis [text-layer?] [wheels] and wells were constructed by the natives of the land without any assistance from the Empire of Delhi" (p. 122, folio 113); "There is not the slightest doubt that' skilful irrigation has existed in Sind since the earliest days" (p. 135, folio 126); "Sind has always been a pioneer of irrigation in India"; Kalhora irrigation was probably "superior to that prevailing during the regime of the Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs]"; Scott: "the whole system of canals in Scinde is one of makeshifts and expedients" (p. 138, folio 129); lift methods "are still in use today and can be seen all over Sind" (p. 139, folio 130); the canal system "during Moghul and Kalhorn [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] days must, within its limits, have been a very important source of the country's wealth" (pp. 142–143, folios 133–134).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Lower Sind", "rural Sjnd" [text-layer?] [rural Sind]; refers to: Moghul days, the Talpur period and "today"; author's argument, ethnographic present and quoted sources, Pottinger, von Orlich, Lieut. James): the agricultural and pastoral population lived in huts that could be put up and moved with the floods; nineteenth-century accounts "accord very much with present-day conditions in agricultural and pastoral areas in Sind" (p. 123, folio 114); "The Talpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpurs], like true Baliichis [text-layer?] [Baluchis], set little store upon magnificent buildings", and "Baliich [text-layer?] [Baluch] influence wa� [text-layer?] [was] partly at least responsible for the decline in the standard of building" (pp. 123–124, folios 114–115); of von Orlich's 1842 Lower Sind clay houses, "All who are acquainted with Lower Sind today will recognize the truthfulness of this picture" (p. 124, folio 115); wind scoops are "still in universal use in Lower Sind to catch the south-west wind" (p. 49, folio 40); "The Sindhi village must in fact have reminded one strongly of the villages of early medieval Europe ... This description is still true in most particulars of the small agricultural villages scattered throughout the length and breadth of rural Sjnd [text-layer?] [Sind] today" (p. 125, folio 116), with Hindu traders in the bazaar and "the Musulman cultivators" in landhis round it (p. 126, folio 117).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and Kalhoro times" against "today"; author's argument): forced labour (begari) "had always been in practice" in Sind, "Indeed several of the biggest canals were dug by labour con­scripted in this way" (p. 130, folio 121); "The labourers, artisans and peasants were therefore relatively little worse off in Moghul and Kalhoro times than they are today" (p. 131, folio 122); the moneylender in Sind advanced "money against a share of the crop ... The system still prevails"; children herding cattle are still "one of the chief obstacles in the education of the peasant class" (p. 132, folio 123); "In Sind no less than in India generally throughout the Moghul and Kalh6ro [text-layer?] [Kalhoro] periods, circumstances continually conspired to grind the poor in their poverty" (p. 135, folio 126).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Moghul times and 1762; book's historical prose): "three kinds of rupees were in circulation in Sind" (p. 70, folio 61); Moghul coins "except possibly the pagoda, were used in Sind" with laris "in common use in Southern Persia, Sind and Western Asia" (p. 133, folio 124).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sindhi", "the Sindians"; refers to: "are, and always have been", Moghul and Kalhoro days, Crowe, and the author's day; author's argument, ethnographic present and quoted source, Crowe): "The peasantry of Sind are, and always have been, a happy-go-lucky people fond of laughter and amusement"; the Sindhi's "fondness ... for conversation and listening to songs and stories", and the Id and Holi/Diwali festivals celebrated with "whole-heartedness" (p. 145, folio 136); Crowe: "The Sindians are excessively fond of singing" (p. 146, folio 137); wrestling is "as popular as it is today, when it has claims to being the most popular sport of the country"; folk dances, "of which Sind possessed and still possesses a great variety"; polo "does not appear to have been played in Sind" (pp. 148–149, folios 139–140); "Intoxication with liquor and drugs was undoubtedly a curse of the country" (p. 146, folio 137), "very prevalent everywhere" by Burton's evidence (p. 147, folio 138).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the time of the British occupation" compared with "the Moghuls and the Kalh5ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora]", "from 1740 onwards"; author's argument): "the system prevailing at the time of the British occupation of Sind did not differ greatly from the system of the Moghuls"; the state share was taken in kind and "taxation as a whole was extraordinarily comprehensive" (pp. 153–154, folios 144–145); "Sind was then approaching a kind of natural and self-contained economy which argued a great .increase in administrative efficiency from 1740 onwar.ds [text-layer?] [onwards]" (p. 155, folio 146); local cesses "were not abolished" under the Kalhora and "under the Talpiirs the collection was even tightened up" (p. 156, folio 147); tax-farming was "One of the great curses of Moghul, Kalh6ro and Talpiir rule alike", the farmers "mostly Hindus" (p. 157, folio 148); a sar shumari poll tax fell on "nc;m-Baluchi [text-layer?] [non-Baluchi] and Jat Muslims" (p. 155, folio 146); this taxation, "backed by the Baliich [text-layer?] [Baluch] soldiery, made the Government far from popular with the common man" and enriched the ruling house (p. 159, folio 150).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seven­teenth and eighteenth century Sind", "Moghul and Kalhoro days", the Talpurs, and Burnes (1828 visit); author's argument and quoted sources, James and Burnes): "Sind was a Muslim state and in theory justice was administered according to the tenets of the Koran"; "In Moghul and Kalhorn days every man was to some extent the defender of his own rights"; the police "lamentably insufficient" (p. 159, folio 150); collective village responsibility "might very well apply word for word to the Sind of Moghul and Kalh6ro days"; "Sind contained then many expert thieves, and ... equally expert trackers"; crime "comparatively speaking, so little rife in Sind", save cattle stealing and "the wildness of the Baliichi tdbes [text-layer?] [tribes]" (pp. 160–161, folios 151–152); trial by ordeal in "a semi-barbarous land"; "Un�er [text-layer?] [Under] the �alpiirs [text-layer?] [Talpiirs] there were no regular civil courts" (p. 161, folio 152); Burnes: "As the state religion of Scinde is taken from the E:oran [text-layer?] [Koran], so the system of jurisprudence is derived from the same source"; Hindus settle disputes "by panchayats"; "The Baliichis seem generally to take the law into their own hands" (p. 162, folio 153).
- (book's word: "Sind", "eighteenth century Sind"; refers to: Moghul and Kalhora times, 1847, and the author's day; author's argument and quoted source, the Commissioner in Sind): the Kalhora's deference to the priestly class "led to the extraordinary reverence for pirs, Sayids and holy men, which may be said to have been the predominant characteristic of eighteenth century Sind"; "The system of land tenure in Sind, so far as is known, has always been feudal" (p. 163, folio 154); jaghir and inam land was put in 1847 "at one-tenth of the total area of the Government land"; jaghirs "were held mostly by Baliich chiefs", puttadaris "in Upper Sind especially ... mostly by Afghan settlers dating from the time of Nadir Shah, and even earlier" (p. 164, folio 155); of the jaghirdar tribes "The vast majority of these are Baluchi tribes"; the zamindar's representative "was called Mukhadam or Arbab, a title that has not yet died out in Sind" (p. 165, folio 156).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Moghul and later still truer of Kalh6ro and Talpiir days", with Burnes, Crowe, MacMurdo and Pottinger; author's argument and quoted sources): "The extent to which holy men ... were reverenced and pampered in Sind is 'almost outside the bounds of credibility"; a Persian proverb about Multan "applied -equally to Sind that it consisted of heat, dust, beggars and tombs"; "The social religion of Sind was built up round the reverence of the murid for the murshid" (p. 166, folio 157); Dr Burnes: "There is no country in Asia or rather on earth that is so perfectly priestridden.
