- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: Moghul, Kalhora and Talpur rule, with Pottinger's nineteenth-century division; author's argument and quoted source, Pottinger): Pottinger's three classes: the privileged ("Amirs, Chiefs, Baliichis, Sayids and religious mendicants"), "the pastoral agricultural and other Mussulman tribes ... constituting the majority of the population", and the Hindus "tyrannized over in . the most infamous and open manner" (p. 170, folio 161); under the Kalhora "the true Sindhis, the Jats and camelmen, the fishermen and hunters, were fully e_xploited [text-layer?] [exploited]"; "The Hindus' position deteriorated still further", yet they "continued to live in Sind advancing money to the zamind.ar class, financing trade and commerce, farming the revenue, and owning all the petty shops in the villages"; "A quasi-theocratic state" could have taken no other form (p. 171, folio 162).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sindhi"; refers to: "seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind", "the mid-eighteenth century", and "the middle age of Islam"; author's argument): "In seventeenth and eighteenth century Sind a great gulf yawned between Islam's ideal and the practical working of Islamic . belief"; "Sind was a Muslim state which struggled to semi-independence in the mid-eighteenth century ... amongst a population composed for the most part of converted Jats ... - (book's word: "Sind", "Upper and Lower Sind"; refers to: "the eighth century" to "about A.D.1461"; book's historical prose, citing Ray, Elliot and the Ain): "The indigenous population of the Lower Indus valley in the eighth century con­sisted of Hindu races speaking a Sanskritic language"; "Towards the end of the tenth century Islam in Sind appears to have been influenced by a wave of Qarma­tian heresy" (Ray, quoted); "Most of the population was converted to Islam in the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries"; the Sumra regime ran "from circa ro25 [text-layer?] [1025] to circa 1360", with confusion "as to the authority wielded over Upper and Lower Sind, which have had very different histories" (p. 174, folio 165); under Jam Nizamuddin "about A.D.1461, Islam spread widely over Si.nd [text-layer?] [Sind] an d [text-layer?] [and] the mass of the common people became Muslim" (p. 175, folio 166).

"The Ain-i-Akbari states that the Siimro [text-layer?] [Sumro] Rajpiit [text-layer?] [Rajput] line of thirty-six princes reigned for five hundred years. The Tarikh-i-Tahiri says that from the year of the Hijra 7 00 [text-layer?] [700] until 843 the Hindu tribe of Siimra [text-layer?] [Sumra] were the rulers of Sind"; a "Siimro [text-layer?] [Sumro] king" Chanesar "reigned in Lower Sind for about 18 years, circa A.D. 1228" (p. 270, folio 261). Glossary: "Siimro" [text-layer?] [Sumro] "Sindhi tribe. At one time rulers of Sind" (p. 431, folio 422).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" for their political rise, "1613" for Withington, and Sorley's present for the district statement; book's historical prose and author's present): "The Baliichis [text-layer?] [Baluchis] have been in Sind for many generations. Their political importance, however, dates from the time when they were called in by the Kalh6ra [text-layer?] [Kalhora] in the eighteenth century to stiffen the military forces at the disposal of the Prince of Sind. Long before that, however, tribes of Baliichis [text-layer?] [Baluchis] were settled in various parts of the country"; in 1613 "the Baliichis [text-layer?] in the lower part of the country at that time had no good reputation for law-abiding habits"; "Upper Sind, however, rather than Lower Sind was the chief region to attract them, and it is chiefly in what are now the Upper Sind Frontier, the Larkana and the Sukkur districts that most of the Baliichi [text-layer?] [Baluchi] population was found. The Upper Sind Frontier district is still a kind of Baliichi [text-layer?] preserve" (p. 255, folio 246); "the modem [text-layer?] [modern] Baliichi [text-layer?] in Sind prefers the open fields to a house in a street" (p. 256, folio 247); "Baliichis and Sindhis [text-layer?] [Baluchis and Sindhis] have been living together long enough now" for poetry to share a starting ground; "The Doms visited the villages of Sind wherever there were Baliichis [text-layer?] to listen to them" (pp. 257–258, folios 248–249).
- (book's word: "' Sindh '" in Baluch poetry, "the Sindh country"; refers to: period not stated; quoted source, Longworth Dames, *Popular Poetry of the Baloches*, with the author's gloss): the book itself marks this "Sindh" as differently bounded: "There are many references to ' Sindh ' in Balii.ch [text-layer?] [Baluch] poetry. It is true that Sind so used means not the modem [text-layer?] [modern] political division thus named but the valley of the Indus", though some localities named are "actually Sindhi places, jn [text-layer?] [in] the country that lies in the Indus valley south of Multan and reaches far down the river on its way to the sea" (p. 256, folio 247); the Jatois and Mazaris are "tribes strongly represented in Sind" (printed "Jat6is" [text-layer?] [Jatois]); a Baluchi bard: "' Drunkards … are the young men of the Sindh country : there is much water and bhang is cheap an� [text-layer?] [and] wood is plentiful near their houses '" (printed "an�" [text-layer?] [and]) (pp. 256–257, folios 247–248). Weigh: this "Sindh" is the Indus valley, not the province.
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries", and Sorley's present ("still"); author's argument): "The historical portion of this work has given a picture of the rural condition of Sind in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. It was a land of cultivators and herdsmen, keepers of camels, buffaloes, sheep, goats and oxen, of tombs and Sayids, of dust and heat, of the annual rise and fall of the waters of the Indus but inefficiently controlled by a crude system of irrigation" (p. 265, folio 256); "few countries are as rich as Sind is in bird life"; "the crow which in Sind is a harbinger of good tidings" (p. 266, folio 257); "No one familiar with Sind will dispute how true a portrayal this is of a simple rustic civilization of husbandmen and cattle herds, and how typical it all is still of the predominant features of this agricultural countryside" (pp. 267–268, folios 258–259); "the poem�·a:re [text-layer?] [poems are] loved by a�l [text-layer?] [all] classes in Sind" (printed "poem�·a:re" and "a�l" [text-layer?]) (p. 268, folio 259).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: "the Sind of pre-Islam days" for the tales, Sorley's present for the last clause; author's argument): the Risalo's stories "are all Hindu in origin. It is plain that most of them relate to the Sind of pre-Islam days" (p. 269, folio 260); Hindu races immigrating "from the Punjab" form "an important element in the population" (see punjab above) (p. 270, folio 261); the stories other than the Punjab and Cutch ones "are stoiies [text-layer?] [stories] of Lower Sind ctirrent [text-layer?] [current] [printed "Sind'ctirrent" [text-layer?]] amongst the Hindu peoples living �m [text-layer?] [on] the borders of the Thar prior to the general conversion of these people to Islam", so that "the present-day Hindu peoples of Sind feel that their cu,lture [text-layer?] [culture] [printed "cu,lture" [text-layer?]] no less than the culture of Islam has gone to the fashioning qf [text-layer?] [of] the final product" (p. 271, folio 262).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Simi" [text-layer?] [Sind]; refers to: period not stated, with Burton's "under the native governments" and "formerly"; author's argument, quoted sources Tarikh-i-Tahiri, Elliot, Burton (*History of Sindh*)): "THE practice of Sufism by the common people in Sind was a very different thing from the refined system of the poets and theologians"; "It is not possible to trace in any detail the progress of tasawwuf in Sind"; "There is no reliable account of the progress of the movement in Simi [text-layer?] [Sind]" (p. 298, folio 289); Elliot on the Tuhfat-al-Kiram's saints, "so numerous that there is scarcely a village in that priest-ridden country which has not its tombs of holy men" (p. 298, folio 289); Burton: "' Tasuwwuf … under the native governments was as formidable a political engine as most of the secret confraternities recorded in history … Even among the mild Sindhis a noted Pir (religious s�perior [text-layer?] [superior]) formerly might safely order one of his Murids or disciples to murder an enemy. Yet the native princes encouraged it partly from superstition and partly because the price of every Pir was well known to them '"; "The Pirs held positions of great personal authority. The Sufi fakirs of Sind belonged to the two great orders of Jelali and Jemali darwishes" (p. 299, folio 290); "The Sind Jemali fakirs belonged to foµr [text-layer?] [four] [printed "foµr" [text-layer?]] orders, the Kadiri, the Nakshbandi, the Suhrawardi and the Chishti"; the Kalandars and "so-called Sufi beggars who wandered far and wide over Sind taking their toll of the superstitious country people" (p. 300, folio 291).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated ("always"; present practice); author's argument, quoted source Longworth Dames): "In Sind under the name of Khwajo Khizr this saint has always exercised a great power and has in popular superstition become somehow identified with the spirit of the waters and … with the deity of the river Indus"; "The Daryapanthis of Sind are believers in the sanctity of the River God or the God of Waters, and the belief affects Hindu and Muslim alike" (p. 301, folio 292); the belief that saints sleep in their tombs "is the solid foundation of the extraordinary devotion paid in Sind to the tombs of the deceased saints, pirs and holy men", bound up with the pir–murid relation (p. 302, folio 293).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "From the thirteenth century onwards", and "from the eleventh century onwards" for the rulers; author's argument): "From the thirteenth century onwards there was a great immigration into Sind of learned and religious-minded men from the centre of the Muslim world"; "The great ascendancy of the Sayids in Sind doubtless dates from about this time"; earlier "there had been signs of unorthodoxy amongst the governing oligarchy in Sind … more than a trace of Qarmatian doctrine", affecting "some of the native Sindhi rulers who succeeded the immigrant Muslims as rulers from the eleventh century onwards"; "Most of the Sayids were of Shia persuasion and it is possible that Sind was for a time a congenial home for these immigrants. That there has been any consistent progress of Sufism in Sind I do not believe" (p. 302, folio 293); "Siifism [text-layer?] [Sufism] in Sind has been handed down by a handful of learned men from age to age" (p. 303, folio 294). Also: between "the thirteenth century when Lal Shahbaz flourished and the eighteenth century … there is very little that can be said to be securely established in the way of verified historical fact" about Sind's Sufi poets (p. 299, folio 290).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; glossary): "Si:Iiwan" [text-layer?] [Sawan] "Rainy season in Sind—July and August" (p. 430, folio 421).

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_pain_and_grace.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's whole second part. The running form is **Shah Abdul Latif**; the book also uses "Shah Abdul
Latif of Bhit" (p. 24, folio 20), "Shah Latif", "Shah", "Latif", "Shah Sahib" (p. 153, folio 153),
"the mystic of Bhit" (p. 195, folio 195), and, in Burton's quoted words, "Shah Bhitai" (p. 152,
folio 152). The index prints him as `"Abdul Latif Bhita?i[OCR?], Shah` (p. 300, folio not stated) —
**and gives him no headword of his own in the main alphabetical run**, only the parenthetical
attribution on some twenty-eight `Sur` headwords and the place entry **"Bhit Shah 25, 151, 195"**
(p. 301, folio 301). The index's own map of the treatment is `"Abdul Latif Bhita?i[OCR?], Shah —X, 20,
21, 22, 24, 25-27, 138n, 151-262, 263-69"` (p. 300, folio not stated) — **treat those numerals as
OCR-suspect and do not reproduce the run as clean**.

- **Saint**
  - **Dates — given three times and never varied: "(1689-1752)".** On the part-title leaf,
    `SHAH ABDUL LATIF OF BHIT / (1689-1752)` (p. 150, folio not stated); in the Foreword, "the greatest
    mystical poet of the Lower Indus Valley, Shah Abdul Latif (1689-1752)" (p. 2, folio xii); and in
    the body, "It is Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit (1689-1752) who died ten years before Shah Waliullah left
    this valley of tears" (p. 24, folio 20). **The book states them flatly, with no "not established
    with complete certainty" qualification.** **No day, no month and no hijri date of birth or death
    are given anywhere** — **(not stated)**. The chapter text itself (pp. 151–262) never repeats a
    birth or death year.
  - **A second, independent date for the death: a chronogram on the tomb, and the book does not
    convert it.** "One of the Persian chronograms composed at his death and inscribed at the northern
    side of the dome says: / When he became intoxicated by union from the goblet of 'Come back'
    (Sura 89/17). / The hidden inspirer said: 'The year of his journey was Ridwan-i Haqq,' (God's
    satisfaction) = 1165" (pp. 29–30, folios 25–26), with "Other chronogramms in Qani', Lc."[OCR?]
    [l.c.] "p. 428 f." (p. 30, folio 26, footnote 14). **"1165" is given as the chronogram's value and
    is nowhere equated to 1752 by the book** — see Doubts.
  - **Family and descent — father named, mother's line explicitly uncertain.** "Shah Abdul Latif was
    born into a family of mystics who lived near Hala; his father, Shah Habib, was a descendant of one
    of the first noted poets in his mother tongue, Shah Abdul Karim of Bulrri"[OCR?] [Bulri] (p. 29,
    folio 25). **The father is named only "Shah Habib"; no death date, burial place or shrine for him**
    — **(not stated)**. On the mother: "about his mother's family the traditions are not unanimous.
    Yet, it is said that he (like Mir Dard) was a sayyid, his lineage going back to the seventh
    zmam Misa al-Kazim"[OCR?] [imam Musa al-Kazim] (p. 29, folio 25) — **"it is said that" is the
    book's own hedge**. Elsewhere: "This man from a sayyid and Sufi family" (p. 151, folio not stated)
    and "Both mystical writers come from Sayyid families; hence, they are particularly fond of their
    ancestors, the Prophet and the caliph Ali" (p. 268, folio 268). **"sayyid" is the only affiliation
    term used of him in the entire book.**
  - **Silsila — THE LOAD-BEARING NEGATIVE, and the single most important page in this book for this
    row.** "It is said that Shah Latif was an wwazsi[OCR?] [uwaisi] mystic, e.g., that he had no formal
    spiritual master, and hence did not belong to any of the acknowledged chains of initiation"
    (p. 191, folio 191). **Schimmel reports this as tradition ("It is said"), not as her own finding,
    and immediately qualifies it**: "Yet, he quotes with approval an Arabic saying about the necessity
    of the mystical guide: 'Who walks on the Path without shaikh?' (Dési[OCR?] [Desi] II 3) and
    continues his instructions to Sassui with the famous Sufi saying: 'Who has no shaikh, his shaikh is
    Satan' (id. II 4)" (p. 191, folio 191). **The row's silsila cell is empty; this page is direct
    evidence for why, and should be cited rather than filled.** No pir, no bayʿa and no order are given
    for him anywhere in the book — **(not stated)**.
  - **Sectarian position, reported and not attested.** "he is related to have called himself 'something
    between Sunni and Shii Islam;' that means he continued in the tradition of the
    tash-ayyu' hasan"[OCR?] [tashayyu' hasan] ", the loving attitude towards Muhammad's family"
    (pp. 268–269, folios 268–269). **The technical term is the book's; the poet is not quoted
    directly.**
  - **Three years with wandering Yogis, twice told and hedged the second time.** "Shah Abdul Latif
    spent three years with a group of yogis, wandering through the country, up to the sacred mountain
    Hinglaj in Balochistan" (p. 29, folio 25), footnoted to "M. Jotwani has given as many biographical
    details as are available in his book, 'Shah Abdul Latif', New Delhi 1975" (p. 29, folio 25,
    footnote 13). And later: "Shah Abdul Latif had spent some time—probably three years—in the company
    of wandering Yogis, and it is said that he visited with them many of the places mentioned in the
    Risalo. That may or may not be true—but the deep impression which the Yogis left in his heart
    cannot be denied" (p. 219, folio 219). **"probably three years" is Schimmel's own estimate; the
    itinerary claim is reported as hearsay and left undecided. No dates, no companions** — **(not
    stated)**.
  - **Settlement at Bhit, death, childlessness — all in one sentence.** "Later Latif settled in Bhit,
    where he died without issue in 1752, leaving behind him the collection of thirty Sindhi poems that
    has become known as the Risal6"[OCR?] [Risalo] (p. 29, folio 25). **No foundation of the village
    is described and no year is given for the settlement** — **(not stated)**. **"died without issue"
    is the book's phrase and is the only statement in it bearing on a hereditary succession at this
    shrine.**
  - **The illiteracy claim, named to its source and rejected.** "Mir Ali Shir Qani' claims in his two
    major historical works that the mystical poet was ummi, 'illiterate;' but that is impossible to
    accept when one enters into the complicated web of thoughts displayed in the poems, and decorated
    with Arabic quotations, or allusion to Persian mystical poetry" (p. 29, folio 25). **Schimmel's
    rejection is her own, argued from the text of the poems.** The same claim recurs as legend: "Many
    legends tell that a saint was illiterate and knew only the alif: that is said about the Turkish
    folk poet Yunus Emre, the Panjabi poet Bullhe Shah, and our Abdul Latif as well" (p. 208,
    folio 208).
  - **Musicianship, with an instrument invented.** "Shah Latif was an excellent musician, who even
    invented a new type of tambura (drone instrument), and whose blending of traditional Indian raga
    and Sindhi folk tunes enhances the beauty of his verses which ought to be sung, not analyzed, to
    enjoy their full charm" (p. 29, folio 25). Also: his music, "though indebted to the classical
    Indian tradition, utilizes the folk songs of his home province" (p. 263, folio not stated).
  - **A quarrel with a Kalhoro prince, and its repair — the only event of his life the book gives
    besides the yogi years.** Rumi's Mathnawi "was loved and recited at the Mughal court in Delhi, and
    served the Kalhoro prince to gain back the sympathies of Shah Abdul Latif" (p. 165, folio 165).
    **Schimmel does not name the prince here, give a year, or say what the estrangement was** — all
    **(not stated)**.
  - **A relation to Shah Inayat of Jhok, given as a probability.** "It is possible that Shah Abdul
    Latif alluded to his fate in some of his verses; in any case he has quoted a Persian line usually
    connected with Shah Inayat" (p. 26, folio 22); the line is quoted at "he quotes in Persian a line
    that is usually connected with the fate of Shah Inayat of Jhok (exec. 1718): / The head is
    sacrificed at the Friend's foot, / which is in order (Sohn. II)" (p. 228, folio 228).
  - **Relations to two other archive figures, both stated as relative chronology only** — Girhori is
    "Shah Latif's younger contemporary" (p. 201, folio 201) and "Shah's younger contemporary" (p. 261,
    folio 261), and "A verse by him expresses also his admiration for Shah Abdul Latif, whom he may
    have met when still a child" (p. 28, folio 24); Sachal Sarmast is "his younger contemporary"
    (p. 184, folio 184) and "met him when still a boy" (p. 30, folio 26); Muhammad Zaman Lanwari is
    "his younger compatriot" (p. 208, folio 208).
  - **His unnamed friend and servant.** "A little joke about Shah Latif's friend and servant forms the
    last chapter of this Sur; he, though 'stinking', 'a glutton', 'ugly,'—to mention only a few of his
    epithets—becomes purified in the rose-water-like company of the master" (p. 160, folio 160).
    **The servant is not named** — **(not stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The burial, located three ways in one sentence.** "Shah Abdul Latif is buried in a beautiful
    mausoleum in Bhit Shah near Hala, north east of Hyderabad/Sind" (p. 151, folio not stated).
    **This is the book's only statement of where he lies.**
  - **The building, its patron and its date.** "His shrine, crowned by a graceful minaret, was erected
    by the Kalhoro prince Ghulam Shah Kalhoro in 1765" (p. 151, folio not stated). **1765 is the only
    building year in the book, and it is stated flatly, with no "according to tradition" and no
    source.** **No cost, endowment or grant accompanies it** — **(not stated)**.
  - **The fabric, described from a visit.** "it is covered with blue and white tiles as are typical of
    Hala district; they show flower motifs, and the pillars at the corners seem to grow out of large
    tulips" (p. 151, folio not stated). "The ceiling of the entrance hall shows the traditional
    workmanship of this area: between the wooden framework small brownish-olive-yellowish rectangular
    tiles are set in, a style found in many saints' tombs in Sind" (p. 151, folio not stated). **No
    craftsman is named** — **(not stated)**. The author's verdict: "Although not outstanding as a work
    of architecture the building and the adjacent graveyard impress the visitor by their peculiar
    charm" (p. 151, folio not stated).
  - **An "adjacent graveyard" is named and not described, and nobody buried in it is named** — **(not
    stated)** (p. 151, folio not stated).
  - **A dome carrying Persian chronograms on its northern side** (pp. 29–30, folios 25–26, quoted
    above). **This and the minaret are the only two elements of the fabric the book dates or locates.**
  - **A generic Sindhi saint's tomb, described but not named or located** — "the blue and white tiles
    which cover a lonely saint's tomb match the purity of the blue sky" (p. 263, folio not stated).
    Recorded here because the tile description matches the one given for Bhit Shah.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The ʿurs: a month, a crowd that is not counted, and a building put up for it.** "To accomodate
    at least part of the innumerable visitors who flock to Bhit Shah in the days of the "urs[OCR?]
    [ʿurs], when the memory of the mystic's death is celebrated in the month of Safar, a guest house
    has recently been built. A cultural center is being developed" (p. 151, folio not stated).
    **"the month of Safar" is the only calendar anchor; no day, no duration and no visitor figure are
    given — the crowd is only "innumerable"** — **(not stated)**. **The ʿurs runs over "days", not a
    day.** **Who built the guest house and who is developing the cultural centre are both in the
    passive and are (not stated); "recently" and "is being developed" date them only against the
    book's own present (1976).**
  - **The weekly observance: day, hour, performers, place.** "Every Thursday evening, till late at
    night, the few dervishes who still continue the tradition of Shah Abdul Latif sing his poetry
    inside the compound of the mausoleum" (p. 151, folio not stated). **Note "the few dervishes who
    still continue the tradition": the book records the practice as surviving and diminished in the
    same breath.** Their number, names, standing and support are all **(not stated)**. **This is the
    book's one statement of living, routine practice at any archive shrine.**
  - **Recitation practice a century and a quarter earlier, from Burton.** "The learned praise it for
    its beauty, and are fond of hearing it recited to the sound of the guitar. Even the unlearned
    generally know select portions by heart, and take the trouble to become acquainted with their
    meaning" (p. 152, folio 152, quoting "R. Burton, 'Sind and the races ...,' p. 203"). **"the sound
    of the guitar" is Burton's word as printed; the Sindhi instrument is not named** — **(not
    stated)**.
  - **The Risalo as sung, with a performance rule attached to one Sur.** "for the Rrsalo"[OCR?]
    [Risalo] "is meant to be sung, not to be theologically and grammatically dissected" (p. 162,
    folio 162); "Sur Yaman Kalyan is a derivation from Kalyan, and should be sung in the evening; it
    is meant to bring peace to the soul" (p. 195, folio 195). Each Sur "is named according to the
    musical mode in which it is sung, and which points to the contents" (p. 154, folio 154).
  - **Instruments named in his verse** — "all veins have become a rabab (Kal. II 6, Ripa I 21)"
    (p. 166, folio 166); "the fiddles of the Yogis, those 'golden instruments'" in "Ramakali (1 19ff)"
    and "the murli, the snake charmer's instrument" (p. 166, folio 166); "Those, whose body is a
    rosary, the soul a bead of the rosary, the heart a tanboura" (p. 194, folio 194).
  - **Transmission by labour rather than by assembly — the book's own account of how the poetry
    circulated.** "They could sing Shah Abdul Latif's poetry when ploughing or going out for fishing,
    picking cotton or spinning it, and thus the central concepts of Islamic mysticism—Love of God and
    the Prophet, and trust in the eternal divine wisdom—became part and parcel of their daily lives"
    (p. 269, folio 269). **Four named occupations and a mechanism that is neither shrine nor book.**
  - **EXPLICIT NEGATIVES for this row, stated across the whole book: no mela, no langar, no offering
    or nazrana, no sajjada nashin or gaddi nashin, no custodian or caretaker family, no Auqaf or any
    state department, no waqf, inam, jagir or land grant, no revenue, no visitor figure, and no
    dispute over custody or succession at Bhit Shah** — all **(not stated)**. The only ʿurs fact is
    the month; the only building fact is 1765 and the tiles; the only recurring rite is the Thursday
    singing.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The existence of a legendary corpus is asserted and not one legend is given.** "Contrary to Mir
    Dard, Shah Latif did not leave any notes about his life or the development of his mystical
    theories; besides a few dry facts we know about him merely some legends of more or less authentic
    character. But his poetry is enough to make us acquainted with his personality" (p. 151, folio not
    stated). **No karama of Shah Abdul Latif is told anywhere in this book** — **(not stated)**.
  - The one legend attached to him by name is the illiterate-saint legend, shared with Yunus Emre and
    Bullhe Shah (p. 208, folio 208, quoted above), which Schimmel reports and does not endorse.
- **Significance**
  - **The book's superlatives, three of them.** "the greatest mystical poet of the Lower Indus Valley"
    (p. 2, folio xii); "the greatest mystical poet in the Sindhi language" (p. 24, folio 20); "Shah
    Abdul Latif's poetry is the first apex of a long literary development, the beginnings of which we
    cannot properly distinguish" (p. 161, folio 161).
  - **The reception history, and why this row must be handled carefully — the most citable statement
    in the book about the political appropriation of a shrine saint.** A leading Pakistani scholar,
    quoted at length: "Shah was the crown of the Sufis, Shah was a folk poet, Shah was the master of
    ragas, Shah was a patriot, Shah was a congressman, Shah was a Muslim Leaguer, Shah was Rumi, Shah
    was Goethe, Shah has produced Hir, Shah has made alive Marui—in short, Shah is the medicine for
    every illness, Shah is the remedy for every pain. That means, the same thing that has been done to
    Ghalib and Iqbal we have done to Shah Sahib" (p. 153, folio 153, footnote 3 citing "HL Rashdi[OCR?]
    [H. … Rashdi], 'Shah kafia j6 mijid na ahe,'[OCR?] in: Mehran jin maupin[OCR?], p. 218.").
    Schimmel's own generalisation of it: "there is always the danger that great poetry will be
    interpreted by every reader according to his own feelings, and also according to certain
    opportunist or even political tendencies; and since contradictions cannot be excluded in the verse
    of a great poet, let alone a religious, or mystically minded, poet, everyone finds in him what he
    has sought" (p. 153, folio 153).
  - **The second load-bearing negative: the Risalo names no order and no sanctuary.** "None of the
    great tarigas[OCR?] [tariqas] is mentioned, nor any of the sanctuaries to which the inhabitants of
    Sind flocked at many occasions" (p. 190, folio not stated). **The subordinate clause is itself
    evidence that flocking to sanctuaries was normal in the poet's Sind: the author asserts the
    practice while denying its presence in the poems.**
  - **The third: he founded no order and preached no community.** "He certainly did not call his
    listeners to rebellion against the outward forces, or to the formation of a closely knit mystical
    community. His mysticism is individualistic, and has as its center only one story: that of the
    return of the loving soul to her Lord" (p. 187, folio 187). **Taken with the uwaisi statement
    (p. 191) and the no-tariqas statement (p. 190), these three passages make one consistent argument:
    the Risalo is not an institutional document.**
  - **The pir in the poetry, and the limit of it.** "All the way through the chapters of the
    Risdlé[OCR?] [Risalo] the importance of the mystical leader is implicitly understood… Only in a
    few places the mystical guide is mentioned overtly as a conditio sine qua non for the seeker, and
    in Sarirag he assumes the figure of the pilot who leads the boat safely through the ocean of this
    world so that its precious cargo cannot be plundered. However, as much as the necessity of the Pir
    is understood, Shah Latif never indulges in deliberations about his veneration or the duties of
    the initiate towards him: only in poetical allusions he introduces his listeners to the mysteries
    of the Path" (p. 187, folio 187). **Against this stands the Satguru passage — "the true mystical
    guide, the Satguru, as Shah Latif calls him, is he 'who has the dla Allah,'[OCR?] [illa Allah]"
    (p. 223, folio 223) — and Schimmel's own note of surprise that "Shah Latif, in this connection,
    does not mention the necessity of a mystical guide" (p. 214, folio 214). The book does not
    reconcile the three.**
  - **The book's answer to the syncretism question, given as its chapter's last sentence.** "We may sum
    up the Islamic contents of the Risalo, which, it scems to me, are undeniably strong, by saying
    that the God of the Rusald"[OCR?] [Risalo] " is a living and loving God; not a philosophical or
    mystical principle, but rather the Creator, the Merciful and Maj-estic, the First and the Last,
    and always the Lord as revealed through the Quran" (p. 262, folio 262). The chapter opens by
    naming the reading it opposes: "The way Shah Abdul Latif unites his descriptions of Sufis and
    Yogis by explaining the wondrous life of the Hindu ascetics with Quranic terminology has induced
    many of his Hindu admirers and interpreters to regard his poetry as a perfect blending of the two
    religious traditions of India" (p. 236, folio not stated).
  - **But the Hindu material is inside the canonical text, and the book says so plainly.** "How much
    Shah Latif has islamized his Yogis becomes clear from verse 32, where he praises the
    Shiva-followers 'whose leader is Ali.'" (p. 221, folio 221); "The application of Quranic words to
    the Hindu ascetics is quite surprising, and worth quoting in full" (p. 224, folio 224); "Shah
    Abdul Latif, as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who
    were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267);
    "Pre-Islamic ideas are natural in literature that draws upon Hindu tales; thus Sassui is sometimes
    called a banbhan (Abri I 10), a 'Brahmin girl,' or is praised because she is becoming a yogini
    (Abri III 10); the heroines also speak of the funeral pyre (thus Sam. I 8)" (p. 164, folio 164).
    **His Hindu and yogic sympathies are placed inside the chapter's argument about conversion:
    "Shah Abdul Latif's admiration for the true Yogis belongs to this group of phenomena, and fits
    very well into the religious situation in the country" (p. 29, folio 25), the "group of phenomena"
    being Hindus who "became devoted disciples of Muslim saints" (p. 28, folio 24).**
  - **His audience straddles both communities, in his own reported verse.** "You are a liar in
    infidelity—don't call yourself an infidel: / You are not at alla[OCR?] [at all a] Hindu, the
    brahmin's thread does not fit you, / The caste-mark is for those, who are true in their paganism
    (Asa IV 14, 15)" (p. 191, folio 191), glossed "whether Muslim or Hindu, monotheist or idol
    worshipper, man has to be sincere and not to make claims to sanctity which he cannot realize"
    (p. 191, folio 191).
  - **The typology that closes the book.** He "stands before us as the representative of that type of
    the rural mystical tradition in which his contemporaries in the Panjab and, to a certain extent,
    in the Pashto speaking areas were rooted" (p. 267, folio 267); the pairing with Dard is "the
    urban, sophisticated, and highly intelligent trend" against "the rural, simple and unassuming
    Sufism" (p. 269, folio 269). And a claim about what his verse does *not* register: "Shah Abdul
    Latif apparently had no problems of this kind" — i.e. no crisis of Muslim self-identification
    (p. 267, folio 267).
  - **Endurance, stated as a duration.** "his poetry would certainly not have attracted the Sindhi
    speaking people for more than two centuries, had he only dwelt upon highly mystical topics"
    (p. 168, folio 168); one verse, "Thou makest the leaf drown in the stream, and makest the stone
    swimming ("Dahar II 19")", has "become almost proverbial in Sindhi" and the lines "have consoled
    many pious souls in the afflictions of life and the years of poverty and misery that came over the
    country time and again, and which form the title of several contemporary short stories" (p. 241,
    folio 241).
- **The Risalo — text, structure and editions** (kept here because a shrine entry citing this row will
  have to cite the book)
  - **Thirty Surs, in the Bombay edition, and a different count elsewhere.** "The titles of the thirty
    Surs (chapters) in `"Shah jo Risald"[OCR?]` [Shah jo Risalo], ed. Kalyan Adwani, Bombay, 1957, are
    abbreviated from the list given on pp. 154 ff." (p. 4, folio not stated); "Shah Abdul Latif's
    Risa/lo"[OCR?] [Risalo] "consists (in the excellent Bombay edition) of thirty chapters, Sur, which
    deal with different topics" (p. 154, folio 154). Against it: "the Hyderabad edition, 1974" has a
    different and longer sequence of which "Some of the chapters are not found in the other editions"
    (p. 154, folio 154, footnote 5). **The two editions also place the same verse in two different
    places** — the Kanz–Quduri–Kafiya verse is added by "the Trumpp edition" at one point while "The
    Hyderabad edition has this verse, with a long line of similar verses, at the end of Yaman Kalyan,
    v. 41-44" (p. 202, folio 202, footnote 13).
  - **The internal form of a Sur.** "Each Sur has a number of chapters, consisting of irregular numbers
    of verses in Indian meters; each chapter closes with a wd 'i"[OCR?] [waʾi]", that is, a longer
    poem with one main line which is repeated, in singing, after every line of the poem" (p. 154,
    folio 154). Schimmel's distributional claim about it: "It seems typical that most of these
    allusions are inserted into the final part of each chapter, e.g., the wa i, where Shah liked to
    express his truly Islamic feelings" (p. 238, folio 238); and her caution, "The final songs, in
    fact, do not always exactly sum up the contents of the chapter which they follow" (p. 256,
    folio 256).
  - **The term "Risalo" glossed.** "That was the technical term for collections of poetry which are
    arranged according to the musical modes" (p. 29, folio 25); the precursor is named — Shah ʿInat's
    collection was "the first to be arranged in 19 Surs, and was therefore called a Risalo" (p. 162,
    folio 162).
  - **The folk-tale base, dated.** "Many Surs rely upon well-known folk tales, as is the case in the
    Panjab, too. Some of these stories have a historical basis and can be dated back to the 14th and
    15th centuries; those belong to Shah's favorite topics" (p. 154, folio 154). His method: he
    "singles out some particularly impressive moments to develop his teachings about suffering and love
    so that even very worldly heroes and heroines are transformed completely into symbols of the
    Godsecking"[OCR?] [God-seeking] "soul" (p. 154, folio 154).
  - **The Sur sequence as Schimmel narrates it, in order, with modes and hours where given**
    (pp. 154–161, folios 154–161): `Kalyan`, "a purely mystical song" (pp. 154–155); `Sur Yaman
    Kalyan`, "musically a derivation from the first Sur, and like it belonging to the evening
    rdgs"[OCR?] [rags] (p. 155, folio 155); `Sur Khanbhat`, "a night melody" (p. 155, folio 155);
    `Sarirdg`[OCR?] [Sarirag] "(an afternoon rag)" and `Samundi` (p. 155, folio 155); `Sohni`
    (p. 155, folio 155); the five Sassui Surs — "(Abri, 'the weak one,' Ma‘ dhuri"[OCR?] [Maʾdhuri]",
    'the helpless one,' Dési, 'the native one,' Kéhyari"[OCR?] [Kohyari]", 'the mountaineer,' and
    Husaini, in the tragic melody of the dirges in Muharram)" (p. 156, folio 156);
    `Lila Chanésar`[OCR?] [Chanesar], `Mumal Rano`, `Sur Marui`, `Sur Kaméd`[OCR?] [Kamod]
    (pp. 157–158); "The very short Sur Ghatu" and `Sur Sorathi` (p. 158, folio 158);
    `Kédaro`[OCR?] [Kedaro], `Sur Sdrang`[OCR?] [Sarang]", the rain-song", `Sur Asaé`[OCR?] [Asa],
    `Sur Ripa`, `Sur Khéhori`[OCR?] [Khahori], `Sur Ramakali` (p. 159, folio 159); `Sur Barvo Sindhi`
    (pp. 159–160); `Kdpd ?it?`[OCR?] [Kapaʾiti], `Sur Purab`, `Sur Kara il`[OCR?] [Karaʾil],
    `Sur Pirbhati`, `Sur Dahar` and `Sur Bildwal`[OCR?] [Bilawal]" (1, II)" (p. 160, folio 160).
    **No verse locator anywhere in this book may be reproduced as clean — see Doubts.**
  - **The Surs Schimmel names as the most technically mystical**: "These Surs are Yaman Kalyan, which
    deals with the ideal Sufi, and Ramakali, where the Yogis are praised as the perfect models of the
    sanctified man, further Khahori, in which the theme of the Yogis is once more elaborated" (p. 195,
    folio 195). **A singular textual fact**: Sur Ramakali is "the important chapter which also (the
    only time in the `Risald!`[OCR?] [Risalo]) contains the expression `‘adam`, 'not-being'" (p. 195,
    folio 195).
  - **The Risalo as a designed whole.** "His Surs are connected with each other by many internal
    cross-relations so as to form, carefully read, a complete whole, as different as the topics of his
    poems look at first sight" (p. 268, folio 268). **Offered without manuscript evidence.**
  - **Four printed editions of the Risalo are listed in the bibliography, one dated by manuscript** —
    "based on Three Manuscripts written in 1269 and 1270 AH" (p. 294, folio 294).
  - **A publishing and translation economy around the saint, itemised.** Trumpp's Leipzig 1866 edition,
    "the first print of the famous collection of poetry", from a manuscript of "more than 1200 pages"
    (p. 152, folio 152); Watanmal 1889 (p. 152, folio 152); Sorley 1940 (p. 153, folio 153); Elsa
    Qazi's partial verse translation (p. 153, folio 153); and beneath them "an almost limitless amount
    of speeches, articles, booklets, and devotional literature was published, mainly in Sindhi"
    (p. 153, folio 153). **Research continues across the 1947 division**: "Even after partition, both
    the Sindhi Hindus in India and the Sindhi Muslims in Pakistan continued their work, which resulted
    in a number of editions and analyses" (p. 152, folio 152) — **"partition" is used without a
    year** — **(not stated)**.
  - **Schimmel's verdict on the existing scholarship.** Of Sorley: "Sorley, however, was not a trained
    [slamicist[OCR?] [Islamicist]; some of his usually correct statements must be verified and
    deepened in the light of history of religions" (p. 153, folio 153). Of the language: "the style of
    the Risalé[OCR?] [Risālo] is extremely difficult and offers problems even to those whose mother
    tongue is the complicated and melodious Sindhi" (p. 152, folio 152), with her caveat that her
    informants "sometimes differed considerably" and that "other translators may as well reach
    different conclusions" (p. 152, folio 152, footnote 2).
  - **A whole appendix of the Risalo in German verse**, pp. 277–290, arranged by Sur with locators
    (p. 277–290, folios 277–290); and inside it the poet names Rumi six times in one passage, each
    time with a different tag (p. 279, folio 279).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`bhit-bhit-shah` is mapped on the figure, not on the site, through most of Part II.** Chunks
  covering pp. 173–235 and 236–254 contain **no mention of Bhit, of his tomb, of his ʿurs or of any
  observance at his shrine in the body text** — the village name survives there only in the verso
  running header, which is apparatus. **A consolidator must not read those bullets as shrine
  evidence.** The shrine evidence is p. 151 and pp. 29–30, and nothing else.

- **The Bhit Shah building year, 1765, should be collated before it enters an entry.** The book
  states it flatly and without a source (p. 151, folio not stated), and on p. 153 it names and relies
  on Sorley without comparing tomb dates. **Check against the other sources already in this queue.**

| row | the book prints | the archive row |
|---|---|---|
| `bhit-bhit-shah` | "(1689-1752)" (p. 2, folio xii; p. 24, folio 20; p. 150, folio not stated) | 1752 — **agrees** |

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind", "this province"; refers to: "711" onward, as printed; book's historical prose): "the Arabs conquered Sind, the southern part of present day Pakistan, in 711, and this province never ceased to be a center of Islamic studies and a seat of Islamic mysticism" (p. 6, folio 2).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated ("has always been praised"); author's argument, then quoted source, "A Sindhi author", footnoted to J. Parsram, 'Sind and its Sufis', p. 78 f.): "Sind has always been praised as the country with a particularly strong inclination towards mysticism"; the quoted author: "Sind has been flattened and has been in a state of negation. This is the cause of the many disadvantages from which it suffers; but it has been also the cause of a great blessing ... something of which India is in need" — "namely, of the mystical tradition" (pp. 24–25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Indus Valley"; refers to: "From the day that Lal Shahbaz Qalandar settled in Sehwan in the 13th century" onward; book's historical prose): from then and from Baha'uddin Zakariya's Suhrawardi center "at the northernmost border of the country, in Multan, Sind had produced an immense number of mystics, whose shrines are scattered all through the Indus Valley, and who invented short songs in their mother tongue to express the mysteries of love and union" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Ahmad Shah Abdali's invasion, which took place during Shah Abdul Latif's life time"; quoted source, Divan Gidumal, "the minister of the Kalhoro princes", footnoted "Id. p. 54" [Parsram]): Gidumal offered the invader two bags which "contain the most valuable of Sind's gifts; they contain the holy dust from the tombs of numerous saints and Pirs of Sind" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Lower Indus Valley", "Upper Sind"; refers to: "since 1595", then "1717" to "January 1718" — as printed; book's historical prose): "Sind was, since 1595, part of the Mughal Empire, and although representatives of the Delhi court came there, the indigenous rulers enjoyed much freedom. The first obvious sign of the struggle for power in the Lower Indus Valley becomes visible in 1717"; "The Kalhora clan from Upper Sind, particularly active in the struggle against Inayat, soon afterwards became independent in Sind"; the army against Jhok "came from various places in Sind and Hind" (p. 25, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Upper Sind"; refers to: "1781" — as printed; book's historical prose): most of Abdur Rahim Girhori's books "were, however, destroyed during the Afghan raids in Upper Sind in 1781" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "18th century Sind", "Sind"; refers to: "18th century", the "14th and 15th centuries", and "still in the 1920's" — as printed; author's argument with quoted sources, H. T. Sorley and J. Parsram, 'l.c.' pp. 75, 84): it would be wrong to regard Girhori "as a typical exponent of Sindhi Sufism. On the whole, there was 'a common basis of understanding, by Muslim and Hindu alike, of the mystical message of Islam,' as H. T. Sorley says"; Sorley's analysis of "the complex religious situation in 18th century Sind" contains "the 'almost incredible veneration for sayyids as such and a great accretion of superstitious practices in the belief of the multitude'"; the understanding between the communities "may perhaps have been facilitated by the activities of the Ismaili missionaries who came to Sind in the 14th and 15th centuries"; "'neither Hindus nor Muslims are orthodox in Sind'" and "still in the 1920's, there were 'numerous Hindus and amongst them some of the best brains of Sind, old and new, who are Sufi by religion'"; "a number of Hindus became devoted disciples of Muslim saints" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Upper and later in Lower Sind"; refers to: the Kalhoro rise after "Aurangzeb's death", Nadir Shah "1739", Adam Shah Kalhoro executed "1651", the Talpurs "in 1781", the British "in 1843" — as printed; book's historical prose): "the native Kalhoro dynasty took over the rule in Upper and later in Lower Sind, only to become the vassals of the invading Nadir Shah, who first conquered Sind and the Panjab before he reached Delhi in 1739"; Abdali's influence "on the scene in Sind is not as important as it was in the northern areas of present day Pakistan"; the Kalhoros were overthrown by "the former disciples of their saintly ancestor, Adam Shah Kalhoro"; "The Talpurs, who thus came to power in 1781, ruled the country until it was conquered by the British in 1843"; "in Sind, too, the 18th century can be called the most important period for the formation of mystical poetry and prose" (pp. 30–31, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind" / "rural provinces"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and "later" Sindhi Sufi poets, period otherwise not stated; author's argument): "Many later Sindhi Sufi poets, mainly those of Baloch origin, used also the northern dialect of Sindhi, Siraiki", and the imagery of the linguistic areas is "very closely related, as is to be expected from the inhabitants of predominatly [OCR?] [predominantly] rural provinces" (p. 154, folio 154).
- (book's word: "both Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "Sohni is one of the favorite folk tales in both Sind and the Panjab" (p. 156, folio 156).
- (book's word: "14th century Sind", "Lower Sind"; refers to: the "14th century" and "the 15th century" — as printed; book's historical prose): the Lila Chanesar story "can be dated back to the time of Jam Chanesar, one of the Summa rulers in 14th century Sind"; the Mumal Rano tale "originated in Lower Sind some time in the 15th century" (p. 157, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; quoted source, E. Trumpp's introduction to Sur Sorathi): "how much even the strong monotheistic deism of Islam has become modified and corrupted in Sind, as in other parts of India, by its contact with Hinduism" (p. 158, folio 158).
- (book's word: "the poetry of Sind and the Panjab", "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): the spinning of cotton is "one of the most important occupations of women" and a frequent topic of the region's poetry; "the crow, kang, is the typical messenger bird in Sind, as was the pigeon in high Persian poetry" (p. 160, folio 160).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the country"; refers to: "had, and still has" for the folk poetry, and "shortly after 1530" for the first known Sindhi verses — as printed; author's argument): "Sind, as other countries, had, and still has, a big treasure of folk poetry, ballads, and working songs; folk tales are located in the different parts of the country, be it the hill slopes of Balochistan, the desert region of Thar Parkar, the fertile plains that were watered by the Indus, or the shifting creeks near the Indus mouth. It seems that in Sind, as in other parts of the Muslim world, the Sufis are responsible for the development of the spoken language into a literary one" (p. 161, folio 161).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "In Sind there were many pious who, by their recitation of the Mathnawi, 'kept the marketplace of union warm', and Rumi's work was quoted by stern Naqshbandis as much as by members of the more emotional orders" (p. 165, folio 165).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind countryside", "Sindhi shrines"; refers to: "the early 18th century" for Latif's picture, and "still" / "still in our day" for the beds and shrine trees; author's argument and ethnographic present): "The imagery of the swan must have been popular in Sind for a long time"; Latif "offers in his verse a vivid picture of the conditions of the Sind countryside during the early 18th century"; Lila's swinging bed — "these colorful, laquer-work beds are still a specialty of Sind"; the merchant's wife binds cloth on sacred trees (Sam. V 2): "still in our day the trees near Sindhi shrines are decorated with many pieces of cloth, preferably red, in the shape of little flags or tiny cradles, which are attached there for the sake of a vow, as it is usual almost everywhere in the Islamic world" (p. 168, folio 168).
- (book's word: "the western border of Sind"; refers to: present day of writing; author's anecdote): Sassui's run through the desert at sunset (Hus. I 8-11) is "a scene which can be appreciated by those who know the glowing evenings at the western border of Sind, when all of a sudden, darkness overtakes the wayfarer, and nothing is left but the sounds of a few animals" (p. 170, folio 170).
- (book's word: "the Sindhis"; refers to: period not stated, present tense; author's argument): the letter-writing topos "is general among the Sindhis, so that most of the girls who never learned how a pen looks or knew even a single letter always complain that they do not receive letters from their friends, or want to write toward their families. This topic occurs also often in wedding songs" (p. 172, folio 172).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "traditionally", period not stated; author's argument): "The Mohana traditionally belonged to the lowest social strata in Sind, and their women were noted for beauty and not too strict morals" (p. 175, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the inhabitants of Sind"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's *Risalo*, period not stated; author's argument): in the *Risalo* "None of the great tarigas [OCR?] [tariqas] is mentioned, nor any of the sanctuaries to which the inhabitants of Sind flocked at many occasions" (p. 190, folio 190).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the last indigenous Sindhi rulers"; refers to: "from 1520 onward" — as printed; book's historical prose): the Turkish word *otaq* [printed "6tdaq" [OCR?]] "points to the Turkish influence that was prevalent in Sind from 1520 onward, when the Turkish dynasty of the Arghun, descending from Afghanistan, overcame the last indigenous Sindhi rulers; they themselves were followed by the Turkish family of the Tarkhan under whom Sind fell to the Mughal Empire" (p. 207, folio 207).
- (book's word: "18th century Sind"; refers to: the 18th century, as printed; author's argument): an Arabic saying "which was very popular in 18th century Sind: al-bala' lil-wala? [OCR?] ka l-lahab lt? [OCR?] dh-dhahab, 'Affliction is for saintliness the same as are flames for the gold'" (p. 222, folio 222).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the poet's lament, period not stated; translation of the poet's verse, Mar. Mut. 30): "Marui ts [OCR?] [is] no more in Malir, / and there ts [OCR?] [is] no Hamir— / Punhun is no longer in Kech, / nor ts [OCR?] [is] in Sind an Amir; ... There ts [OCR?] [is] in the convents no Pir" (p. 235, folio 235).
- (book's word: "Eastern Sind", "dear Sind"; refers to: Sur Sarang, period not stated; author's argument and translation of the poet's verse): the cloud of mercy, "beginning from the desert area in Eastern Sind, the Thar Parkar", is seen floating "from Africa to China", and the poet, "coming closer and closer to his home region", prays: "My Lord, be kind over dear Sind, / Friend! Sweet darling! You may make the whole world happy!" (p. 259, folio 259).
- (book's word: "the villages in Sind", "native provinces"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, the evocative opening of "To Sum Up"): "the villages in Sind are covered with a veil of golden dust; the walls, built of unbaked clay, look like bronze, and the blue and white tiles which cover a lonely saint's tomb match the purity of the blue sky"; Dard's and Latif's approaches are "as different as are the sunsets in their native provinces" (p. 263, folio 263).
- (book's word: "his successors in Sind"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and after; author's argument): Latif, "as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267).

## From Waris Shah, Hir and Ranjha (Usborne tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/waris_shah_hir_ranjha.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the usage of "The Arab historians", period not stated; quoted source — the Arab historians and the Chach Nameh, as reported in an unattributed note — the book itself marks a differently bounded unit): "The Arab historians speak of Hind and Sind separately. Sind, In their description, is bounded by Multan, Jaisalmere and Gujrat and Hind, or Hindustan, begins where Sind ends. The Arabic title of the famous Chach Nameh one of the earliest histories of Sind, is The History of Hind and Sind (Tarikh-ul-Hind was [text-layer?] [reading uncertain] Sind)." (p. 55, folio 54, note 1). The note apparently glosses Ranjha's blessing on the Raja, "may you rule over horses, camels, elephants, batteries, Hindustan’ and Sind.’" (p. 54, folio 53); the marker match is not confirmed. This boundary is the book's (its Arab historians'), not the modern province's: weigh accordingly.
- (book's word: "old Sind", "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical/literary): "Sassi-Punnoo is a story of old Sind." — Sassi "belonged to Bhambore. an old city", Punnoo "was a Baluch chief"; the Sohni and Sassi romances, printed "SoJtni-Mahiwal [text-layer?] [Sohni-Mahiwal] and Sossi-Funoo [text-layer?] [Sassi-Punnoo]", have "travelled beyond their original setting, being popular both in the old Punjab and Sind." (p. 12, folio 11, note 5).

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindhis"; refers to: present day, "Even to this day"; translator's argument — FLAG as above: a people-word, kept because it describes the region's population): "Even to this day, most Punjabis and Sindhis are more likely to have heard a kafi than read one" (p. 15, folio 15).

## From Richard M. Eaton, Essays on Islam and Indian History

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/eaton_essays_islam_indian_history.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 711; book's historical prose): "Muslim navies sailed to the coasts of western India where in 711 they conquered and occupied the densely-populated Hindu—Buddhist society of Sind. Thus began the long and eventful encounter between Islamic and Indic civilizatiens [text-layer?] [civilizations]"; the next sentence is damaged: "While Arab rule in Sind was being consis [text-layer?] [word cut off] other Arab armies continued the overland drive eastward" (p. 30, folio 18).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: 711, with crops carried west "Between the eighth and tenth centuries"; reported view of Andrew Watson): "A key event in Watson's analysis was the Arab conquest of Sind in 711, which established a direct and regular contact between India and the Fertile Crescent"; it "threw open western Asia, Africa, and Europe to the agricultural treasures of India, effectively incorporating all these regions for the first time into a single agricultural universe" (p. 39, folio 27).
- (book's word: "eighth century Sind"; refers to: the eighth century; book's historical prose): "Arab rule in eighth century Sind having weakened and died, it was left to Persianized Turks to establish a permanent Muslim presence in India from the thirteenth century" (p. 47, folio 35).
- (book's word: "Punjab and Sind"; refers to: "Beginning in the fourteenth century and continuing through the Mughal period (1526-1858)"; author's argument): Sind is one of the western "wings of India that became Muslim-majority regions", where "the growth of Muslim societies correlated with the adoption of sedentary agriculture", a "frontier" society not yet deeply penetrated by Brahmanical values, and where "shrines of great Sufis attracted and integrated pre-agrarian and non-Muslim pastoral clans" (p. 48, folio 36). The same bullet stands under punjab.
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "between the seventh and eleventh centuries", Hsuan Tsang in the seventh century, the Chach-nama in the eighth; book's historical prose, citing Irfan Habib): the Jat pastoralists "had been moving up from Sind into the Multan area between the seventh and eleventh centuries"; Hsuan Tsang "wrote of river groups in Sind who 'gave themselves exclusively to tending cattle and from this derive their livelihood,' 'have no masters,'" with "'an unfeeling temper'" and "'a hasty disposition'"; the Chach-nama "styled these groups 'Jatts', located them in the wastes of the Indus valley in Sind, and noted an absence among them of social hierarchy" (p. 224, folio 212); the essay sums this up as the Jats' "migration north from Sind to Punjab" (p. 227, folio 215).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the eighth century"; book's historical prose, citing Y. Friedmann): "there is evidence that in the eighth century, when Arabs replaced Brahmans as rulers of Sind, the new rulers merely continued the earlier practice of requiring the Jats to associate themselves with dogs—unclean to Muslims as well as to Hindus—and in this way to affirm their lowly status" (p. 224, folio 212).

## From Saiyid Athar Abbas Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India, vol. 1

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rizvi_history_of_sufism_india_1.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the time of the Umayyad Caliph al-Walid I (705-15)", with the conquest "between 711 and 712", as printed; book's historical prose): Rizvi calls it "a myth" that the conquest of Sind began as a punitive expedition against "the ruling Raja Dahir": "In reality, the conquest of Transoxiana and Sind was initiated by Hajjaj, Walid's governor in Iraq, and was one aspect of his expansionist policy. In 710 Muhammad bin Qasim al-Saqafi began his advance through Makran and Baluchistan with the conquest of Sind between 711 and 712" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the early Arab conquest and "the eighth century", as printed; book's historical prose): the spread of sufism seen in Transoxiana and "Khurasen" [OCR?] [Khurasan] is not matched here: "no such developments in Sind are recorded"; "Early Arab conquerors settled their families in large numbers in the various towns of Sind"; Brahmans in high office converted "to retain their positions", and Buddhists "extremely hostile to Brahman domination" converted too; "Hindu and Buddhist scholars from Sind were sent to the 'Abbasid court", and "In the eighth century Abu Ma''shar¢ [OCR?] Najih (d. 170/787) a scholar of Hadis, and the poet Abu'l-'Ata> [OCR?] (died after 158/774) were leading literary figures from Sind" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the tenth century", the Fatimid Caliphate "from 909 to 1171", and "Between 977 and 985"; book's historical prose): "Lack of information about sufis in Sind may be ascribed to two factors": land routes "between Khurasan, Transoxiana and Sind were slow and arduous", and the rise of sufism coincided with the Isma'ili Fatimid Caliphate; "Mansura, the capital of Sind, ruled by the Sumira dynasty had also accepted the over-lordship of the Egyptian Caliphs", so sufism "was naturally cut, from close relations with other areas" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Lower Sind", "the Upper Sind"; refers to: "until the middle of the fourteenth century" and "After the end of the twelfth century", as printed; book's historical prose): "The Sumira dynasty continued to rule the Lower Sind until the middle of the fourteenth century. After the end of the twelfth century, the Upper Sind came under the domination of Sunni rule" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seven years" after "November 1221", and "the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228", as printed; book's historical prose): Iltutmish ("Itutmish" [OCR?] at p. 152), "seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134); Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya "openly sided with Sultan Shamsu'd-Din Iltutmish when he wished to add Multan and Sind to the Delhi Sultanate" (p. 209, folio printed "19]" [OCR?] [191]); "After the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228, relations between the Sultan of Delhi and the Shaikh became more intimate" (p. 210, folio 192).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the lower Sind"; refers to: the time of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan and Sultan Firuz, with the Thatta campaign "In 763/1362"; book's historical prose, reporting belief): the Saiyid's "assistance was also sought by the governors of Sind"; "It is believed that the Sumirahs of the lower Sind who were Isma'ilis, adopted Sunnism under the influence of the Saiyid. A Jat chief of Sind, later named 'Abdu'llah, is also said to have embraced Islam through a supernatural feat performed by Makhdum Jahaniyan" (p. 301, folio 279).

## From Omar Kasmani, Queer Companions

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kasmani_queer_companions.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- Named once, in a list of the saints whom Sindhi state publications did feature while Sehwan's saint
  went unmentioned: "other saints of Sindh (especially Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai, Sachal Sarmast, and
  Shah Inayat) were regularly featured during this time, and their annual fairs were marked by
  special editions" (p. 70, folio 53). **That is the entire evidence**: the one transferable fact is
  that Sindhi state publications regularly featured him, with special editions for his annual fair,
  in the period before the late 1960s when they ignored Sehwan's saint. No shrine, place, date or
  silsila: **(not stated)**. He has no index headword (p. 223, folio 206).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`bhit-bhit-shah`, `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast` and `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` rest on three
  bare names inside one parenthesis** (p. 70, folio 53). The only transferable fact is that Sindhi
  state publications featured them with special editions for their annual fairs while ignoring
  Sehwan's saint before the late 1960s.

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindhi publications of the state", "other saints of Sindh"; refers to: "until the late 1960s" and "from the 1960s onward", as the book states; author's argument, with the journals named in a note): "Sindhi publications of the state hardly mention the saint or the shrine of Sehwan until the late 1960s", while "other saints of Sindh (especially Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai, Sachal Sarmast, and Shah Inayat) were regularly featured during this time, and their annual fairs were marked by special editions. Only from the 1960s onward do Urdu and Sindhi newspapers offer detailed reportage on the annual fair of Sehwan" (p. 70, folio 53). The note names the source: "two prominent state-run journals, Nayin Zindagi and Mehran, published by the Information Department, Ministry of Information and Broadcasting, Sindh, and the Sindhi Adabi Board, respectively" (p. 189, folio 172, ch. 1 n. 28); and in the present, "State institutions like the Department of Culture or the Sindhi Adabi Board routinely publish books and brochures dedicated to the saint's life as well as to Sehwan's history and heritage" (p. 188, folio 171, ch. 1 n. 20).
- (book's word: "Sindhi society"; refers to: fieldwork present, occasion not dated; author's anecdote, the view of Abba, Amma's husband): "Evoking the concept of niyani, or little sister in Sindhi society, Amma's husband explained how among locals, the greatest of conflicts can be resolved if one of the feuding parties is able get [text-layer?] [able to get] a niyani to intervene on their behalf. Women fakirs were therefore perfect instruments for intercession in his opinion" (p. 94, folio 77).
- (book's word: "in Sindh"; refers to: "most historical accounts", period not stated; author's argument citing Boivin 2012b, 43): "In most historical accounts, it is the figure of Udero Lal who is conflated with Khizr in Sindh" (p. 195, folio 178, ch. 5 n. 9).
- (book's word: "the wider region of Sindh"; refers to: the present ("can still be documented"); author's argument): "Offerings to the Indus River can still be documented in the wider region of Sindh" (p. 195, folio 178, ch. 5 n. 11).
- (book's word: "Hindus of Sindh"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument citing Boivin): Raja Bharthari, "a prince who renounced his throne and established a hermitage in Sehwan", "is also known as Raja Virag by Hindus of Sindh" (p. 188, folio 171, ch. 1 n. 15). The figure is Sehwan-bound; only the naming by the province's Hindus is province-level.
- (book's word: "parts of Sindh and Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, study dated 2010; quoted source, Lubna Chaudhry): unmarried girls in their teens and early twenties "most vulnerable"; "discriminatory food portions within the family and limited access to health care" (p. 191, folio 174, ch. 2 n. 24; title at p. 205, folio 188) — see the Punjab bullet for the full quotation.

## From Michel Boivin, Hindu Sufis of South Asia

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/boivin_hindu_sufis_south_asia.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's names for him: **"Shah Abd al-Latif"**, **"Shah Latif"**, the bare **"Shah"**, once
**"Shah Abd al-Latif Bhittai"** (p. 91, folio 78), once **"Shah Abdul Latif"** (p. 212, folio 199),
twice with a diacritic **"Shah Laṭif"**, once run together in the text layer as **`ShahAbd al-Latif`**
(p. 91, folio 78), once with the space-before-apostrophe artefact **`Shah Abd al-Latif ’s`** (p. 160,
folio 147; also p. 14, folio not stated and p. 48, folio 35), and once **"Shah Ab al-Latif"
[text-layer? Shah Abd al-Latif]** (p. 220, folio 207). His work is **"Shah jo Risalo"**, also "Shah jo
rag" and "Shah's poetry". **"Bhittai", "Bhit" and "Bhit Shah" do not occur at all in chunks 001, 002,
006, 008, 010 or 011.** Present in chunks 001, 002, 003, 004, 005, 006, 008, 009, 010, 011 and 012.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, printed twice in the same parenthetical form**: "the Sufi poet Shah Abd al-Latif
    (**d. 1752**)" (p. 48, folio 35) and "the Shah jo Risalo authored by Shah Abd al-Latif (**d. 1752**)"
    (p. 155, folio 142). **A bare year, no era marker, no `AH` equivalent.** **Life dates once**: "Shah
    Abd al-Latif Bhittai (**1689–1752**)" (p. 91, folio 78) — **no era marker with them: (not stated).**
    Elsewhere in the book he carries no dates at all: **(not stated)** in chunks 001, 003, 004, 006, 009,
    010, 011, 012.
  - **Genealogy and birth**: "He was the great-grandson of **Shah Abd al-Karim, or Shah Karim**. He was
    born in **Hala** in a Sayyid family." (p. 91, folio 78) **No death place is given anywhere: (not
    stated).**
  - **Religious identity, stated once**: he was "a Muslim, a Sayyid, who was a **wujudi**, preaching
    throughout his poetry the Oneness of God." (p. 178, folio 165) **No silsila is given anywhere in the
    book: (not stated).**
  - **The historical record is thin, and the book says so**: "Many legends were framed about him,
    although it is still difficult to trace his historical character." (p. 91, folio 78) **The book
    recounts none of those legends: (not stated).**
  - **Standing**: "a Sindhi Sufi poet who is acclaimed by all Sindhis as the greatest poet of Sindh and
    the Sindhi language" (p. 91, folio 78); "Shah is the symbol of Sindhi culture for Muslims as well as
    for Hindus, in Pakistan as well as in India." (p. 92, folio 79); "Shah Latif retains a unique place in
    Sindhi poetry and literature, and needless to say, not all of his chapters (surs) are equally
    appreciated by the Hindu Sufis." (pp. 94–95, folios 81–82); "**the king of the Sufi poets of Sindh**"
    (p. 213, folio 200, Ch. 4 n. 4); "the greatest Sufi poet" (p. 108, folio 95). Named as one of the
    "'three great Sufi teachers'" (p. 49, folio 36), and as first of the "'trinity of the Sindhi poets'
    by Ram Panjwani (Panjwani 1987: 29): ShahAbd al-Latif, Sachal Sarmast and Sami" (p. 91, folio 78) —
    the three also called "the 'Great Integrators'", a coinage the book attributes in its text to Motilal
    Jotwani but cites as `(Motwani 1986: 150)`.
  - **Placed in the Bhakti movement by a quoted author, and Boivin objects**: "Unfortunately, there is no
    comprehensive argumentation supplying his claim that 'Shah Abdul Latif (was) a late participant in
    the Bhakti movement' (Jotwani 1996: 121)." (p. 88, folio 75 — the parenthesis "(was)" is the book's
    own). The same claim recurs: "'the late participaters in the Bhakti movement which had a major impact
    in Sindh through the spread of Guru Nanak's teaching' (Motwani 1986: 155)" — "here he spoke especially
    of Shah Latif and Sachal" (p. 91, folio 78). **`participaters` is as printed.**
  - **The jogis, and a pilgrimage itinerary**: "he was very fascinated by the jogis, considering them the
    models of renunciation" (p. 91, folio 78); "He probably travelled with them on a number of Hindu
    pilgrimages, including **Dwarka and Hinglaj**. The technical vocabulary used by Shah Latif to name
    the jogis gives evidence that they were Nathpanthis, and that they were therefore an important
    religious community in eighteenth-century Sindh." (p. 92, folio 79) **The hedge "probably" is the
    book's own; no date and no companion is named for the pilgrimages: (not stated).** "The jogi, a
    vernacular form of yogi, is usually the model of renunciation in Sindhi Sufi poetry, especially in
    Shah Abd al-Latif's work" (p. 120, folio 107); the jogi reference "is fully expressed in Shah Abd
    al-Latif's verses" (p. 105, folio 92).
  - **Doctrine**: "His emphasis on the wahdat-e wujud furthered his resonance among different populations
    because it indicated that distinctions, especially religious, were disposable in the face of unity."
    (p. 92, folio 79) But the technical expression is **not** his: "The expression wahdat-e wujud is not
    included in the lexicon, of course, because it was not used by Shah Abd al-Latif." (p. 100, folio 87)
    The word he did use is `wahdat`, rendered into Sindhi as `hekro`; and "tuhnjo Allah hekro, 'Your God
    is One' – which first appears in Shah Karim's verses and then in Shah Abd al-Latif (Lalwani 1890,
    vol. I: 45)" (p. 100, folio 87).
  - **Influences**: "Among his influences, Jalal al-Din Rumi was so dominant that his poetry is sometimes
    called **the Masnawi of Sindh**." (p. 92, folio 79, endnote 5)
  - **Quoted as a doctrinal authority by a Theosophist, and both claims are Parsram's, not the book's**:
    "He added that Shah Abd al-Latif himself referred to the 'one curved word', namely om. He provided a
    translation of Shah Latif's quotation, according to which om is as light in the darkness, and that
    the Sufi has to keep mim (m) in his mind, and place alif (a) before." (p. 49, folio 36); and "he had
    met a very old Sufi who claimed to be a recipient of their teaching, as Shah Latif had been before
    him" (p. 50, folio 37). Both reported from Parsram 1924.
  - **Quoted for a doctrine of secrecy**: "Rochaldas quotes Shah Abd al-Latif's verses, where the poets
    express that silence is golden, and exposure is harmful, and that love is great but secrecy is
    greater (Hari 1995: 211)." (p. 166, folio 153) The context is the warning that a disciple who exposes
    his ishq haqiqi will share "the destiny… of Mansur Hallaj".
  - **Quoted for a definition of the Sufi, at second hand**: "Panjwani quotes him in defining who is a
    Sufi: ‘A Sufi belongs to no sect. No one can label him. He fights with his own selves and divulges
    nothing. He extends help even to those who are hostile to him’ (Panjwani 1987: 34)." (p. 160,
    folio 147) **The quotation is Panjwani's attribution, not the book's own text of Shah Abd al-Latif.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The darbar at Bhit Shah as the book's type case for a shared Sufi tomb — the hardest shrine fact in
    the book for this row**: "The first category is the most common: a sacred place is a Sufi tomb which
    is visited both by Muslims and Hindus. For example, this is the case with **Shah Abd al-Latif's darbar
    in Bhit Shah**. The religious identity of the saint is not challenged: he was a Muslim, a Sayyid, who
    was a wujudi, preaching throughout his poetry the Oneness of God. The Hindus belonging to different
    paths and castes come every day and for the annual urs. They go to the tomb, circumambulating around
    it, touching it, and then attend the **Shah jo rag**, the singing of Shah's poetry by the local
    faqirs." (p. 178, folio 165) **The ʿurs is dated only as "annual"; no month, no day, no attendance, no
    custodian: (not stated).**
  - **The shrine named as a negative, and a custodian asserted but unnamed**: "while Shah Abd al-Latif's
    poetry was highly praised, **his darbar at Bhit Shah is not mentioned as a reference point**, and no
    link has been mentioned between the local sajjada nashin and the faqirs." (pp. 60–61, folios 47–48)
    **A sajjada nashin at Bhit Shah is asserted to exist and is not named: (not stated).**
  - **His tomb as an instance of shared ziyarat, in a sentence the book leaves ambiguous**: "Known as
    ziyarat, both Hindus and Muslims bring textiles to put on the tomb, or they only touch and kiss the
    tomb for obtaining blessings from the buried saint. This is the case of the visit to the dargah by the
    great mystic poet Shah Abd al-Latif." (p. 50, folio 37) **The wording is the book's and is ambiguous
    as printed — it can be read as the dargah *of* the poet or a visit *by* him; recorded as printed, not
    resolved.**
  - **The site named once more, and only inside a publisher's name**: the 1985 *Shah jo risalo* is issued
    by the "**Shah Abdul Latif Bhitshah Cultural Centre Committee**" — the full entry as printed: "Shah
    Abd al-Latif Bhittai, 1985, *Shah jo risalo alias Ganje Latif*, 3 volumes, Revised, Annotated and
    Translated by Muhammad Yakoob Agha, Hyderabad, Shah Abdul Latif Bhitshah Cultural Centre Committee"
    (p. 232, folio 219). **This is the only place in the index chunks where "Bhitshah" is printed.**
  - **No tomb description, no building date, no architecture, no ʿurs date, no custodian's name, no
    Auqaf, no revenue and no visitor figure is given for Bhit Shah anywhere in the book: (not stated).**
  - **Index**: the locator run `al-Latif, Shah Abd` at folios **1, 4, 21, 35–7, 48, 55, 75, 78–80, 81–2,
    87, 92–3, 107, 142, 143, 144, 145, 147, 148, 152, 153, 165, 173, 187, 199 n.13, 200 n.4** (p. 239,
    folio not stated) — the longest run in that chunk for any archive figure. A second run for his book,
    listed separately from the man: `Shah jo risalo (al-Latif)` at folios **1, 4, 21, 34–5, 50, 79, 87,
    142, 157** (p. 249, folio 236); the two runs share folios 1, 4, 21 and 87.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **His poetry sung, and the one place it is not used.** At Bhit Shah, the "Shah jo rag, the singing of
    Shah's poetry by the local faqirs" (p. 178, folio 165); "the bhagat's repertoire always includes Sufi
    poetry – **Shah jo Risalo** – first as well as other Sufi kalams. Nonetheless, it is an oral
    transmission." (p. 170, folio 157) **The book does not on that page say whose work the Shah jo Risalo
    is: (not stated).** Chanted at Daulatram's versi (p. 150, folio 137). **Against that, the book's
