From a Kohli Bheel to a Syed, every one considers Bhitai his spiritual guide" (p. 95, folio not stated). Sindhi nationalists see Latif "as the ideal Sindhi—the perfect example for the tolerant and “syncretic” history of Sindh", while "more orthodox writers aim to explain away Latif's journey as “youthful recklessness”" (p. 95, folio not stated). Khadim Soomro: "A Sindhi has a special place in human civilization because he stands for good and liberal attitude and behavior" (p. 96, folio not stated). Latif is dated "(1690–1751)" here (p. 95).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument): "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh, offers a whole range of salvific spaces that are frequently visited by different religious communities and form a network of pilgrimage routes or ritual journeys. Sindhi nationalists often emphasize these characteristics of Sindh as a landscape of “coexistence”" (p. 107, folio not stated). Legends linking Latif and the Goddess "connect Hindu and Muslim sacred spaces in Sindh and show that imagined landscapes do not necessarily have to be separated using clear-cut religious taxonomies" (p. 109, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh Hindoos", "Sindh", "Sind Hindus"; refers to: Burton "1851", McMurdo via Mubarak 1985, Thakur 1997, and "the past few centuries" up to today; quoted sources): Burton: "a description of them [the Lohanas], therefore, will be a general portrait of the Sindh Hindoos" (Burton 1851, 314); McMurdo: "The Lowannas or Lohannas compose the great body of Hindus in Sindh" (Mubarak 1985, 55); "Over the past few centuries, the Lohana community absorbed many other groups and clans, now encompassing a significant portion of the upper classes of Pakistani Hindus" (p. 79, folio not stated). Thakur: Lohanas form "the general body of the Sind Hindus" (p. 80, folio not stated). "Due to the heterogeneity of the Lohanas in Sindh, it is not possible to make any universal claim for the whole community" (p. 216, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" (Kalhora rule), "the nineteenth century", and "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Lachaier 2008, Cook 2010, Lari 1994, and Zahid-Khan quoted in Cook): "in the nineteenth century, the Lohanas were the most important trading community in the areas of Sindh, Kutch, and Saurashtra (Lachaier 2008, 82). One reason for this was the established rule of the Kalhoras in Sindh in the eighteenth century, which brought new business opportunities for merchant professions (Cook 2010, 140)" (p. 80, folio not stated). Zahid-Khan "attributes this merging of different groups to the small number of non-Muslim Sindhis who united in the face of the majority"; "Sindh's location between trading routes connecting Afghanistan, Khorasan, Arabia, Persia, Turkey, and other places made the province appealing to a wide array of merchants from the entire continent"; "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century, a separation occurred within these merchant groups of Sindh": Lohanas working for local rulers became "Amils", "initially 10 to 15 percent of the Lohanas (Lari 1994, 211)", and those who kept trading were "Bhaiband" (p. 81, folio not stated). (Kutch and Saurashtra are named alongside Sindh; see `not attached`.)
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present, plus the censuses of "1881" and "1891"; author's argument citing Lari 1994 and Thakur 1997): "Many Lohanas in Sindh follow the teachings of Nanak, somewhat obscuring their religious affiliation for the colonial officers. Hence, in the 1881 census, Lohanas from Shikarpur and Hyderabad were counted as Sikhs, while ten years later, in the 1891 census, they were classified as Hindus (Lari 1994, 211f.)" (p. 80, folio not stated). "many middle- and upper-class Hindus in Sindh are followers of the Nanak paṃth" (p. 257, folio not stated); "Many upper-caste Hindus in Sindh, especially from the Lohana community, refer to Nanak and his teachings (Thakur 1997, 141), and it is common to cover one's head before walking into a tikānā (Sin.), the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib" (p. 286, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh", "other provinces of Pakistan"; refers to: Thakur's *Sindhi Culture* "(1951)", cited as Thakur 1997, and the fieldwork conversations; quoted source and ethnographic present): Thakur writes that the "[c]aste system is almost absent in Sind" (Thakur 1997, 25), a perception the author links to "the assimilative role of the Sindhi Lohana jāti" and to "Sindhi nationalistic discourses, which portray Sindh as a place of religious harmony and tolerance". Devoted Sindhi Hindu nationalists told him caste discrimination "exists today only in India or other provinces of Pakistan"; "Many upper-caste Sindhi Hindus often deny or ignore acts of segregation", which "Dalit organizations" fiercely oppose (pp. 277–278, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument and ethnographic present): the HSM's idea of progress and education "stands in sharp contrast to low-caste rituals in Sindh such as the sacrifice of animals or the consumption of alcohol as prasād" (p. 278, folio not stated); "many other Hindu groups in Sindh, and in fact all over Pakistan, sacrifice animals for the fulfillment of certain wishes or during particular rites of passage" (p. 310, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: Burton, "1851", colonial; quoted source, Burton, via endnotes): Burton "confirms the unusual use of the term “Vaishiya” in Sindh, which is mainly applied “in a limited sense to signify operatives and mechanics, opposed to merchants and shopkeepers”" (Burton 1851, 237) (pp. 98–99, folio not stated, endnote 16). Burton says the Saraswat Brahmins, many of whom were thought to be low caste, "were known for their devotion to the Devi who, in most areas of Sindh, was called Hinglaj (Burton 1851, 312)" (p. 100, folio not stated, endnote 28).
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh", "the Sindh", "the people of Sindh"; refers to: mythic time in the Brahmakshatriya story, and for the Charani Avar a birth "in the year 888 CE" and Huns "dated to the eighth century by Samaur", both as the sources state; mytho-historic narrative, as reported by the author): in the Brahmakshatriya story, Parashurama "arrives in the province of Sindh" to kill Kshatriyas, and "The mighty king Ratnasena, the ruler of Sindh" flees (pp. 61–62, folio not stated). In the Charan story of Avar, famine drives her family "to go to Sindh, then ruled by Umar Sumra who had recently converted to Islam and was now known by the name of “Hamir”". The Samma and Bhati Rajput kings, who "had wanted to occupy the Sindh for some time", help Avar kill him and "then divided the Sindh between them and ruled in peace". Another story has Avar kill "fifty-two of these Hun-demons" and so save "the people of Sindh" (Samaur 1999, 509) (p. 72, folio not stated). These are legends, not historical statements.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "for many generations" before Partition, and after it; ethnographic present, Vagri oral history): "According to Vagri history, the community migrated back and forth between Sindh and Gujarat for many generations and, like other nomadic groups, were eventually forced to settle on one side of the border after the Partition. Since there is no written account of their movement, the clan's detailed history can only be based on speculation" (p. 308, folio not stated).

## From Pnina Werbner and Helene Basu (eds.), Embodying Charisma

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/werbner_basu_embodying_charisma.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's argument, Jamal Malik, ch. 9): the holy-man cult is "particularly evident in the countryside, where major landlords and large-scale land concentrations are crucial features, especially in Punjab and Sindh"; "the local pîr represents the local landlord or is at least closely related to him" (p. 199, folio 188).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: British rule — "against the British" — with pir Pagara's present authority "in present-day Pakistan"; no years given; contributor's endnote, Charles Lindholm, ch. 10, n. 12): to his argument that in South Asia under indirect rule "Sufis were usually equally content to maintain the peace as long as they were allowed to continue their religious routines and keep their estates and authority" (p. 237, folio 226), Lindholm notes: "Exceptions include the two Hur rebellions against the British in the Sind by the followers of pir Pagara, a landlord, political leader and hereditary head of a Sufi brotherhood who still retains great authority in present-day Pakistan (see Lambrick 1972 for a fictionalized account)" (pp. 241–242, folios 230–231). "the Sind" is the book's form, carried as printed; no boundary is supplied.

## From Shemeem Burney Abbas, The Female Voice in Sufi Ritual

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/abbas_female_voice_sufi_ritual.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's most heavily documented archive figure, present in all eleven chunks. The book's names for
him: "Shah Abdul Latif", "Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit", "Shah Latif", "Sain Latif", "Shah"; for the site,
"Bhit Shah", "Shah Abdul Latif's shrine in Bhit Shah", "the jamālī (aesthetic) shrine".

- **Saint**
  - Placed only by name and work. The book prints `Shah Abdul Latif (d.  )` [text-layer?: digits
    dropped] (p. 50, folio not stated) — **the death year is printed on the page and destroyed in the
    text layer; 1752 has NOT been taken from the archive row.** No birth date, no silsila and no
    custodian appear anywhere in the eleven chunks: all **(not stated)**.
  - Descent: "both Muslim and non-Muslim devotees venerate Shah Abdul Latif's shrine because he was a
    Syed, who claimed descent from the prophet Muhammad through his daughter Fatima and son-in-law and
    cousin Ali ibn Abi Talib" (p. 50, folio not stated).
  - Influence: "he was influenced by Rumi" — Alan Faqir (p. 157, folio not stated).
  - He is filed by the book's own index under `Suﬁ musicians, poets and scholars`, not under `Suﬁ
    Saints belonging to Pakistan-Indian silsilās` (p. 239, folio not stated).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - "Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit Shah" is one of five shrines where "qawwālī is performed every evening
    after the prayers", with performances that "continue well past midnight" (p. 42, folio not stated).
  - **The daily ritual, stated as continuous since the shrine was built.** "Even today, his faqīrs
    sing his traditional poetry at the shrine, in the melodies that he composed. They sing his poetry
    in the falsetto, imitating a woman's voice. The ritual of singing Shah's poetry to his melodies
    has been performed every evening since the shrine has been there" (p. 96, folio not stated); and
    "even today when his faqīrs (mendicants) sing the daily mystical concert at his shrine, they chant
    in the falsetto that imitates the female voice" (p. 50, folio not stated). The glossary restates
    it: "Shah's faqīrs at his shrine in Bhit Shah are renowned for singing the vāī in the falsetto"
    (p. 189, folio not stated).
  - **Why the shrine carries its epithet.** "It is precisely for this reason that his shrine is known
    as the jamālī (aesthetic) shrine" (p. 50, folio not stated) — the reason being the falsetto
    female-voice chanting. Restated at p. 167 (folio not stated): "Shah's shrine is known in the region
    as a jamālī (aesthetic) shrine". An endnote gives the typology: "Jamālī mysticism is attained
    through intuition and creativity, such as by Shah Abdul Latif, a poet and musician. In contrast to
    this, jalālī mysticism is achieved through rigor and discipline" (p. 197, folio not stated).
  - The faqīrs wear black: "Shah's poetry is kind of melancholic—that is why the faqīrs at his shrine
    wear black—it is the same with his followers—his faqīrs sing his poetry like females—creating the
    female voices—his poetry is sung throughout the night at his shrine—there are maqāms in his poetry
    when certain sections are sung at particular times of the night—his poetry is sung on the tambūrā
    [stringed instrument]—it is sung in rhythm" — Sikander Baloch (p. 63, folio not stated).
  - Invited singers: "In the Sind, performers like Abida Parvin are invited to sing at Shah Abdul
    Latif's shrine" (p. 55, folio not stated).
  - **A woman is caretaker of a subsidiary set of graves.** "A woman was the caretaker of the tombs of
    Shah Abdul Latif's female relatives who were buried in a compound of the shrine" (p. 20, folio not
    stated). **She is not named and the female relatives are not named: (not stated).**
  - **Negative evidence, stated as such.** The loṭevālī roving-minstrel tradition does not occur here:
    "I have not seen such a tradition at Bhit Shah" — Naseer Mirza (p. 61, folio not stated).
  - Non-Muslim devotees, with the book's own two reasons. "Their women are ardent devotees at Muslim
    shrines such as those of Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit Shah. They go there primarily because the shrine
    is located in Hala, the heart of a fertile agricultural belt, where they can make a living working
    on the large feudal farms" (p. 46, folio not stated) — **wage labour first, devotion second, in the
    book's own order**; and "This further explains why the non-Muslim Kolhi and Bhil communities are
    devotees of Shah Abdul Latif's shrine in Bhit Shah, Sind: his poetry is linguistically diverse and
    is tolerant of other beliefs" (p. 50, folio not stated). See Doubts on "located in Hala".
  - **The author's own observation in the courtyard.** "Early in [numeral dropped, text-layer], I
    photographed non-Muslim Kolhi and Bhil peasant women at Shah Abdul Latif's shrine in Bhit Shah. I
    was standing in the shrine courtyard when I suddenly saw a train of graceful, colorfully dressed
    women in their ghāgrās (long skirts). They were all clad in flowered veils. They walked toward the
    shrine exit and hastened to pickups parked by the roadside in which their menfolk waited to drive
    them back" (p. 134, folio not stated). **The year of the visit is destroyed: (not stated).**
  - A plate is captioned "Non-Muslim Kolhi women: Shah Abdul Latif's shrine, Bhit Shah, Sind" (p. 47,
    folio not stated) — **figure number dropped**.
  - A devotee-musician: Alan Faqir "claimed to be a faqīr of the Bhit Shah shrine" (p. 78, folio not
    stated); "He considered himself a faqīr (mendicant) of the Bhit Shah shrine of Shah Abdul Latif"
    (p. 211, folio not stated); "Shah Latif—he is my mentor—he is my murshid" (p. 157, folio not
    stated). Two shrine musicians are listed in the bibliography: "Shah, Syed Ghulam Ali, and Nur
    Muhammad Shah. Bhit Shah musicians. Audiocassette. Hyderabad: Institute of Sindhology." — **this
    entry prints no year at all** (p. 217, folio not stated).
  - **An ʿurs exists but is never dated or described.** It is attached only through a conference
    title: "Proceedings of Shah Abdul Latif Conference on His [numeral dropped, text-layer] Urs
    Celebrations" (p. 219, folio not stated). **The ordinal is destroyed and must not be guessed.**
  - No sajjada nashin, no Auqaf, no endowment, no land, no building date, no visitor number:
    all **(not stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - "Among musician communities in the Sind, Shah's Risālo is treated with great respect because of
    its sacred Quranic context, and the book is not allowed to lie around on the floor" (p. 51, folio
    not stated); "Since large sections of the Quran are quoted in his Risālo, musicians in Sind hold
    Shah's written text in the utmost veneration" (p. 166, folio not stated).
  - "The findings at two major shrines in Sind, that is, Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit Shah and Hazrat Lal
    Shahbaz Qalandar at Sehwan Sharif, point toward significant female input in Sufi rituals" (p. 67,
    folio not stated).
  - Sufiānā-kalām "is a feature of the shrines in Sind, especially those of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz
    Qalandar and Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit" (p. 85, folio not stated).
  - **Fifty overnight camping places, as an informant's claim.** Shaikh Ghulam Hussain: "In the case
    of Shah Latif you will find at least fifty places where he used to spend the nights. Audiences
    would come there and sit there at night—there would be a paṛāo [a camp]—they would make a
    fire—and they would have a mehfil—and when he sang his narratives, there would be faqīrs and
    dervishes with him" (p. 51, folio not stated). **Reported by Abbas as the informant's claim, not
    her own count; "fifty" is spelled out and therefore intact.**
  - Outside the shrine, Manganhar women of Alan Faqir's family "would sing the arifānā-kalām of Shah
    Latif—some songs do not have a poet—you sing songs for the love of God—you sing ''Allāh hū—Allāh
    hū'' and finish off with ''and so says Shah Latif—O Latif, I say your sanā [praise]''" (p. 79,
    folio not stated).
- **The Risālo**
  - "Shah Abdul Latif's entire Risālo is based on these narratives, and he evokes mystical poetry for
    his tragic heroines to become symbols of otherworldly love" (p. 120, folio not stated). "Each one
    of these romances is sung in its own sūr. Thus, there is a ''Sūr Sohni'' in Latif's poetry to sing
    about Sohni, ''Sūr Mumal-Rano'' to sing about Mumal-Rano's tale, and ''Sūr Marvi'' to narrate
    Umar-Marvi's story" (p. 120, folio not stated).
  - **The book's count of the sūrs contradicts itself three ways and all three are kept.** "Altogether,
    there are thirty sūrs in the Risālo, and at least ten are dedicated to female heroines, or sūrmīs"
    (p. 120, folio not stated); the glossary: "It is based on thirty sūrs; **nine** of the sūrs are
    named after Shah's female heroines (sūrmīs)" (p. 187, folio not stated); an endnote: "**Ten** of
    the sūrs in the Risālo are built around female heroines (sūrmis) and thus are called Sūr Sohni,
    Sūr Sassi, Sūr Marvi, Sūr Mumal-Rano, Sūr Leela-Canesar, Sūr Sassi-Maazuri, and Sūr Sassi Desi"
    (p. 198, folio not stated) — **and that list names seven.** All three figures are spelled out in
    words, so **this is the book contradicting itself, not text-layer damage.**
  - An endnote restates the work: "A Sufi epic based on three books. In Sindhi risālo means 'the
    message.' The Risālo narrative is based on thirty sections, each of which is built around a sūr
    that is a form or mode of singing, a rhythm, or melody" (pp. 197–198, folios not stated).
  - Named sūrs in the glossary: `Sūr Maruī/Marvi`, "a melancholic sūr"; `Sūr Mumal-Rano`; `Sūr Rām
    Kalī`, "Early-morning sūr"; `Sūr Sasui/Sassi`; `Sūr Suhni/Sohni`; `Sūr Holī Kamach`, "A sūr named
    after the Holi festival" (pp. 187–188, folios not stated).
  - Heroines: "Shah's sūrmīs (heroines) such as Sassi, Sohni, and Marvi are drawn cross culturally
    from the centuries-old pre-Islamic myths of the region that he blended into the Islamic mystical
    framework. He made his sūrmīs into representations of the pining soul in search of the beloved"
    (p. 47, folio not stated). The gloss: "The male equivalent of the sūrmī is a sūrma, which means a
    hero; someone who has prowess. Therefore, Shah intends his sūrmis to be perceived as women with
    prowess" (p. 120, folio not stated). **The book's own macron is inconsistent — `sūrmīs`/`sūrmī`
    against `sūrmis` in adjacent sentences — and is carried as printed.**
  - Jogīs: "in Shah's Risālo, especially in the section called ''Sūr Rām Kalī,'' there are innumerable
    references to mendicants (faqirs) who are ascetic with slit ears and who wear large rings in their
    ears… They travel from place to place in search of divine wisdom and wear colored robes" (p. 51,
    folio not stated); "Somehow Shah's jogīs are closely interlinked with his female voices, and there
    is hardly a sūr (a melody) in his Risālo where there is not a reference to one or the other"
    (p. 53, folio not stated). The eight Sindhi terms he uses for them, as printed: "jājak, maganhār,
    atāi, pān, chaṛan, rāgī, baraṭ, and rabābī" (p. 53, folio not stated; repeated at p. 183, folio
    not stated).
  - **A transmission claim, given as report.** "Mai Naimat, a maidservant of Shah Abdul Latif, from
    whose memory his entire Risālo is said to have been reconstructed" (p. 21, folio not stated).
    **Abbas gives this as "is said to have been", with no date and no account of the loss it
    repaired: (not stated).**
  - **His editorial principle, in Alan Faqir's words, and it turns on graves.** "Latif put a message
    in his legends—whether it is Marvi or Sassi or Sohni—or Leela-Canesar—whether it is
    Shireen-Farhad—or Rano—he puts them all in his narratives—he does not talk of Hir-Ranjha—of
    Layla-Majnun—because they have no graves—Shah only wrote of that which could be verified"
    (p. 157, folio not stated).
  - A journey: Abida Parvin, "Shah Abdul Latif—he went to Rajasthan—and the person there is a
    Rajasthani—the Shah took very simple words from him… although the individual from this region is
    not literate—the Shah acquired great wisdom from him—from the people" (p. 52, folio not stated).
  - Cotton-spinning songs: "Cross culturally, the same theme is found in Shah Abdul Latif's kapāītī
    (cotton-spinning songs) in his Risālo" (p. 152, folio not stated), four lines quoted: "Not
    withstanding the spinners / Carded the strands well / And spun out very fine yarn / Accumulated
    wealth, O mother" (p. 152, folio not stated).
  - Sung outside Pakistan: "Jairazbhoy and Catlin's recent film of folk performers in Kacch of Indian
    Gujrat demonstrates that Shah Abdul Latif's poetry of the Risālo is sung there in the Sindhi
    language" (p. 155, folio not stated).
- **Significance**
  - He opened Abida Parvin's Islamabad concert: the programme's first item is "Shah Abdul Latif of
    Bhit / Invocation / Sindhi" (p. 101, folio not stated); "she initiated the performance with a
    mystical invocation from Shah Abdul Latif's poetry in Sindhi" (p. 113, folio not stated).
  - He is one of only two poets whose Sindhi only she among the performers can sing: "she is able to
    sing the poetry of Sufi poets like Shah Abdul Latif and Sacchal Sarmast in Sindhi, which her male
    qawwāl counterparts cannot" (p. 105, folio not stated).
  - A political reading, attributed to the informant: Alan Faqir "told me in his interview that Sassi
    and Marvi in Shah's poetry also represent the land pillaged by conquerors and invaders. He claimed
    that when he sang Shah's poetry he did it with a passion that would convey the exploitation of the
    land" (p. 196, folio not stated); "These are all Alan Faqir's political interpretations of the
    female myths in Shah Abdul Latif's Risālo" (p. 210, folio not stated).
  - Works on him cited (**every year destroyed**): M. Yakoob Agha, trans., *Shah Jo Risālo alias Ganje
    Latif* (Hyderabad: Shah Abdul Latif Bhit Shah Cultural Center Committee) (pp. 205, 210, 213, 219,
    folios not stated); Lilaram Watanmal Lalwani, *The Life, Religion, and Poetry of Shah Latif*
    (reprint, Lahore: Sang-e Meel) (p. 205, folio not stated); Tirathdas Hotechand, "The Risālo: Its
    Musical Compositions" (p. 197, folio not stated); N. A. Baloch, "Shah Abdul Latif: The Founder of
    a New Music Institution", in *Folk Music of Sind*, ed. G. A. Allana (Jamshoro: Institute of
    Sindhology) (p. 197, folio not stated); H. T. Sorley, *Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit* (Delhi: Ashish);
    Ernst Trumpp, *Shah Jo Risālo* (reprint, Hyderabad: Institute of Sindhology) (both p. 234, folio
    not stated); *Risālo* (Hyderabad: Institute of Sindhology, **n.d.** — the book's own statement,
    not damage) (p. 232, folio not stated).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Bhit Shah "located in Hala" against "in Bhit Shah, Sind"** (pp. 46, 50, folios not stated). **The
  archive carries `bhit-bhit-shah` at "Bhit (Bhit Shah), Bhit, Sindh" and a separate row
  `shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala` at "Hala, Matiari District, Sindh". Whether "located in Hala" is the
  book's loose usage for a district or belt, or an error, cannot be decided from the text.**

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Sind province", "Sind"; refers to: "the coming of Islam in [numeral dropped] when Muhammad bin Qasim conquered the region", and then "After his conquest"; book's historical prose): "The Sufi mystical traditions came to the subcontinent of Pakistan and India through the Sind province"; Muhammad bin Qasim "came to Sind through the land route from Iran. After his conquest, a long line of Sufi mystics came to the region through Baluchistan, Mekran, Multan, and Sind itself" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the province of Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's statement, unsourced — "are said to be"): "In the province of Sind alone there are said to be more than a hundred thousand Sufi shrines" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind region"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's time (his death year is dropped, `(d.  )`) and "even today"; book's historical prose): "In the Sind region Shah Abdul Latif (d. [numeral dropped]) and other Sufi poets composed poetry in the vernacular languages and dialects in which they sang as roving mendicants within an Islamic framework. For fear of the clergy mystic poetry sung to music remained confined to the minstrel class" (p. 50, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind-Rajasthan area", "the Sind-Rajasthan continuum"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "There is evidence of roving minstrels in the Sind-Rajasthan area. Among them are the jogī snake charmers and flute players" (p. 77, folio not stated). The unit is the book's own cross-border compound; only the Sind half is attached here.
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork "in Sind in [numeral dropped]" and present tense; ethnographic present, glossary definition, endnote): "The Kolhis are the indigenous peasant communities of Sind who are sometimes migratory. Usually they are temporary farm laborers in the large feudal holdings. They are non-Muslims, and together with the Bhils they are the native inhabitants of the Sind-Rajasthan continuum" (p. 46, folio not stated); because Shah Abdul Latif wrote "in the Sindhi vernacular, the Kohli and Bhil male and female peasants can understand and relate to it" (p. 47, folio not stated). The Glossary defines "Bhīls Aboriginal landless roving peasants in Sind" (p. 179, folio not stated) and "Kolhis Aboriginal landless roving peasants in the Sind" (p. 184, folio not stated). An unpaired endnote reads "Non-Muslim migratory peasants who work on large landholdings in Sind and Indian Gujrat" (p. 200, folio not stated). The text prints both "Kolhi" and "Kohli".
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, glossary definition): "In Sind there can be no ritual without the Manganhar, be it a birth, a wedding, or a death. They are the designated community musicians, and each community has its own Manganhars" (p. 77, folio not stated); "In Sind, the Sidis of African descent and the Manganhar musicians render the ceremonial singing of devotional narratives" (p. 169, folio not stated). The Glossary gives "Manganhār Hereditary professional musicians in the Sind and Rajasthan", and "mangiārī" and "mangtī" as "A female musician in the Sind and Rajasthan" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind province of Pakistan", "the Sind"; refers to: "now", the author's fieldwork among the Sidi in Karachi Mori (year dropped), with the arrival placed "through the ages"; ethnographic present, plus the informant's statement of Qalandar Shah): Sidis "were initially brought as slaves from Africa to work in the subcontinent of Pakistan and India. Although they are now free, some of them are attached to the feudal households in the Sind province of Pakistan. Their native language is Sindhi" (pp. 67–68, folios not stated). Qalandar Shah says: "the Africans are present in the Sind in large numbers—because of the slave trade—they were brought here in large numbers—and then—with the abolition of slavery—here too they were set free"; "They had been coming continuously—through the ages" (p. 69, folio not stated). Also: "the Sidis in Sind and Kacch claim descent from Hazrat Bilal" (p. 167, folio not stated). The Glossary gives "Sidi Also known as Sheedi or Syedi in Sind and Gujrat" (p. 188, folio not stated), and the mugarmān drum is "held sacred by the Sidi communities in Sind, Baluchistan, Rajasthan, and Gujrat who use them in their shrines and for rituals" (p. 185, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present (years dropped); informants' statements reported by the author, and author's argument): "The Sidi, Mohana, and Manganhar women associated with feudal households in the Sind said that they sing devotional Sufi poetry at their mentor's shrine only on special occasions, such as his urs or a religious event", "only perform before female audiences in their murshid's shrine", and "their female patrons pay them well" (p. 95, folio not stated); these musicians "sing in the Syed households in the Sind among women audiences only" (p. 112, folio not stated); "Sidi women render ritual musical services in the households in the Sind" (p. 167, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Sind"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present, and present tense; informant's statement of Taj Mastani, Alan Faqir's own words, glossary definition): Taj Mastani can negotiate "with television stations and her clients from the affluent feudal households in Sind, who can reach her for ritual ceremonies" (p. 34, folio not stated); the Glossary defines "vaderā A feudal landlord in the Sind" (p. 189, folio not stated); Alan Faqir: "you see the vaḍerās [landowners] have disputes with each other", and they come to the faqīr for a duā (p. 78, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "In Sind", "the Sind", "Sindhi Sufi poetry"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present (the Radio Pakistan interview year is dropped); author's argument, informants' statements of Sikander Baloch and Naseer Mirza, glossary definition, endnote): "In Sind this form of choir singing is called sūng" (p. 40, folio not stated); Baloch and Mirza "used the indigenous term sūng for the practice in the Sufi poetics and music where the lover woos the estranged beloved through a serenade on the capṛī", and "The speech and song create ecstasy, which is a very special context of some shrines in Sind" (p. 55, folio not stated); Baloch: "that is why there is this school of thought in Sindhi Sufi poetry—the sūng—in which the lover sings to win back the estranged beloved—this is called manānā" (p. 63, folio not stated). The Glossary has "sūng A choir that sings at the Sufi shrines in Sind" (p. 188, folio not stated). An endnote says "A native Sindhi informant corrected this term to mean a choir of mendicants who sing mystical poetry, but I retain the context in which I interviewed Baloch and Mirza" (p. 198, folio not stated). These three senses are kept as printed.
- (book's word: "the shrines in Sind"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): sufiānā-kalām "is usually sung in the vernacular and is a feature of the shrines in Sind, especially those of Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar and Shah Abdul Latif at Bhit" (p. 85, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sindhi shrines"; refers to: the author's fieldwork present; author's argument): among the areas of female participation she identified: "Women as singers/musicians/participants of Sufi songs, sometimes even called the faqīrīanī in the Sindhi shrines" (p. 21, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: "now", the speaker's twenty years with Radio Pakistan; informant's statement of Naseer Mirza (`M:`), with Reshma's answer): the loṭevālī gypsy women sang only at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine, "certainly, that is the only place in the Sind now where the loṭevālīs come"; "I have not seen such a tradition at Bhit Shah—not at Sacchal Sarmast—not at Rikhail Shah—or any of the Sufi dargāhs [shrines] in the Sind—no loṭevālī passes by these shrines—except at Qalandar Lal Shahbaz" (p. 61, folio not stated). Asked whether it is "more of a tradition in the Sind—in the Rajasthan area that women will sing at the Sufi shrines", Reshma answered "Yes—this is so at the shrines—not so much in Rajasthan—but it is at the Shahbaz Qalandar shrine" (p. 60, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind"; refers to: the time of the Radio Pakistan interview, year dropped; informants' claim, Baloch and Mirza): Abida Parvin uses the ḍohra or bait within her narrative, a style she took from her male mentors, and "They claim that no other woman musician in the Sind has this style, which is essentially a unique male style" (p. 55, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sind", "Sind"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): "Among musician communities in the Sind, Shah's Risālo is treated with great respect because of its sacred Quranic context, and the book is not allowed to lie around on the floor" (p. 51, folio not stated); "Since large sections of the Quran are quoted in his Risālo, musicians in Sind hold Shah's written text in the utmost veneration" (p. 166, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Baluchistan"; refers to: present tense, from ethnomusicologists the author met, period not stated; author's argument reporting researchers, and an endnote citing a film): the mystical lore of "the Kacch-Rajasthan-Sind-Baluchistan continuum": "The mystical lore of the region that includes Sind, Baluchistan, and the coastal belt of Iran shares myths, melodies, and linguistic codes", among them "the famous 'Mast Qalandar' dhun" (pp. 47–48, folios not stated); the dhun is "a tune reported to be sung all along the Rajasthan, Sind, Baluchistan, and Iran continuum", and "Almost every folksinger in the region can sing it" (p. 57, folio not stated); an endnote reports that Michael Wood's film *In the Footsteps of Alexander* portrays "the Indus along the Baluchistan coast back through Persia as a route that seems to have been used for centuries" (p. 210, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Baluchistan"; refers to: the myth's origin, period not stated, and present-day singing; author's argument): the Sassi myth is "told in the lands of Sind and Baluchistan. In the oral traditions of the region the legend is sung with all the emotion that manifests Sassi's grief" (p. 118, folio not stated); "Performers love to sing the Sassi myth in the regional languages and dialects of Sind, Baluchistan, and the Siraiki-speaking regions" (p. 120, folio not stated); "the Sassi myth that originated in Baluchistan and Sind, the area currently referred to as a continuum in ethnomusicology" (p. 156, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "all the regions of the Sind"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): the Sohni myth "is sung in Sufi poetry in all the regions of the Sind, the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab" (pp. 126–127, folios not stated).
- (book's word: "northern Sind areas"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument): "In the Sufi poetry sung in the oral traditions in the Siraiki belt around the Multan, Bahawalpur, Mianwali, and northern Sind areas, the female voices are again prominent" (p. 148, folio not stated). Only the "northern Sind" part attaches here; the Punjab side is named by towns only.
- (book's word: "the Sindhi devotional contexts"; refers to: present tense; author's argument): "virāhīnī poetry is also sung in the Sindhi devotional contexts, such as the mauluds that celebrate the Prophet's birth. In these songs the devotee is a young bride or bride-to-be who awaits her bridegroom, who is equated with the prophet Muhammad" (p. 149, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhri"; refers to: centuries of invasion, "when the Mongols came—the Tartars came—the Sassanids came", spoken at the author's interview (year dropped); informant's statement of Alan Faqir, glossed by the author in endnotes): "Alan Faqir asserted in his interview about Sind: … It has been looted—it has been plundered—it has been assaulted—but it goes on—it has not died—it will not die—it has existed for centuries and it shall exist—Sindhri is beaten—and yet Sindhri goes on" (pp. 156–157, folios not stated). On his recited line "Punnu is not in the Kec / Sind is not the amir" (p. 156, folio not stated), the author's notes say "the reference is to the fact that Sind does not lead the land politically" and "'Sind is not the amir' means that Sind is not in the position of leadership or amirship"; "Sindhri" is "A name used for Sind in the poetic traditions"; and "These are all Alan Faqir's political interpretations of the female myths" (pp. 210–211, folios not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; glossary definitions): ceremonial music and song of the region: "gīc Ceremonial songs usually sung by females at births and weddings in the Sind" (p. 182, folio not stated); "jamālo A folk tune in the Sind; also, a dance" and "kapātī Cotton-spinners' songs, in Sind" (p. 183, folio not stated); "olang A ḍhol or drum rhythm played at the bridegroom's home in Sind" (p. 186, folio not stated); "sādmānā Melody played on ceremonial occasions in Sind" (p. 187, folio not stated); "candar A ceremonial Sindhi melody played on auspicious occasions" and "capr" / "ī" [text-layer?: capṛī] "Wooden castanets that are played for rhythm in Sindhi folk music" (p. 180, folio not stated); "haswārī A ceremonial Sindhi tune usually played with drums and bagpipes; Sindhi musicians claim to play it for the pirs and murshids when they appear at festive ceremonies such as a wedding" (p. 182, folio not stated); "lāon A Sindhi nuptial song" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjab and Sind"; refers to: present tense; glossary definition): "dhammāl The vigorous ritual dance performed at Sufi shrines in Panjab and Sind" (p. 180, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "a traditional Sindhi lunch"; refers to: the day of the Karachi Mori visit (year dropped); author's anecdote): "fish cooked in curry, a traditional Sindhi lunch eaten with rice and flatbread" (p. 71, folio not stated).

## From Robert Rozehnal, Islamic Sufism Unbound

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rozehnal_islamic_sufism_unbound.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: British colonial rule, as above; quoted source: Katherine Ewing): "British policy, especially in the Punjab and Sind, was to treat them in essentially the same way that landlords and tribal leaders were treated" — the hereditary saints (sajjada nishin) (p. 33, folio 23); note 10 cites Ansari, *Sufi Saints and State Power: The Pirs of Sind, 1843–1947* (p. 241, folio 234; bibliographic only).
- (book's word: "Sindhu Desh", glossed in the book "the ethnic Sindhis’ country"; refers to: period not stated — a statement of Shaykh Wahid Bakhsh, who "passed away in 1995"; quoted source: Wahid Bakhsh, in the book's translation, n. 99): "Nationalist parties are raising slogans of “Pashtunistan” [“home of the ethnic Pashtuns” ], “Azad Balochistan” [“a free home for the ethnic Baluchis”], and “Sindhu Desh” [“the ethnic Sindhis’ country”]"; "They do not realize that four smaller countries, which they wish to create, will be swallowed by India overnight." The author: "In Wahid Bakhsh’s view, the homegrown divisions of ethnic rivalry are as destructive as any external enemy" (p. 127, folio 118). The book's word is the slogan, not "Sindh"; attached because the slogan names the Sindhis' country — weigh accordingly.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, As Through a Veil

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_as_through_a_veil.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's most heavily cited Pakistani figure, appearing in ten of the sixteen chunks. He is named
throughout with a destroyed ʿayn and destroyed diacritics: `Shah c Abdul Latif`, `Shah ' Abdul Latif`,
`Shah < Abdul Latif` [all text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf], as well as "Shah Latif", "Shah ʿAbdul Latif
of Bhit", "Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit", "Abdul Latif", "the Sindhi mystic Shah ʿAbdul Latif", and — inside
Ernest Trumpp's title — "Sayyid Abdul-Latif" (pp. 11, 157–158, 166–187, 223–225, 241, 276–292, 307–311,
327–340, 349–363).

- **Saint**
  - Placed only by century and region: "Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Latīf] in
    eighteenth-century 5ind [text-layer?: Sind] teaches his disciples" (p. 158, folio 140); the same
    eighteenth-century placing is repeated at p. 171, folio 153 and p. 187, folio 169. Birth year,
    village and shrine are **(not stated)** in the body text.
  - Called "the Sindhi mystic Shah c Abdul Latif" [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf]; "the only thing
    attached to him geographically is the adjective 'Sindhi'" (p. 241, folio 223).
  - Kinship: he is the great-grandson of Shah ʿAbdul Karim of Bulrri [text-layer?: Bulri] — the mystical
    use of Sindhi folk tales "reach full bloom in the Risiilo [text-layer?: Risālō] of his
    great-grandson, Shah c Abdul Latif of Bhit in the eighteenth century" (p. 171, folio 153).
  - **Death year — the book's two registers disagree in kind, keep both.** The Index of Proper Names
    prints the headword `c Abdul Latif, Shah (d . 1752)` [text-layer?: `ʿAbdul Laṭīf, Shāh`], with the
    work `Risal6` [text-layer?: `Risālo`] as a sub-entry under the name (p. 349, folio not stated).
    The body text gives **no death year anywhere**: Pass-1 workers record "no death year is stated"
    (p. 171, folio 153) and "no shrine, tomb, ʿurs, custodian or death date for him appears anywhere
    in this chunk: **(not stated)**" (pp. 223–225, 241, folios 205–207, 223). The chunk-001 material
    likewise gives "no dates for the poet, and no silsila" (p. 11, folio not stated).
  - No silsila is given anywhere (p. 11, folio not stated; p. 241, folio 223).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Not stated.** The book never describes a tomb, shrine, ʿurs, custodian or endowment for him
    (explicitly noted at pp. 223–225, 241, folios 205–207, 223; also pp. 11, 276–280, folios not
    stated / 258–262).
  - "Bhit" — this row's own toponym — occurs in the book only inside his epithet, "Shah c Abdul Latif
    of Bhit" (p. 171, folio 153), and inside the titles of two studies: "Henry T. Sorley, Shah Abdul
    Latif of Bhit, His Poetry, Life and Times" (p. 283, folio 265) and "Sorley, Herbert T. Shah Abdul
    Latif of Bhit. London: Oxford University Press, t940 [text-layer?: 1940]; repr. 1966." (p. 340,
    folio 322). A Pass-1 worker marks the second: **"'Bhit' is the toponym of this row"**.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - He stands in the book's list of saints attached to the alif legend: "The story that this or that
    saint was acquainted only with the letter alif, the first letter of the Arabic alphabet and cipher
    for Allah, is commonplace in the legends of Muslim saints from Turkey to India (Yunus Emre, Shah c
    Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Latīf], Bullhe Shah, Bedil Rohriwaro, etc.)" (p. 157,
    folio 139). The teaching quoted from him is the alif verse itself: "Those who remember the line
    which begins with alif: / 'There is no goal but Him in both worlds,' [that is what] they think"
    (p. 158, folio 140).
- **Significance**
  - *European reception.* "And some forty years later (1867) Ernest Trumpp made some poisonous remarks
    about one of the masterpieces of classical Sindhi poetry, Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shah
    ʿAbdul Laṭīf] 's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], which he edited with great pains although he
    thoroughly disliked anything that smacked of mysticism." (p. 11, folio not stated; the date `1867`
    is quoted as printed — four-digit years survive intact in this book). "Yet even he could not help
    admitting that some of Shah c Abdul Latif's verses were worth reading." (p. 11, folio not stated),
    which is Schimmel's judgement on Trumpp, not on the poet. She returns to Trumpp at p. 283,
    folio 265: "The overcritical approach of the German missionary-philologist to Sindhi mystical
    poetry is due partly to his 'protestant,' violently antimystical attitude, partly also to the fact
    that the texts which he collected were hopelessly confused."
  - *Folk-tale method.* He is the source of Sindhi folk-poetry motifs "beginning at least from Shah c
    Abdul Latif's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō]" (p. 166, folio 148). "Since the original tales were
    known to everyone, he usually begins his poetical renderings with the most dramatic moment in order
    to describe, with remarkable psychological insight, the moods of the soul on its way to the
    beloved." (p. 171, folio 153)
  - *The woman-souls.* **Sohni**, who "swims every night through the Indus to meet her beloved on an
    island, until the jar which she uses as a kind of life-vest is shattered and she is drowned"
    (p. 171, folio 153); **Sassui**, who follows "her beloved Punhun through burning deserts and over
    terrifying hills", loses him during the "sleep of heedlessness" and "is completely transformed into
    Love" (pp. 171–172, folios 153–154); **Marui**, "the ideal soul", "Captured from her native village
    by the powerful c omar [text-layer?: ʿUmar] of Omar-kot" (p. 172, folio 154); and **Nuri**, "a
    historical figure, the consort of the Sindhi ruler Jam Tamachi in the fifteenth century", "though a
    simple fishermaid" (p. 172, folio 154).
  - *Gendered soul.* "Shah c Abdul Latif applies to one of his heroines the famous saying of the
    thirteenth century Chishti saint, Jamaluddin Hanswi: talib al-maula mudhakkar . . . , 'He who seeks
    the Lord is male . . . .'" (p. 173, folio 155). His heroines "who write with tears of blood (Ma-rui
    II, v. 4) would like to decorate with golden threads the wings of the messenger crow that would
    bring news from the beloved (Sur Purab I, v. 8)" (p. 284, folio 266). He "expresses Ruzbihan-i
    Baqli's ideal: the lover becomes completely transformed into Love" (p. 283, folio 265), with the
    verses "0 voice in the desert, as though it were a wild goose" and "It is the song of Love-only
    common people thought it / To be a woman's voice" (p. 284, folio 266).
  - *Music.* "The musical imagery in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō] culminates in
    King Dhyaj's [text-layer?: name uncertain] offering his head to the minstrel whose ravishing
    melodies had kindled his love and made him forsake honor and life." (p. 169, folio 151). "The
    admittedly very cruel Hindu story of Sorathi and King Dhyaj, located originally in Svar-ashtra
    [text-layer?: Saurashtra — not certain, see Doubt], becomes for Shah Latif an allegory of the
    absolute power of the mystical guide for whose sweet songs the initiate gladly sacrifices
    everything, including his life." (p. 283, folio 265). "Shah c Abdul Latif praises also the
    instruments of the Yogis for their bewitching power (Sur Ramakali)." (p. 283, folio 265). `Sorathi`
    stands as its own headword in the Index of Proper Names (p. 362, folio 344).
  - *Tree image.* Named among "popular Sufi poets-Shah Latif in Sind as much as the Anatolian minstrel
    Yunus Emre-[who] see the unbeliever or the ungrateful person as a dried-up tree which cannot be
    moved by the breeze of Divine kindness and should be cut off and burned (a subtle allusion to Sura
    111)" (p. 160, folio 142).
  - *Unity of being.* Set beside Yunus Emre at a chapter's close: "Shah c Abdul Latif in
    eighteenth-century Sind knows that: hika qa:iaru dara lak'ha [text-layer?: transliteration damaged]
    / One castle, a hundred thousand doors, / Windows without number- / Wherever I look, there the
    Beloved confronts me." (p. 187, folio 169)
  - *Prophetic devotion.* "The great mystical poets of Sind have devoted wonderful songs to the Prophet,
    who appears as the dawn between the night of created human life and the Divine day" — the named
    example that follows is Shah ʿAbdul Latif (p. 223, folio 205). "The lyrical parts at the end of many
    chapters in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālō] speak of the poet's hope for
    Muhammad's intercession" (p. 223, folio 205). "The most beautiful example among them is Sur Siirang
    [text-layer?: Sur Sārang], in which the poet dramatically describes the longing of all creatures for
    rain" (p. 223, folio 205), the sentence completing at p. 224, folio 206: "rain brings fertility to
    the parched soil, milk to the cows, bread to the poor". "Out of this description Shah Latif turns to
    a short, touching song in which the Prophet, once more the cloud of mercy, is praised because he
    will protect his community at Doomsday" (p. 224, folio 206). "And poets who followed Shah c Abdul
    Latif's example saw this blissful rain cloud extending not only from Istanbul to Jaisalmer, from
    Delhi to Omarkot but from Kairouan to Rohri and from England to China" (p. 224, folio 206).
  - *Reception by Hindu commentators.* Schimmel states that "the Hindu commentators on Shah c Abdul
    Latif's poetry failed to understand the importance of the Prophet (the same i s true for Panjabi
    poetry)" (p. 308, folio 290). She names "Lilaram Watanmal, the author o f the first major biography
    of Shah ' Abdul Latif (Hyderabad/Sind, 1889)", quotes him at length, and closes: "These patronizing
    remarks (pp. 37-38) are certainly out of place." (p. 308, folio 290). Watanmal is quoted as writing
    that "some of the verses in praise of the prophet, somewhat vulgar in language, are not our poet's .
    . . . But it cannot be doubted that there are several genuine verses in which our poet has expressed
    his full belief in the prophet." (p. 308, folio 290), and further: "It may be that Shah Latif wanted
    to lead the minds of his followers slowly and gradually into higher Sufism by allowing them to
    believe first in their prophet, and then by degrees to ascend higher and higher." (p. 308,
    folio 290).
  - *Formal firsts.* "the first known example in Sin-dhi occurs in Shah ' Abdul Latif, Risalo, Sur
    Ramakali" — said of the golden-alphabet form, the `Tih ak'haryun` edited by "Dr. N. A. Baloch"
    (p. 281, folio 263). "the first known example is again in Shah c Abdul Latif's Risalo, Sur Ramakali"
    — said of poems enumerating the days of a week or of a month (p. 282, folio 264).
  - *Marthiya.* "Shah c Abdul Latif, in Sur Kedar6 [text-layer?: Sur Kedāro] VI, vv. 10 and 11, takes up
    the motif of nuptials for the death of the martyrs", with the quoted line "The houris wind rose
    chains; flower decorations for the martyrs!" (p. 285, folio 267).
  - *Symbolic geography.* "With Shah c Abdul Latif, pfirab [text-layer?: pūrab] always denotes the
    'mystical' East, the home of the soul" (p. 288, folio 270).
  - *Transmission from Rumi.* Note 418 to chapter 3: "M I 1704; the verse was translated verbatim into
    Sindhi poetry by Shah ' Abdul Latif, Risa/6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Sassui Abri I, 8. See also
    D 445/4691" (p. 276, folio 258).
  - *Satan as true monotheist.* "the Sindhi mystic Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf]
    calls Satan 'Lover': cashiq c Azazil [text-layer?: ʿāshiq ʿAzāzīl], Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālo],
    Sur Yaman Kalyan V 24" (p. 241, folio 223). He is named third in a series of three holders of that
    view, after "Ahmad Ghaz­zali's remark, 'Who does not learn tauhid from Satan, is an infidel'" and
    "similar remarks … made by the Persian poet Sarmad at the Moghul Court"; the note frames all three
    against "Ritter, Meer der Seele" for the claim that in "Hallaj's and his followers' view Satan
    'becomes more monotheist than God Himself.'" (p. 241, folio 223).
  - *Other citations of the Risalo by sur and verse* (the numerals are internal locators of the cited
    work, not pages of this book): `Ristil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Yaman Kalyan V, verse 2i
    [text-layer?: verse 21]` (p. 279, folio 261); `Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Dahar` and
    `Risiil6 [text-layer?: Risālō], Sur Ripa II, v. 8.9` (p. 280, folio 262); "Shah ' Abdul Latif,
    Risalo, Sur Barvo Sindhi II, v. 1 i . [text-layer?: v. 11]" and "Shah c Abdul Latif, Risdl6
    [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Kapa'iti I, v . 4 ." (p. 281, folio 263); "Risa/a [text-layer?: Risālo],
    Sur Ripa II, vv . 13-15" and "Risii/6 [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Sohni I, vv 17, 18" (p. 284,
    folio 266); "Ristil6 [text-layer?: Risālo], Sur Kalyan, I v. 20" (p. 292, folio 274); "Thus `S/zii/z
    j6 Risiil6` [text-layer?: Shāh jo Risālo], Sur Dahar II, 1-3", cited in note 169 for three
    invocations of the Prophet as lord of Medina (p. 307, folio 289). Note 20 to chapter 1 cites "a
    Sindhi poet [who] admonishes his listeners to tum [text-layer?: turn] 'to the end of The Afternoon,
    al-'aiir' [text-layer?: al-ʿaṣr]", glossed as the last word of "the Surat al-'a$r (Sura 103), which
    is $abr, 'patience.'", the reference printed "(Shah c Abdul Latif, Shiih jo Risiilo [text-layer?:
    Shāh jo Risālō], Sur Kalyan III 8)" (p. 237, folio 219).
  - *Cross-reference in the book's own apparatus.* Note 169 continues "For details see Schimmel, 'The
    Veneration of the Prophet Muhammad, in Sin­dhi,' p. 134 ff." (p. 308, folio 290). The title also
    stands as its own index headword with a cross-reference: `Risalo, see ' Abdul Latif` (p. 363,
    folio 345).
  - *Editions and studies the book prints.* Bibliography, as printed: "c Abdul Latif Bhita'i, Shah. Shah
    jo risiilo, ed. Kalyan B . Adwani. Bombay: Hindustan Kitabghar, 1958." (p. 311, folio not stated) —
    the editor is "Kalyan B . Adwani", the publisher "Hindustan Kitabghar" of Bombay, 1958 (p. 311,
    folio not stated). "Jotwani, Motilal. … Shah Abdul Latif, His Life and Work. University of Delhi,
    1975." (p. 327, folio 309). "Kazi, Elsa. Risalo of Abdul Latif. Selections. Hyderabad/Sind: Sindhi
    Adabi Board, 1965 ." (p. 327, folio 309). "Rashdi, Sayyid Hussamuddin." … "'Shah kafia jo miijid na
    ahe.' Mehran jufi maujufi [text-layer?: Mehrān jā maujā]. Karachi: Sindhi Adabi Board, 1956. ('Shah
    c Abdul Latif did not invent the kaff [text-layer?: kāfī],' see pp. 215-24 .)" — Schimmel supplies
    the English gloss of the Sindhi title herself (p. 334, folio 316). "Watanmal, Lilaram. The Life of
    Shah Abdullatif. Hyderabad/Sind, 1889." (p. 343, folio 325), that author himself indexed as
    "Watanmal, Lilaram (d. after 1900)" (p. 364, folio 346). Three studies are listed together at
    p. 283, folio 265: "Henry T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit, His Poetry, Life and Times; Motilal
    Jotwani, Shah Abdul Latif, His Life and Work; Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace, Part II."
- **Doubt:** the chunk-001 material is an **author-only mention** — he is "named twice on one page, only
  as the author of a Sindhi classic, in the course of Schimmel's account of European scholarship on
  mystical poetry", with no epithet (`Bhittai` does not appear), no shrine, no tomb, no site, no dates
  and no silsila; the `Risalo` is called "one of the masterpieces of classical Sindhi poetry" and nothing
  more (p. 11, folio not stated). The Pass-1 worker raised the identification question explicitly. The
  mapping rests on the 18 September 2026 person rule.
- **Doubt:** the title of his work prints **four different ways in four citations in chunk 011 alone**
  (`Risa/6`, `Ristil6`, `Risii/6` at pp. 276–280, folios 258–262) and several more ways elsewhere
  (`Risalo`, `Risdl6`, `Risa/a`, `Shah jo risiilo`, `S/zii/z j6 Risiil6`, `Shiih jo Risiilo`,
  pp. 281–311, folios 263–289). Pass-1 workers flagged this and did **not** unify it (BRIEF §7).
- **Doubt:** "Svar-ashtra [text-layer?: Saurashtra]" is explicitly **not certain** (p. 283, folio 265).
- **Doubt:** the chunk-002 Pass-1 bullet heads him as "the book's 'Shah c Abdul Latif [text-layer?: Shah
  ʿAbdul Latif] in Sind'" but carries **no facts and no page reference** — the bullet is empty as
  transmitted. A human should check whether chunk 002 in fact held material that was lost.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Shah Abdul Latif's death year appears in the index and nowhere in the body.** The index prints
  `c Abdul Latif, Shah (d . 1752)` (p. 349, folio not stated), but every chunk of body text and
  notes that names him records the death year as **(not stated)** — he is only "eighteenth-century"
  (p. 158, folio 140), "of Bhit in the eighteenth century" (p. 171, folio 153), and unfixed in the
  Prophet chapter and the chapter-5 notes (p. 223, folio 205; p. 308, folio 290). The 1752 rests on
  the index alone.

- **Names that could match two rows.** "Abdul Latif": `bhit-bhit-shah` against `bari-imam` (Sayyid
  Abdul Latif Kazmi) — resolved once on "Shah" plus "in Sind" (p. 43, folio 33) and once on the date
  plus the `Risal6` sub-entry (p. 349, folio not stated), but the bare name reads either way. "Shah
  Inayat": `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` against `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri`, settled by
  the book's own "of Jhok" and 1718 (p. 175, folio 157; p. 285, folio 267; p. 338, folio 320).
  "Shams" is refused for `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari`, `shamsabad` and `sial-sharif`
  wherever it is Rumi's companion (p. 118, folio 108; p. 143, folio 125; p. 365, folio not stated;
  p. 376, folio 358) — and, separately, the book **does not identify** "Shams Tabriz of Multan"
  (p. 285, folio 267; p. 326, folio 308) or the venerated Multan shrine of "Shams-i Tabriz" (p. 175,
  folio 157) with any archive figure; `shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari` sits in the Multan head
  **because of the place only**, and the distinction must not be collapsed. "Omarkot" →
  `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir` on spelling alone (p. 224, folio 206; p. 359, folio 341), and was
  refused elsewhere (p. 172, folio 154). "Rohri": no archive row is *in* Rohri, both candidates name
  it as adjacent, and the chunks split between parking it (p. 224, folio 206) and heading it (p. 361,
  folio 343). "Sehwan": taken as the saint's epithet in one chunk, claiming only `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`
  (p. 287, folio 269), and as a bare toponym in another, adding `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`
  (p. 361, folio 343). "Hyderabad": the book prints "Hyderabad/Sind", "Hyderabad/Deccan", "Hyderabad
  AP" and one bare, undisambiguated "Hyderabad" (p. 301, folio 283; p. 321, folio 303; p. 355,
  folio 337) — only the first carries `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat`. "Hinglaj" is headed with
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` only; `chandragup-baba-chandragup` (Hingol National Park) is
  the other candidate and is not claimed (p. 354, folio 336). A bare "Zakariya" occurs twice and must
  not be merged with the Multan saint: the prophet in al-Kisāʾī (p. 285, folio 267) and a bare index
  headword (p. 363, folio 345), against "Baha'uddin Zakariya Multani" (p. 286, folio 268; p. 351,
  folio 333).

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after Bada'uni, no dates given; book's historical prose): mystical poets "in Sind and the Punjab" developed "the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the first half of the sixteenth century" (Qadi Qadan) to "the eighteenth century" (Shah 'Abdul Latif), as printed; book's historical prose): "In Sind, the range of folk tales utilized for mystical purposes is even wider." Early allusions appear "however veiled, in Qadi Qadan's verse"; they "become more obvious in Shah 'Abdul Karim of Bulrri's [text-layer?] short verses, and reach full bloom in the Risiilo [text-layer?] [Risālo] of his great-grandson, Shah 'Abdul Latif of Bhit"; "Since the original tales were known to everyone, he usually begins his poetical renderings with the most dramatic moment" (p. 171, folio 153).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "shortly after 900", as printed; book's historical prose): Sind is "the country which he [Hallaj] traversed shortly after 900 to call people to God" (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote): "It is moving to listen to illiterate folk musicians in remote corners of Sind ... when they repeat his name [Hallaj's] in ecstatic songs, and claim that he was the true lover who paid with his life for his love" (the same sentence names "qawwals in the dargah of Gulbarga"; that part is not Sind) (p. 175, folio 157).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated, "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): poets "in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him [the Prophet] into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "The great mystical poets of Sind have devoted wonderful songs to the Prophet, who appears as the dawn between the night of created human life and the Divine day" (p. 223, folio 205).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the author's own time, date not stated; author's anecdote in endnote 48): "Incidentally, I have learned more about Islamic piety from women in Anatolia and Sind than from most of my learned colleagues or from sophisticated books—they still cling to 'the faith of the old women' as it was praised in early Islam, and represent the values of a moderately mystical Islam" (p. 281, folio 263).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: present tense, and "in more recent times" for the Pir Pagaro verses; author's argument in endnote 109, citing Annemarie Schimmel, "Hochzeitslieder der Frauen im Industal"): "in Sind, where the women often use endearing diminutives (Piral, Ghauthal [text-layer?]) in addressing the saints, we find poems in honor of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan ... and, in more recent times, verses devoted to the Pir Pagaro" (p. 287, folio 269). Only the region-wide part (the women's diminutives and the existence of saint-poems in Sind) belongs here. The Lal Shahbaz/Sehwan detail ("often implored when a woman wants a son") is a site statement under ruling (a).

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Mystical Dimensions of Islam

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_mystical_dimensions_of_islam.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The most heavily cited Pakistani figure in the book, named in thirteen of the twenty-nine chunks.
His name is printed at least fourteen ways: `Shah cAbdul Latlt`, `Shah c Abdu D l-Latif`,
`Shah cAbduDl-Latif`, `Shah cAbduDl-LatIf`, `Shah cAbdu3l-Latif`, `Shah ^bdu^l-Latif`,
`Shah 'vAbdu^l-Latlf`, `Shah "vAbdu^l-Latif`, `Shah 'Abdu'l-Latif`, `Shah "Abdu'l-Latif`,
`Shah Abdu^l-Latif of Sind`, `Shah Latif`, `Schah Abdul Latif`, `Shah CAbdul Latif Bhitefi`, and —
inside Ernst Trumpp's title — `Sayyid c Abd-ul-Latif` [all text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf]; six of
those renderings fall within the five pages 420–424 alone (pp. 49, 144, 200, 216, 226, 244, 248,
265, 331, 358, 414, 418–424, 450, 482–496, 510, 525).

- **Saint**
  - Birth, with place and year: `Shah ^bdu^l-Latif was born into a family of mystics in 1689 in Hala,
    near present-day Hyderabad-Sind,` (p. 421, folio 390). `1689` prints cleanly in four digits.
  - **Two registers of his dates, both kept.** The running text gives `Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit
    (1689-1752)` (p. 414, folio 383) and, separately, `His younger contemporary, Shah cAbduDl-Latif
    in Sind (d. 1752)` — `His` referring to `Sultan Bahu of the Punjab`, so the book places him as
    Sultan Bahu's junior contemporary (p. 200, folio 169). The `INDEX OF NAMES AND PLACES` prints the
    headword `Abdu3l-Lat!f Bhita3!, Shah (d. 1752),` [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf Bhitāʾī, Shāh], with
    a detached ʿayn as a bare `c` on the line above (p. 510, folio 479). Elsewhere the book dates him
    only by century: `the eighteenth-century mystic` (p. 226, folio 195), `in the eighteenth century`
    (p. 265, folio 234), `the eighteenth-century poet` (p. 358, folio 327), `in Sind in the eighteenth
    century` (p. 171 material not in these notes — see p. 414, folio 383).
  - Ancestry: he is named as the descendant of `Shah cAbduDl-Karim`, whose poems are `closely related
    to Latif's poetry` (pp. 420–421, folios 389–390) — see **shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri**.
  - Wandering and the Hinglaj journey, with Schimmel's own hedge kept: `In his early years Latif
    wandered through the country in the company of a group of yogis and perhaps reached the sacred
    mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan, which is often mentioned as "the goal" in Sindhi mystical
    poetry.` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - Settlement: `He then settled down in Bhit, not far from his hometown, and gathered a number of
    disciples around him.` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - **No silsila is given anywhere in the book** — `(not stated)` at pp. 49, 144, 200, 226, 248, 265,
    331, 358, 414–424, 450, 494, 510.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The **one** description of the tomb, and it is a craft attribution rather than a building
    account: the tomb is `covered with blue and white tiles (typical of Hala)` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - Beyond that sentence the book gives **no** ʿurs, **no** custodian, **no** endowment, **no**
    building date and **no** visitor number — explicitly `(not stated)` at pp. 144, 200, 216, 226,
    248, 265, 331, 358, 414–424, 450, 494, 510.
  - `Bhit` reaches most chunks only through apparatus, inside the title of a secondary study:
    `H. T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life, and Times (1940; reprint ed., Oxford,
    1966).` (p. 216, folio 185, footnote 94; again p. 244, folio 213; and as the bibliography entry
    `Sorley, Herbert Tower. Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life, and` / `Times. 1940. Reprint.
    Oxford, 1966.`, p. 495, folio 464). `1940` and `1966` print cleanly.
  - The index carries a **separate bare place entry** `Bhit Shah, 383, 390` (p. 513, folio 482). It
    is recorded and was **not** used to fire ruling (a) — the row is reached by the person rule
    instead. See `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - The epithet `Bhittai` is never used in running text; it appears only inside the footnoted title
    `Makhzan Shah ^Abdul Latlf Bhita?i` [text-layer?: Makhzan Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf Bhitāʾī] (p. 450,
    folio 419) and in the index headword (p. 510, folio 479).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **He recognises the boy Sachal**, and Schimmel undercuts the story in the same breath: `There is
    a story that once when a boy came to his convent, the mystic immediately recognized that he would
    one day "open the lid from those things" that he had kept secret.` — followed by `The anecdote
    may have been derived from the pen name Sachal used in his Persian poetry, namely, Ashikdr
    [text-layer?: Āshikār], "Open."` (p. 424, folio 393). The pairing is the material and should not
    be split.
  - **The Ḥallājian vision of the vegetable world**: `seeing in every tree and plant the vision of
    the suffering mystic, proclaiming anffl-haqq and therefore doomed to death` (p. 427, folio 306 as
    printed — the `folio = PDF − 31` rule gives 396).
- **Significance**
  - Schimmel's ranking: `The most outstanding master of popular Sufi poetry in Pakistan is, no doubt,
    Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit. He is not the first in the long line of Sindhi mystical poets.`
    (p. 420, folio 389).
  - *Sufi / Kufi.* `still in eighteenth-century Sindhi mystical poetry the Sufi is called Id-kufl`
    [text-layer?: lā-kūfī]`, "non-Kufi," i.e., not bound to a particular religious rite.` (p. 49,
    folio 18), footnoted to Schimmel's own `"Shah cAbdul Latlt's Beschreibung des wahren Sufi," in
    Festschrift fur Fritz Meier, comp. Richard Gramlich (Wiesbaden, 1974` [text-layer?: undamaged]`).`
    (p. 49, folio 18).
  - *The camel and the nafs.* `it is only natural that poets all over the desert areas, like Shah c
    Abdu D l-Latif in Sind, are particularly fond of this comparison.` (p. 144, folio 113).
  - *The dry tree.* `devoted a whole chapter of his poetical work to the dry tree that needs the
    water of recollection to be revived.` (p. 200, folio 169). The chapter is not named —
    `(not stated)`.
  - *Iblīs as the one true lover.* `Even the poetry of the eighteenth-century mystic Shah
    cAbduDl-Latif in the remote province of Sind calls the reader to admire Iblis as the one true
    lover and to follow his example—a verse that has caused considerable puzzlement to the
    commentators.` (p. 226, folio 195), localised by footnote 6 to `Sur Yaman Kalyan` (p. 226,
    folio 195). The `°` of `Schah °Abdul Latif's` in that footnote is an **ʿayn**, not a digit.
  - *The Prophet as rain-cloud, twice.* `he may be compared, as Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Sind has done,
    to the cloud that sends down the life-giving rain to the thirsty fields, quickening what seemed
    to be dead.` (p. 248, folio 217); and `the poetry of Shah cAbdu3l-Latif contains a fine
    description of the Prophet through the image of the rain cloud, the symbol of divine mercy, whose
    help is implored for the Day of Judgment.` (p. 418, folio 387).
  - *Against the fetish of dervish dress.* `And in the eighteenth century Shah Abdu^l-Latif of Sind
    advised the true Sufi to throw his cap into the fire instead of boasting with it.` (p. 265,
    folio 234) — the sentence closes Schimmel's paragraph on the *tāj* and *khirqa*.
  - *The desert heroine's cry*, with the verse quoted in full: `O voice in the desert—as if the
    grouse were crying . . . / cry from the water's depths—it is the sigh of love. / O voice in the
    desert, like sound of a violin- / It is the song of love—only the people unaware took it / to be
    a woman's song.` (p. 331, folio 300), sourced by footnote 15 to `Shah c Abdu D l-Latif, "Sur
    Macdhurl," in Shdha jo risdld, ed. Kalyan B. Adwani (Bombay, 1958), i:lines 21-22.`
    [text-layer?: Sur Maʿdhūrī; Shāha jo risālo; `i:lines` = 1:lines] (p. 331, folio 300). Schimmel's
    gloss: `This mystery of transformation from chaste human love to the summit of divine love remains
    one of the central themes of great poetry in Iran and its neighboring countries` (p. 331,
    folio 300).
  - *Rūmī in his verse.* `Quotations from Rumi permeate the Sindhi verses of the eighteenth-century
    poet Shah cAbduDl-LatIf, and it would be easy to draw up an inventory of all the verses used in
    Indo-Pakistani literature written in the vernaculars that are inspired by the great mystic's
    work.` (p. 358, folio 327).
  - *Letter symbolism.* Quoted for a couplet on mīm and alif, introduced `says Shah ^bdu^l-Latif`:
    `Put a mlm` [text-layer?: mīm] `into your soul, / and an alif in front of it` (p. 450, folio 419),
    sourced to `Shah jo risdlo` [text-layer?: Shāh jo Risālo] and to Schimmel's Festschrift article
    (p. 450, folio 419). His name also carries the footnote for the alif-numerology of an unnamed
    `Sindhi Shia author` (p. 449, folio 418), cited to `Makhzan Shah ^Abdul Latlf Bhita?i` — **the
    chunk does not say whether that work is by him or about him** — (not stated) (p. 450, folio 419;
    and as a title-only bibliography entry, `Makhzan Shah CAbdul Latif Bhitefi. Hyderabad, Sind, 1954.
    In Sindhi.`, p. 485, folio 454).
  - *Generation.* Named as Bulleh Shah's Sindhi contemporary (p. 419, folio 388 — quoted under
    **mazar-of-bulleh-shah**).
  - *Works and reception, as the bibliography prints them.* `Shah c Abdul Latlf. Risdlo. Edited by
    Kalyan B. Adwani. Bombay, 195` / `In Sindhi.` — **the year is cut short at the right margin as
    `195` and the sentence-final period is missing; it is not completed** (p. 494, folio 463);
    `Kazi, Elsa, trans. "Risalo" of Shah Abdul Latif. Hyderabad, Sind, 1965.` (p. 483, folio 452);
    `Jotwani, Motilal. "Shah Latif: Man and Poet." Indian Literatures 6, no. 2 (1963).` (p. 482,
    folio 451); `Trumpp, Ernst. "Sorathi. Ein Sindhi-Gedichtaus dem grossen Divan des Sayyid` / `c` /
    `Abd-ul-Latif." Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenlandischen Gesell-` / `schaft 17 (1863).` —
    `Sindhi-Gedichtaus` printed without the space, the detached `c` an ʿayn (p. 496, folio 465);
    Schimmel, `"Schah Abdul Latif." Kairos, nos. 3-4 (1961).` and `"Shah cAbdul Latif's Beschreibung
    des wahren Sufi." In Fest-` / `schrift fur Fritz Meier, compiled by Richard Gramlich. Wiesbaden,`
    / `J` / `974-` — the year broken across two lines as `J` + `974-` (p. 493, folio 462). Also
    `An Urdu verse translation was made by Sheikh Ayaz (Hyderabad, Sind, 1963)` and `Elsa Kazi,
    Risalo of Shah Abdul Latif (Hyderabad, Sind, 1965)` (pp. 421–422, folios 390–391, footnote 75).
  - *Index apparatus.* The main entry's locator, quoted verbatim as the book's own printed folios:
    `113. l69- 195. 217, 234- 300, 327, 383, 387, 388, 389-93, 396, 397, 419` [text-layer?: 113, 169,
    195, 217, 234, 300, 327, 383, 387, 388, 389–93, 396, 397, 419] (p. 510, folio 479), with the
    sub-entry `Risdlo,` [text-layer?: Risālo] at `390-92` (p. 510, folio 479); the work is indexed
    again as `Shaha jo risalo ( c Abdu D l-Latif), 390-92` (p. 525, folio 494); and he stands in the
    cross-reference block `Shah. See cAbdu3l-Karim Bulrriwaro; cAbdu'l-Latif Bhita3!, Shah; c Abu
    cAli Qalandar, Shah; c lnayat Shahid, Shah; Ismail Shahid, Shah` (p. 525, folio 494). **These are
    the book's own index locators — printed folios, not PDF pages, and not converted.**
  - **A saying that must NOT be read as his.** The maxim closing "Saints and Miracles" — `It is
    better to restore one dead heart to eternal life than to restore to life a thousand dead bodies.`
    (p. 244, folio 213) — is introduced only as `the saint was right who said`, and its footnote gives
    Sorley's page, not an attribution. Schimmel does not say whose saying it is.
  - Earliest mention in the book is a bare footnote: footnote 23 at p. 49, folio 18, citing Schimmel's
    own article and nothing else.
  - The printed death date `(d. 1752)` is **identical to the one the index gives two pages later for
    `Bullhe Shah`** (p. 510, folio 479; p. 513, folio 482) — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Bullhe Shah's `1752` is suspect on its face.** Two pages away in the same index,
  `Abdu3l-Lat!f Bhita3!, Shah` is printed `(d. 1752)` — **the identical year** — in a chunk where
  chunk 027 measures **`!` standing for `1` ten times**. The archive's Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf row
  (**bhit-bhit-shah**) does carry 1752, so one of the two readings is right and the other may be a
  digit that migrated. Do not let `1752` into a Bulleh Shah entry without the page image.

**Two agreements, recorded for contrast so a reviewer knows the book is not simply wrong.**
`lnayat Shahid, Shah (d. 1718)` (p. 517, folio 486) matches **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**'s
1718; `Shah cAbduDl-Latif in Sind (d. 1752)` (p. 200, folio 169) matches **bhit-bhit-shah**'s 1752 —
with the Bullhe Shah caveat above attached.

- **bhit-bhit-shah** — in chunk 011 it rests on a two-step identification the human should ratify: the text names `Shah cAbduDl-Latif in Sind (d. 1752)` (p. 200, folio 169) and a footnote sixteen pages later cites `H. T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit` (p. 216, folio 185). *"Nothing in the chunk explicitly says that these two are the same man… the agreement is what makes the identification look obvious."*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "711" and "present Pakistan"; book's historical prose): in 711 the Arabs "also reached Sind, the lower Indus valley, and laid the foundation of a Muslim rule that still continues in present Pakistan" (p. 61, folio 30; header printed `go` [text-layer?: 30]).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "711" and "the early centuries"; book's historical prose): the western provinces of the Subcontinent "had become part of the Muslim Empire in 711, the year in which the Arabs conquered Sind and the adjacent provinces northward up to Multan"; "The Muslim pious in these areas were, in the early centuries, apparently interested mainly in the collection of hadith and in the transmission to the central Muslim countries of scientific information from India" (p. 375, folio 344, printed at the foot of the chapter-opening page).
- (book's word: "Sind", "this province", "northern Sind"; refers to: Hallaj's journey "in 905", with "since 711" and "later centuries"; book's historical prose): "From Gujerat he wandered through Sind, the lower Indus valley, which had been a part of the Muslim Empire since 711. The seeds he sowed there grew in later centuries in the mystical poetry of this province"; the Carmathians "ruled not only Bahrain but also northern Sind and Multan" (p. 98, folio 67). Again: "In 905, a mystic like Hallaj traveled extensively throughout Sind and probably discussed theological problems with the sages of this country" (p. 376, folio 345).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated, in the discussion of Bayezid (d. 874); author's argument): on the claim that Bayezid's master was "Abu CAH [text-layer?: ʿAlī] as-Sindi, i.e., from Sind", even if he came "from the lower Indus valley and not from a village called Sind close to Bistam", "not every man from Sind could be expected to know all the intricacies of Hindu monistic philosophy" (p. 78, folio 47). The book itself raises a second "Sind" near Bistam; see sweep notes.
