- (book's word: "the remote province of Sind"; refers to: the eighteenth century, Shah ʿAbduʾl-Latif's time; author's argument): "Even the poetry of the eighteenth-century mystic Shah cAbduDl-Latif in the remote province of Sind calls the reader to admire Iblis as the one true lover" (p. 226, folio 195; header printed `1Q5` [text-layer?: 195]).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Sir Richard Burton, writing about 1850", and "Old Sindhi songs", undated; quoted source, Burton, *Sind and the Races That Inhabit the Valley of the Indus* (London, 1851), p. 177, as the footnote gives it): "Old Sindhi songs describe how his [ʿAbduʾl-Qadir Gilani's] spiritual realm extends from Istanbul to Delhi"; Burton "says that there were about a hundred large trees in Sind, all called after Gilani. Each had a pole and a flag hung upon it to fulfill some vow made in adversity or sickness. Sweetmeats were distributed to the poor in hono [text-layer?: honor, cut short at the margin] of the saint, and the fruit and leaves of the trees were not allowed to be touched even by cattle"; "Proper names like Ghauth Bakhsh, 'gift of the help,' in Sind and Balochistan point to his popularity" (p. 279, folio 248).
- (book's word: "Sind", "other provinces"; refers to: period not stated ("It is said"); reported tradition, author's prose): "It is said that some mystics in Sind—and it may be true for other provinces as well-kept only three books for spiritual nourishment: the Koran, the Mathnawi, and the Diwdn [text-layer?: Dīwān] of Hafiz" (p. 358, folio 327). Same page: the Mogul emperors "were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab" (see `punjab`).
- (book's word: "Sind, to the lower Indus valley"; refers to: present day, "almost as popular today as they were 150 years ago"; ethnographic present): the visitor who goes on "to Sind, to the lower Indus valley, ... will enjoy listening to the moving mystical songs of Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit (1689-1752) or the ardent poems of Sachal Sarmast (d. 1826)" (p. 414, folio 383).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi tradition"; refers to: "Also in the sixteenth century" and "From that time on"; book's historical prose): "a group of mystics migrated from Sind to Burhanpur in central India, where they founded a new branch of the Qadiriyya tariqa; they are reported to have used Sindhi verses during their samcf [text-layer?: samāʿ] sessions. From that time on the indigenous Sindhi tradition contains innumerable stories of saints, mainly of those who are buried on Makli Hill near Thatta" (p. 415, folio 384). The same page gives the fifteenth-century date for native-language mystical poetry "both in Sind and in the Punjab" (see `punjab`).
- (book's word: "Sind", "both provinces"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): the Punjab–Sind similarity of mystical literature, the spinning-and-weaving motif "in a cotton-growing country", and the hymns to Gilani, as under `punjab` (pp. 416–417, folios 385–386); "One of the first 'geographical' poems in Sindhi was composed in his honor" (p. 417, folio 386).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Shia community of Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): Hindu sharing in the Muslim orders is "more visible in Sind than in the Punjab"; "Hindu writers wrote mystical poems in which they used the imagery of the Muslims; they composed poems in honor of the Prophet and even wrote tcfziya [text-layer?: taʿziya] for the Muharram mourning of the Shia community of Sind; or they devoted ballads to the fate of famous Sufi martyrs" (p. 418, folio 387).
- (book's word: "all over Sind"; refers to: present day; ethnographic present): dervishes at Latif's tomb perform his music on Thursday nights, "and the melodies are well known all over Sind" (p. 421, folio 390).
- (book's word: "Sind", "The country", "The province"; refers to: "after the British occupation in 1843", "a new Arabic alphabet in 1852", and "Nadir Shah (1739)"; book's historical prose): "Sindhi continued to flourish after the British occupation in 1843. With the introduction of a new Arabic alphabet in 1852, a considerable literature grew up that included mystical poetry and prose. The country had always been proud of its mystical heritage. Did not Diwan Gidumal, the minister, give the invading Nadir Shah (1739) the most precious treasure Sind had to offer, some of the dust of its saints? The province continued to produce mystics who wrote either in Persian or in their mother tongue" (p. 427, folio 396; header printed `306` [text-layer?: 396]). The book marks the unit as Sind after "the British occupation in 1843"; carried as printed.
- (book's word: "the lower Indus valley", "Sind"; refers to: "the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, notably between the two world wars", with "(1829-31)" and "1971"; book's historical prose): "The attitude of some mystical leaders in the lower Indus valley ... proved quite decisive in the political arena", e.g. "the hurr, the 'Free,' the loyal and absolutely obedient disciples of the Plr Pagaro [text-layer?: Pīr Pagaro] who were gathered first to support Ahmad Brelwi's struggle against the Sikh (1829-31) and later actively joined in the struggle against the British"; "The hurr even played a minor role in the Indian-Pakistani war in 1971"; "This was not the first time that mystical leaders in Sind, or in other parts of the Muslim world, were engaged in politics or in social reforms" (p. 427, folio 396; p. 428, folio 397).
- (book's word: "southern Sind", "the Indus valley"; refers to: "in the beginning of the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Mystical speculation flourished in the Indus valley as it did elsewhere. A strong Naqshbandiyya influence in the beginning of the eighteenth century, however, changed the spiritual climate of southern Sind to a certain extent"; Makhdum Muhammad Hashim of Thatta and his relatives attacked the Sufis "who defended the emotional side of religion", and "this Naqshbandi group contributed to promoting a more sober mystical piety" (p. 428, folio 397).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: present day ("partly still continue", "modern Sindhi short stories"); author's argument): "the majority of the masses continued, and partly still continue, in the veneration of their saints, which is echoed in hundreds of simple folk songs and popular verses. The exaggerated veneration of a saint's tomb has been made the target of some of the best modern Sindhi short stories. The discrepancy between the wealth of the shrines and their guardians and the poverty of the masses indeed provides an impressive theme for social and religious criticism. That holds true not only for Sind, but for other parts of the Muslim world as well" (p. 429, folio 398).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the later Naqshbandiyya", period not stated further; author's argument): "In Bengal as in Sind, the Naqshbandiyya and some more militant mystically inspired groups tried to curb the overflowing emotionalism expressed in the lovely folk songs" (p. 432, folio 401; p. 433, folio 402, header printed `4O2` [text-layer?: 402]).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sind and the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, Appendix 1): in Golden Alphabets words with the same letters were combined in one verse, "since alliteration was a frequently used poetical device, particularly in Sind" (p. 454, folio 423); "In the folk poetry of Sind the heroines always complain that no letter from the beloved has reached them" (p. 455, folio 424); the Ahmad bila mim tradition among "the mystical bards of Sind and the Punjab" (p. 451, folio 420).
- (book's word: "The province of Sind", "Sind"; refers to: Burton "long ago", footnoted to *Sind and the Races* (London, 1851), p. 230, and the present; quoted source (Burton) and author's argument, Appendix 2): "The province of Sind, generally famous for its saint worship, is particularly rich in legends connected with women saints, as the critical observer Richard Burton long ago observed: 'To the credit of the Sindhi it must be said that they do not refuse to admit the religious merits of the softer sex'"; he speaks of the "faqlrdni [text-layer?: faqīrānī] (feminine for faqlr [text-layer?: faqīr])": "a few of them occasionally rise to the high rank of a murshid" (p. 464, folio 433). His remarks on "Blbi [text-layer?: Bībī] Fatima Hajram, a haftza [text-layer?: ḥāfiẓa]" are "corroborated by the numerous books devoted by Sindhi scholars to the religious life of their country. As elsewhere in the Muslim world, we find in Sind whole groups of women saints, like the haft cafifa [text-layer?: haft ʿafīfa], 'the Seven Chaste,' ... or the mukhaddardt-i abddliyya [text-layer?: mukhaddarāt-i abdāliyya], 'the veiled women in the rank of abddl [text-layer?: abdāl],' and countless others" (p. 465, folio 434). Same page: the longing soul as a woman "In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab" (see `punjab`).

## From Haroon Khalid, Walking with Nanak

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_walking_with_nanak.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day; book's historical prose): Nanak Panthi Hindus, "thousands of such Sikhs", live "in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab and Sindh in Pakistan" (p. 98, folio 80); the communities who refused the Khalsa and called themselves followers of Guru Nanak "still exist in Sindh and in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa. Baptised Sikhs look down upon them"; at Manora the author saw them "now merging into the larger groups of Sikhs" (p. 230, folio 204).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day; travelogue): at the Bahawalpur–Uch road "the cultures of Punjab and Sindh merge" — "Punjab Hotel" beside "Sindh Hotel", Siraiki as "an amalgamation of the two languages", Uch's Siraiki "more like Sindhi" (p. 276, folio 250).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: present day, seen from Manora; travelogue): "the Makran mountain range, the boundary line between Sindh and Baluchistan" (p. 228, folio 202).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the day of the visit to Inderjit, present day, date not stated; travelogue): television news "covering the floods in Sindh and lower Punjab" (p. 306, folio 280).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: Nanak's lifetime, "towards the end of his travels", after settling at Kartarpur; travelogue voice citing tradition and Iqbal Qaiser): "Nanak visited Uch Sharif on his way to Sindh, towards the end of his travels, after he had already visited the East, South, North and West" (p. 278, folio 252); Iqbal Qaiser's *Historical Sikh Shrines of Pakistan* identifies the Clifton temple as "the temple which Nanak had visited on his tour of Sindh" (p. 217, folio 191).

## From Haroon Khalid, A White Trail

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_a_white_trail.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "inner Sindh"; refers to: the present, c. the book's writing; book's historical prose): the majority of the remaining non-Muslims "are Hindus living in the 'faraway' 'peripheral' regions of Southern Punjab, inner Sindh and Baluchistan" (p. 12, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the days after the Babri Mosque demolition, "6th of December 1992"; book's historical prose): "Hindu communities in Sindh and Quetta were attacked" (p. 22, folio not stated). Quetta is a town and is not attached to Balochistan here.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the book's present, with "the census of 1998" cited; book's historical prose): "according to the census of 1998, the Hindu minority is the largest minority in the country, with Christians in the second place, most of the Hindus are scattered in Sindh, Baluchistan, and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "Sindh", "the province of Sindh"; refers to: the present, c. 2011; book's historical prose): Nanak Panthi Sikhs "belong to the areas of South Punjab and Sindh"; their temples "in Sindh and Southern Punjab" hold portraits of Guru Nanak alongside Hindu deities; "They are found in very large numbers in the province of Sindh" (p. 197, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the book's present, the author's fieldwork period, no year stated; author's argument, Afterword): "The majority of the Hindu community is based in Sindh and Southern Punjab, where they have a proportionate representation from the lower and the upper castes"; there they "are a part of the social fabric of the area". The author could not "delve into much detail about the experiences of the community from these regions" (p. 233, folio not stated).

## From Haroon Khalid, In Search of Shiva

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_in_search_of_shiva.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- **Scope of the book** (book's word: "Sindh"; period: the author's research travels, years not stated; author's own account): "southern Punjab and Sindh are excluded, where there are many such shrines. These shrines also represent similar characteristics as discussed in this book" (p. 6, Introduction, folio not stated).
- **Syncretic shrines** (book's word: "Sindh"; period: the present; informant: Mussadiq): "several shrines in the Multan region and in Sindh where people still practise Hinduism and give it the name of Islam" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **Tableeghi focus** (book's word: "rural Sindh"; period: the present; author's argument): Tableeghi preachers "focus on southern Punjab and rural Sindh where syncretistic religion held sway" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **Deobandi focus** (book's word: "Sindh"; period: "In recent years"; Tahir Kamran as cited): "the focus of the Deoband has been on South Punjab and Sindh where there is an ubiquitous saint and shrine culture" (p. 11, ch. 5, folio not stated).
- **"Saeen"** (book's word: "Sindh"; period not stated, present tense; book's prose): "Saeen is a title used to refer to a feudal lord or a spiritual leader from lower Punjab and Sindh, where the shrine culture thrives" (p. 11, ch. 5, folio not stated).

**Label-only / toponym uses (not attached):** "Shikarpur, Sindh" — the blast at "the village of Marri near Shikarpur, Sindh" (p. 12, ch. 6) is adjectival/locational. Mohenjodaro (p. 10, ch. 4) treated as a named site, not Sindh material.

## From Sujan Rai Bhandari, Khulasat al-Tawarikh

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khulasat_ut_tawarikh.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: sindh

*The book's `sindh` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- **Qubad Khan at suba Thatta** (c. 004; book's words: "صوبه داری تهتهه" (p. 49); period: the pursuit of Dara Shikoh, Muharram to Rabi al-Thani, the year not stated in the chunk; a Mughal suba): "قباد خان[OCR?] بموجب فرمان والا بصوبه داری تهتهه قیام ورزید" (p. 49).
- **Qubad Khan's appointment** (c. 006; book's word: "صوبه داری تهتهه"; period: the same campaign, Muharram of the year printed "۱۰۶۸[OCR?]" / "۱۰۶۹[OCR?]"; a Mughal suba): at the Lahore halt "قباد خان[OCR?] از کوکیان[OCR?] شینخمیر[OCR?] بصوبه داری تهتهه از اصل اضافه بمنصب چهار هزاری و سه هزار سوار سر افراسته[OCR?] گردید" (p. 64).
- **Mirza Isa Tarkhan's appointment** (c. 009; book's word: "صوبه داری تهتهه[OCR?]"; period: Shah Jahan's march from the Deccan to his accession — after leaving Junnar on "بست[OCR?] و سویم[OCR?] ربیع الاول" and before reaching Akbarabad on "بست[OCR?] و ششم[OCR?] جمادی الاول", in the year printed "۱۰۳۷[OCR?]" (pp. 104–106); a Mughal suba): during a seven-day halt at Ahmadabad, "میرزا عیسے ترخان بمنصب چهارهزاری ذات و دو هزار سوار و صوبه داری تهتهه[OCR?] سرافرازی[OCR?] یافتند" (p. 105).
- **The sultans of Sind and the conquest of Thatta** (c. 014; book's words: "ولایت سنده" (pp. 171, 172, 173), "سلاطین ولایت سنده" (p. 173), "ممالک تهته" (heading, p. 175), "ولایت تهتهه" (p. 175), "درین دیار" / "مردم این ملک" (p. 174); period, as the book states: from the descendants of "بنی تمیم[OCR?] انصاری" and the Soomras, through the Sammas from "سنه ۷۳۵[OCR?]" and the Arghuns and Tarkhans, to the annexation in "سال سی و هشتم مطابق سنه هزار و یک[OCR?] هجری"; not called a suba in this chunk):
  - Source: "پوشیده نماند که در تاریخ سلطان بهادر شاه[OCR?] احوال سلاطین ولایت سنده چنان بقلم آورده اند" (pp. 173–174).
  - Earliest rulers: the descendants of "بنی تمیم[OCR?] انصاری" ruled "یکصد و سی و نه سال"; then the "سومرگان" took over [illegible], standing out among the zamindars for strength and followers; "پانصد سال در خانواده سومرگان حکومت ماند"; rule then passed to the "سمه گان[OCR?]" (p. 173).
  - The first Samma ruler was "جام انزار"; the word "جام", applied to their "مقدم و کلانتر", is traced "از [illegible] و جمشید" (p. 173).
  - In the time of "جام بانی تهتهه[OCR?] بن جام انزار", "سلطان فیروز شاه فرمان روائے دهلی" marched against the Jam three times, finally took "ولایت سنده", brought the Jam to Delhi, and, pleased with his service, restored the country to him (p. 173).
  - The Samma Jams and their reigns, as printed (pp. 172–173): Jam Anzar, from "سنه ۷۳۵[OCR?]", "[illegible] سال و شش ماه"; Jam Junan[OCR?], his brother, 14 years; Jam Bani Thatta[OCR?], son of Anzar, 15 years; Jam Tamachi[OCR?], his brother, 13 years; Jam Salahuddin, 11 years and some months; Jam Nizamuddin, son of Salahuddin, 2 years and some months; Jam Ali Sher, 6 years and some months; Jam Karan[OCR?], son of Tamachi, "یک و نیم روز"; Jam Fath Khan, son of Sikandar, 15 years, raised by the notables "چون آن ولایت از حاکم خالی گردید"; Jam Tughluq, son of Sikandar, "هیزده[OCR?]" years; Jam Mubarak, 3 days; Jam Sikandar, son of Fath Khan, 1 year 6 months; Jam Sanjar, 8 years and some months; Jam Nizamuddin, "که بجام نندا مشهور است", 62 years; Jam Firuz, 19 years.
  - The end of the Sammas: Jam Salahuddin, a kinsman of Jam Firuz whose sister was married to Sultan Muzaffar of Gujarat, twice raised armies from Gujarat and won; Jam Firuz called in Mirza Shah Beg Arghun of Qandahar; Salahuddin was killed, and "حکومت ولایت سنده بلاشرکت بجام فیروز تعلق گرفت". "در سنه ۹۲۷[OCR?] میرزا شاه بیگ ارغون از قندهار لشکر کشیده تهته را متصرف شد"; Jam Firuz went to Gujarat, served Sultan Bahadur Shah, gave him his daughter and died there; "از آنجا حکومت سلسله سمه گان منقطع گردید" (p. 172).
  - Arghuns and Tarkhans (p. 171): Mirza Shah Beg "بقوت و شجاعت ولایت سنده متصرف گشت" and died "سنه ۹۳۰[OCR?]", his rule "سه سال"; Mirza Shah Husain refortified Bhakkar, took Multan, and died "سنه ۹۶۲[OCR?]" after 32 years; "میرزا عیسی ترخان از امراے میرزا شاه بیگ در تهته و سلطان محمود در بهکر دم استقلال میزدند", sometimes at peace and sometimes at war — Isa Tarkhan died "سنه ۹۶۵[OCR?]" after 13 years; Mirza Muhammad Baqi overcame his brother Khan Baba, kept up his father's dealings with Sultan Mahmud of Bhakkar, and ruled "هیزده[OCR?] سال"; Mirza Jani Beg, "ابن پاینده محمد بن محمد باقی", came to court with the Khankhanan "در سنه هزار و یک[OCR?] هجری", and "آن ولایت داخل ممالک محروسه گردید"; he had ruled eight years.
  - The conquest: the order was "نخستین انتزاع ولایت تهتهه پیش نهاد همت ساز و بعد ازان بتسخیر قندهار پرداز" (p. 175). "در آن نواحی وبائے عظیم روے داد"; dervishes dreamt that the plague came from "سرتابی مردم این ملک", and everyone made vows (see Legends and Practices) (p. 174). Jani Beg was given "منصب سه هزاری و جاگیر تهتهه"; "لاهری بندر که از طرف تهتهه واقع است بخالصه شریفه مقرر گشت" (p. 174).
- **The Khankhanan's conquests** (c. 019; book's word: "تهتهه[OCR?]"; period: the career of Mirza Abd al-Rahim Khan-i Khanan, not stated beyond that): his deeds include "فتح ولایت گجرات و تهتهه[OCR?] و دکن او کرده" (p. 244).
- **The Jams and Arghun in Sultan Husain Langah's time** (c. 023; book's words: "ولایت تهته" and "تهته" (p. 295); period: the reign of Sultan Husain Langah (from "۸۷۶[OCR?]") and Babur's arrival in Punjab, as narrated; the book's own unit, not a Mughal suba): "جام بایزید و جام ابراهیم از جام[OCR?] نند[OCR?] حاکم ولایت تهته رنجیده بخدمت سلطان حسین پیوستند", and were assigned "جاگیر مسر[OCR?]" (p. 295); Jam Bayazid later helped Sultan Husain kill the vizier Imad al-Mulk (p. 295). Babur's farman went "بمیرزا شاه حسین ارغون حاکم تهته", and "میرزا شاه حسین ارغون از تهته آمده" to fight for Multan (p. 295).
- **Sind in Prince Muhammad Sultan's jagir** (c. 031; book's word: "مملکت سنده"; period: Balban's reign, as the book places it): "مملکت سنده با توابع ولواحق ومضافات در جاگیر شاهزاده مقرر گردید"; the next sentence posts him to Multan (pp. 386–387).
- **Campaigns and flights toward Sind** (c. 032; book's words: "سنده" / "سند", also "دریاۓ سنده", which is the river; three periods as the book places them, all campaign or flight mentions): Mahmud's reign — "لشکر عظیم بجانب ملتان وسنده کشیده تا چهار هزار کشتی ساختند", each boat with three iron spikes, one at the prow and one on each side; the opponents sent their families to the islands and fought, and most were killed or drowned (p. 411). Shihab al-Din's fourth campaign, "درسنه ۵[illegible]": "برسر دیول که بتهته مشهور است رفته تمام ولایت درکنار دریاۓ سنده بضبط درآورد ونفایس ونوادر ان دیار بامال بسیار گرفته برگشت" (p. 404); Pass 1 notes "تمام ولایت درکنار دریاۓ سنده" names the river and is attached province-level by judgement. Iltutmish's reign: Jalal al-Din fled "بطرف سند وسیوستان" (p. 399).
- **Mahmud's second and twelfth campaigns** (c. 033; book's words: "سند", also "دریاۓ سنده[OCR?]", the river; period: Sultan Mahmud's reign — one item without a year, the other "دراواخر سنه ۴۱۷[OCR?]"): "لجه [OCR?] بجے [OCR?] راے [OCR?]" left his army "از روے بے همتی و بے تدبیری" and "بجانب سند روان شد"; the Sultan's troops pursued and caught him (pp. 421–420). The Sultan set out to punish "جمعی که دروقت مراجعت ازسومنات برکناره دریاۓ سنده[OCR?] بلشکریان[OCR?] سلطان بے ادبی کرده بودند" (p. 412); the sentence continues on p. 411 in c. 032. Pass 1: the riverbank item is attached province-level by judgement.
- **The Pandavas' last journey** (c. 038; book's words: "ولایت تهتهه[OCR?]"; same period and passage as the punjab bullet): (p. 479).
- **Suba Thatta described** (c. 042; book's words: "صوبه تهته" (p. 530), "درین ولایت" (p. 530), "درین صوبه" (p. 529); period: the author's time, c. 1107 AH, a Mughal suba; the capitals "در پاستان زمان"):
  - Capitals: "برهمن اباد نام شهری بزرگ تختگاه بود و قلعه ان هزار و چهار صد برج داشت هر یک بفاصله یک طناب"; after it "ادپور پائے تخت گردید", with many traces of towers and walls remaining; "پس ازان دیول اکنون تهته و دیبل دار الحکومت است" (p. 530).
  - Land revenue: "درین ولایت سوم بخش از کشاورز به گیرند" (p. 530).
  - Resources: "کان نمک و اهن فراوان"; at "در[OCR?] شش کروهے" a "کان سنگ زرد", cut to size for building (p. 530).
  - Boats: "اکثر مدار بر کشتی است", "از خورد و بزرگ چهل هزار خواهد بود" (p. 530).
  - Game and food: "گورخر", hare, "کوته پاچه", pig and fish; the diet rests "بر برنج و جغرات و ماهی" (p. 530). Dried fish ("قاق") is shipped to the ports, and fish oil ("روغن[OCR?]") is used on boats; a fish called "پلوه[OCR?]" comes from the salt sea into the Sind, is netted, and is found in no other country (p. 530). Curds keep four months; flowers and many fruits, "خاصه انبه", and small melons in the desert (p. 530).
  - The liver-eaters: "جگر خواره این دیار مشهورتر فی الاکناف است"; they take livers, especially children's, by spell, especially at mealtimes; "بعضی گویند" a trance comes on them; they share the liver with fellow-practitioners and the victim dies (p. 530). They make others like themselves by feeding them a piece and teaching the spell; they ride the hyena ("کفتار") and bring news from afar (p. 530). When one is caught the experts split the calf and take out "دانه بسان انار دانه", which is fed to the victim, who recovers (pp. 529–530). A liver-eater will not sink with a millstone tied to the neck nor burn in fire; to cure one, the experts brand both temples, fill the eyes with salt, hang the person in a house for forty days, give saltless food and recite spells (p. 529). Most are women, some men; "در هر بلاد می باشند و اما در بلاد تهته بیشتر باشند" (p. 529).
  - Peoples: north of the line from Multan and Uch to Thatta and Kach Makran, hard-rock hills, home of Baluch and "بعضے افغان"; to the south, from Uch to Gujarat, sand hills and the "بهتی", whose chief's seat is Jaisalmer, and "دیگر اقوام راجپوت[OCR?]"; from Bhakkar to Nasirpur and Amarkot, "سبوده[OCR?]" and "جاریجه" (p. 529).
  - River: "بزرگ ترین دریائے سند است"; merchants from Multan and Bhakkar ship goods by it to Thatta, and travellers and even "عساکر گران" go by river; large armies rarely go by land, because of the jungle and the lack of water (p. 529).
  - Extent: "طول این صوبه از بهکر تا کچ مکران دو صد و پنجاه کروه و عرض از قصبه بدین تا بندر لاهری صد کروه" (p. 529). Bounds: east "گجرات احمداباد", west "کچ مکران", north "بهکر", south "دریائے شور" (p. 529).
  - Administration and revenue: "سرکار تهته و سیوستان و نصیر پور و امرکوت چهار سرکار مشتمل بر پنجاه و هفت محال و پنج بندر و ده[OCR?] کرور[OCR?] و چهل و نه لک و هفتاد هزار دام داخل این صوبه است" (pp. 528–529).
  - Pass 1's Doubts: the book places Hinglaj in its suba Thatta section, but the Hinglaj row's province tag is balochistan; this sindh bullet is not meant to reach it.
- **Sind west of Kutch** (c. 043; book's word: "سند"; period: the author's time, c. 1107 AH, since the book gives no other; the book does not write "صوبه تهته" here): in the Gujarat section, "کچهه ولایتی است جدا گانه", its length and breadth "دو صد و پنجاه کروه", and "مغرب رویه سند است" (p. 531).
- **Thatta as Hindustan's western border; "Sind known as Thatta"** (c. 047; book's words: "تهته", "ولایت سند المشهور به تهته", "ممالک تهته"; period per item; the book's own units, not the modern province):
  - The author's time (present tense, in a work dated "یک هزار و یکصد و هفتم هجری" on p. 581): "ازین مملکت جانب شرق بنگاله و سمت جنوب دکن و طرف غرب تهته بدریائے شور اتصال دارد" (p. 579).
  - The times of the independent sultans, "درازمنه مختلفه" (the label "المشهور به تهته" is the author's own): among his sources is the "تاریخ بهادرشاهی مشتمل بر احوال سلاطین ولایت گجرات احمد آباد و ولایت سند المشهور به تهته" (p. 581); these lands "از تصرف اورنگ نشینان دهلی برامده و به هریک ولایت حاکمی جداگانه استقلال یافته بود" (p. 581).
  - Table-of-contents locators: "دربیان تسخیر ممالک تهته و آمدن جانی بیگ", printed page ۴۱۴, in the run before "رحلت حضرت خاقان زمان" (p. 591); "دربیان بقیه حقیقت تعاقب افواج قاهره و آمدن داراشکوه از تهته[OCR?] بجانب گجرات", printed page ۵۳۸, in the Aurangzeb run (p. 589). *basis: contents headings only*
